### arXiv 2611.34942 - Spectrum Adaptation of AI-Native Autoencoder Communications with Transfer Learning

- categories: cs.NI, cs.AI, eess.SP
- published: 2026-11-25T18:59:57Z
- authors: Yalin E. Sagduyu, Tugba Erpek
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34942

End-to-end physical-layer learning replaces separate modulation, coding, and detection operations with an autoencoder (AE) that consists of a jointly trained encoder-decoder pair at the transmitter and the receiver. This paper studies AI-native communications with AEs and quantifies the sensitivity to training-test mismatch in channel models. Models trained only on an AWGN channel achieve low bit error rates (BERs) under matched conditions but degrade severely on Rayleigh fading with practical front-end impairments. To reduce the time and energy required to adapt to new channel conditions, we propose a low-latency transfer-learning procedure that starts from an AWGN-pretrained system and fine-tunes only a small subset of transmitter and receiver layers while freezing the remainder. We further formulate layer selection as a budget-constrained optimization problem that identifies the most informative parameters to update during adaptation. This preserves the learned signal geometry and the deep decision structure while adapting lightweight front ends to fading- and impairment-induced mixing. For AE communications, we show that partial updates remove the mismatch-induced error floor and approach the BER of fully retrained models. We provide a layer-wise study and quantify resource-performance trade-offs, showing that modest adaptation substantially reduces BER while cutting training time, multiply-accumulate (MAC) operations, energy consumption, and the fraction of updated parameters for resource-efficient implementation.


### arXiv 2611.34934 - Attack Policy Selection can Mislead Monitor Evaluators

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T18:59:28Z
- authors: Charlie Griffin, Oliver Makins, Asa Cooper Stickland, Alessandro Abate, Yarin Gal, Abby D'Cruz
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34934

AI monitors, classifiers that flag dangerous behaviour by AI agents, are often evaluated against transcripts generated by attack policies acting as proxies for dangerous behaviour in deployment. We evaluate an ex-production OpenAI monitor as a case study and find that measured false negative rate is highly sensitive to both the method used to generate attack policies and the level of optimisation applied within that method. This rate ranged from 0.5\% to 20.8\% across optimisation levels of a single method (reinforcement learning), and from 6.4\% to 23.0\% across methods at their best observed optimisation level (optimised prompting vs.\ transcript editing). Every attack policy was developed against proxy monitors only: all reported results show attacks transferring to the held-out GPT-5.4 target monitor. Our results suggest that monitor evasion evaluations can easily mislead: an evaluation using only basic attack methods may give false confidence that a monitor is strong. The sensitivity to attack-policy choice raises a problem: interpreting any particular result as evidence about deployment performance requires justification. The transcripts produced by attack policies are proxies for the dangerous behaviour a monitor must flag in deployment. These proxies can introduce visible artefacts, such as reasoning about a ``side task'' absent from the instructions shown to the monitor, that affect monitor judgements. Given the sensitivity above, the choice of proxy can change an evaluation's conclusion. Reading these results as evidence about deployment therefore requires a separate legitimacy argument for why the proxies are informative, including any justification of conservativeness. Making convincing arguments remains an open problem.


### arXiv 2611.34928 - Diverse Alternative Reasoning Traces for Hard Math Problems

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-25T18:59:11Z
- authors: Lukas Aichberger, Adam Golinski, Arno Blaas, Arwen Bradley, Jacob Springer, Mehrdad Farajtabar
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34928

Post-training large language models (LLMs) for mathematical reasoning often relies on accepted traces whose final answers pass a verifier. For hard math problems, however, such traces can be rare under independent Monte Carlo (MC) sampling. To address this, we propose Diverse Alternative Reasoning Traces (DART), a trace-mining method that starts from a rejected trace, decomposes it into reasoning steps, and ranks them by position-weighted uncertainty. It then generates deliberately distinct alternative candidates for highly ranked steps using reference-answer guidance and a meta-thought before completing the resulting traces. On greedy-unsolved DeepMath-103K problems, DART recovers 37% more problems than MC sampling at a matched number of generated traces. Its estimated generation cost per recovered problem is nearly identical, while its cost per accepted trace is 30% lower. Post-training Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct on traces mined by DART achieves the highest point estimate in five of six evaluations across MATH-500 and AIME benchmarks. These results demonstrate that revisiting rejected traces with DART can expand problem coverage and provide effective post-training data.


### arXiv 2611.34920 - $μ$-Bench: Benchmarking Multi-User Task Completion in Shared Interaction Voice Agents

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T18:58:42Z
- authors: Qihao Lin, Guanxu Chen, Shuhang Zheng, Xiaoya Lu, Jingyi Yang, Terumi Chiba
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34920

Voice agents in shared spaces must satisfy interdependent multi-user requests while upholding per-user authority, privacy, and task constraints. Existing evaluations offer limited coverage when participants react throughout a session. We introduce $μ$-Bench, a benchmark for joint task completion in closed-loop, full-duplex multi-user voice interaction. Simulated participants act from isolated profiles of goals, knowledge, and permissions, adapting to the agent's speech and tool outcomes. Across 20 domains, 100 scenarios vary group size, acoustics, and follow-up demands to form 800 cases (408 tool-augmented, 392 tool-free). Joint Task Success (JTS) requires all user goals, privacy boundaries, and terminal states to hold jointly, while Pure JTS further disallows unauthorized tool calls; Attribution, Threading, and Coordination (ATC) diagnostics localize failures. Across 13 voice agents, proprietary systems peak at 62.24\% tool-free and 65.44\% tool-augmented success, while open-weight models remain below 20\%. ATC diagnostics show that open-weight models lag primarily in multi-turn attribution and threading, and controlled contrasts reveal that larger groups consistently degrade joint task success across capable systems. By coupling reactive multi-user simulation with verifiable session-level outcomes, $μ$-Bench provides a rigorous testbed for shared voice agents.


### arXiv 2611.34899 - Enhancing Maritime Domain Awareness using Latent Space Representations

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-25T18:51:36Z
- authors: Rikard Vinge, Miguel A. Belenguer-Plomer, Isabelle Wittmann, Octavian Dumitru, Michele Lazzarini, Sergio Albani
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34899

This work presents a novel application of Earth Observation (EO) embeddings for Maritime Domain Awareness (MDA) from the perspective of the geospatial foundation model TerraMind. By operating directly on compressed latent-space representations rather than on the original Sentinel-1 imagery, this approach directly addresses the data gravity bottleneck that increasingly constrains operational EO processing, in line with the objectives of the Embed2Scale project. In particular, linear and non-linear probing of compressed embeddings, latent space representations of synthetic aperture radar (SAR) imagery, is used to detect, localise, and characterise vessels from Sentinel-1 GRD images. Vessel detection achieves a ROC-AUC of 0.95, while vessel localisation has a median error of around 300 m for vessels longer than 100 m, and vessel AIS type-code classification achieves 62% accuracy for the majority classes. The latter, however, is highly correlated with vessel length. These results reveal both the potential and current limitations of Geospatial Foundation Models (GeoFMs) when applied to fine-grained MDA understanding in real-world scenarios. Additionally, a new maritime remote sensing dataset is released, pairing about 2,000 Sentinel-1 images with temporally aligned AIS signals from commercial vessels. Through manual inspection, each candidate sample was verified for bounding-box quality and for consistency between the AIS-reported vessel length and the observed vessel extent, cross-checking vessel identity and location against publicly available vessel records and, where available, photographs. The dataset was made ready for use with the NeuCo-Bench neural compression benchmark framework, publicly available on HuggingFace through https://huggingface.co/datasets/embed2scale/S1-single-ship-downstream .


### arXiv 2611.34887 - Interpretable and Accurate Hybrid Decision Trees with Selective Case-Based Splits

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T18:45:35Z
- authors: Cristiano Landi, Alessio Cascione, Riccardo Guidotti
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34887

Decision trees are among the most well-known interpretable models in Machine Learning. Over the years, a wide variety of strategies have been proposed to define split conditions within the greedy learning framework, aiming to guide instance routing and enhance predictive performance. These strategies range from axis-parallel and oblique splits based on feature values to proximity-based splits using exemplar instances and more complex hybrid approaches. However, no single method has emerged as universally superior. In this work, we present two key contributions to the construction of greedy decision trees. First, we propose a hybrid tree architecture that integrates both feature-based and case-based splits, allowing for heterogeneous splits throughout the tree. Second, we introduce a novel type of split that leverages a selective case-based approach: when a split is needed, our method searches for the most informative subset of features to define the split, effectively balancing the specificity of case-based methods with the simplicity of feature-based ones. Experiments on a diverse set of tabular datasets show that our method outperforms standard decision trees and achieves performance on par with ensemble methods, while producing shallow trees that retain or even improve predictive accuracy.


### arXiv 2611.34875 - How Much Do Coding Agents Over-Edit, and Why? An Empirical Study of Unneeded Edits in Repository-Level Agent Patches

- categories: cs.SE, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T18:37:06Z
- authors: Aakash Goel, Benjamin Steenhoek, Yu Hu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34875

Agent patches are larger than developers', which is widely read as over-editing. But patch size does not say whether the extra edits are needed, what they are, or where they come from. We introduce Delta Residue Attribution (DRA), which replays agent trajectories, traces every block (unit) of the final patch to the step that wrote it, reverts each unit, and reruns the task tests to decide whether the unit is needed. We apply DRA to 7,660 resolved GitHub Copilot CLI trajectories from 18 frontier models on SWE-bench Verified (6,728) and DeepSWE (932), and grade developer patches using the same method. Agent patches do change more lines than developers'; on SWE-bench Verified the majority of extra edits add tests (61%) and documentation (13%) while on DeepSWE, most extra edits are code (58%). On both benchmarks, agents' code is on average at least as necessary as developers' (mean $NP_{code}$ 0.887 vs. 0.864 on SWE-bench Verified, where 11 of 18 models leave less unneeded code than developers, and 0.797 vs. 0.785 on DeepSWE). On average, 11% of an agent patch's code units on SWE-bench Verified are still unneeded, and 89% of those change behavior. Debug steps write almost none of the unneeded code. Code necessity differs substantially by model; it is unrelated to resolve rate on SWE-bench Verified and negatively correlated with it on DeepSWE (Spearman -0.59, p = 0.01). On SWE-bench Verified, pruning with DRA using only the tests the agent can see (its own tests and the repository's) removes the majority of unneeded code but also some needed code (67% recall, 46% precision), and 89% of pruned patches still resolve the task; asking the agent to audit its own diff is more precise but less effective (36% recall, 66% precision). We recommend that benchmarks report code necessity next to resolve rate and that over-editing be addressed through model training and harness-level pruning.


### arXiv 2611.34870 - Shape-Aware Three-Way Merge for RDF Knowledge Graphs: The schema defines the conflict: an implementation and evaluation

- categories: cs.DB, cs.AI, cs.SE
- published: 2026-11-25T18:34:46Z
- authors: Steve Brown
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34870

Merging RDF serializations creates ordering conflicts and misses semantic ones. We describe and evaluate a three-way merge in which the schema defines a conflict: set algebra combines canonical triples, and a slot is contended only where a SHACL shape bounds its cardinality and both sides' changes cannot stand within that bound. Unbounded predicates union; contended slots go to a person. The same shapes graph validates the result. We do not claim the first three-way merge for RDF. Our contribution is an implementation in a production multi-agent store and an evaluation against a reimplementation of Quit Store's Context Merge overlap rule, which it extends rather than replaces, and six other strategies. Across five synthetic seeds, the shape-aware operator raised 33 conflicts, all true positives as judged by the same shapes that define them, with no triples lost or fabricated and no SHACL violations admitted. The context-overlap baseline, counted per slot, detected the same 33 conflicts but asked 845 questions. A replay built from recorded production incidents covers 105 human alias repairs (93 after excluding 12 chained pairs) and 939 duplicate-value incidents. It reproduces a triple-level limit: different names for one entity remain invisible (0 of 93 alias repairs detected, matching 0 of 21 synthetic cases). In a study of evolving shape contracts, merging shape declarations first handles the tested cardinality changes, while datatype evolution exposes a detector blind spot caught by post-merge validation. An oracle that reads no shapes agrees with the shipped operator on the facts and history of all 23 reachable single-slot cases. Under wrong shapes, a missing bound loses data silently and a spurious one only adds questions. The supported claim is fewer human decisions with nothing silently lost, for cardinality conflicts in one deployment, not general merge correctness.


### arXiv 2611.34868 - A Survey of Security, Privacy, and Ethics of Large Language Models in Telecom

- categories: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.CY
- published: 2026-11-25T18:33:07Z
- authors: Joy Bose, Srinivas Adyapak
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34868

Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly integrated into telecommunications for customer service, network analytics, and operational support. While offering efficiency and innovation, their deployment raises critical privacy, security, and ethical challenges due to the uniquely sensitive and longitudinal nature of telecom data. This paper presents a survey of LLM risks and safeguards in telecom context. Our survey introduces a telecom-specific taxonomy linking privacy, security, and ethics with use cases and outlines open research challenges such as longitudinal data privacy, cross-border compliance, and scalable guardrails.


### arXiv 2611.34864 - RePace: Reasoning about Demonstration Speedup for Faster Vision-Language-Action Policies

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T18:30:43Z
- authors: Insup Lee, Hojin Jeon, Sunguk Shin, Seoungbin Bae, Taekwan Kim, Jinwoo Shin
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34864

Since vision-language-action (VLA) policies can inherit the slow pace of human demonstrations, accelerating these demonstrations before fine-tuning can lead to faster VLA policies without an additional speed-selection module at deployment. Several offline speedup methods have focused on identifying segments that require less precision and assigning predefined speedup factors to the segments. However, even within a single segment, a speedup factor suitable for steady motion may be too high when precise alignment is required. To this end, this paper presents RePace, an offline reasoning framework that determines how much to accelerate each part of a demonstration. For the same recorded movement, RePace measures how much each candidate speedup factor shortens the sequence and compares the resulting observation-action pairs used for training. Given the task instruction, a pretrained language model uses the visual context and original motion of successive windows to interpret these candidate retiming effects and select a speedup profile for the complete episode. Experimental results demonstrate that RePace outperforms the baselines in overall task execution efficiency on both LIBERO and RoboCasa-Kitchen. On all three tasks with a gripper-equipped Franka Research 3 arm, RePace matches or exceeds the acceleration baselines in success rate and completes successful trials faster.


### arXiv 2611.34862 - BOP-Refer: Benchmark for Monocular 3D and 2D Object Grounding

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.RO
- published: 2026-11-25T18:29:36Z
- authors: Tomas Hodan, Vineet Bhat, Martin Sundermeyer, Jonathan Tremblay, Yash Patel, Médéric Fourmy
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34862

We introduce BOP-Refer, the first benchmark for monocular 3D and 2D grounding of everyday and industrial objects sourced from 9 diverse BOP datasets. We pair images from these datasets with VLM-generated and human-verified referring expressions, and derive accurate ground-truth 3D and 2D bounding boxes of the referred objects from their 3D models and 6DoF pose annotations. Our systematic evaluation of 17 frontier VLMs across the major proprietary and open-weight families (Gemini, GPT, Claude, Qwen, Gemma) shows that the best model, Gemini 4 Carbon, reaches 72.6% AP_IoU2D in 2D but only 12.4% AP_IoU3D in 3D, despite the more lenient IoU thresholds in 3D. The predicted 3D boxes are usually centered on the target objects in the image but often off in depth by more than the object's size, suggesting that metric scale estimation, which needs reliable object size priors, is currently by far the main source of error.


### arXiv 2611.34858 - From Prediction to Decision: Decision-Focused Fine-Tuning of Autoregressive Tabular Transformers for Cost-Sensitive Financial Events

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T18:27:48Z
- authors: Ashkan Golgoon, Giacomo Domeniconi, Samuel Atkins, Sergio Servantez, Zain Khandwala
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34858

Autoregressive transformers trained on tokenized tabular event streams are a simple and strong baseline for predicting financial events such as fraud. Yet institutions do not act on probabilities; they act on decisions whose consequences are asymmetric and heteroscedastic: the cost of a missed high-value fraud and that of a wrongly declined small purchase are not the same. The standard bridge is post-hoc Bayes-optimal thresholding of calibrated probabilities under a cost matrix. We ask whether fine-tuning the model to the decision objective can do better. We fine-tune the same pretrained model so that its restricted softmax over label tokens (already a distribution over business actions) maximizes expected utility under an institution-specified, amount-scaled cost matrix. Because the reward is a known function of (action, outcome, amount) and the outcome is observed in the data, the expected-utility objective and its gradient are available in closed form. This is example-dependent cost-sensitive learning whose per-example loss is linear in the predicted probability, with no sampling, critics, or advantage estimation. A KL anchor to the pretrained model and an auxiliary language-modeling term preserve calibration and the generative event model. A sampled, group-normalized (GRPO-style) variant provides the extension point to trajectory-level rewards.


### arXiv 2611.34852 - DRBench Pro: A Knowledge-Centric Benchmark for Professional Deep Research Agents

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T18:25:30Z
- authors: Han Huang, Yuchen Yan, Xingyu Wu, Yijun Li, Chao Deng, Jie Liu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34852

Deep research agents gather information from multiple sources and synthesize it into reports on complex, open-ended questions. Existing benchmarks assess report quality along several dimensions, but lack a dedicated knowledge-centric evaluation of which research directions a report covers and how deeply it explores them. We introduce DRBench Pro, a benchmark that makes structured knowledge coverage the primary evaluation target. Our automated pipeline builds local knowledge trees from Wikipedia and uses sampled subgraphs to generate research queries and evidence-grounded rubrics. Each rubric corresponds to a relation in the tree, preserving the structure of the knowledge being evaluated. Agents receive only the research query. This structure allows us to measure breadth across major research directions and depth along related knowledge chains, with their geometric mean giving overall coverage. The benchmark includes 144 cases across 24 domains and 44,102 rubrics. Evaluating 16 deep research agents reveals different strengths in breadth and depth, with the best-performing agent reaching 57.04% overall coverage. Rubric judgments agree closely with human annotations, and agent rankings remain stable across the tested metric configurations.


### arXiv 2611.34843 - Probing Visual Idiosyncrasies in Text-to-Image Models

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T18:20:18Z
- authors: Yongqian Peng, Hongjie Li, Yichao Zhou, Chi Zhang, Yixin Zhu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34843

Text-to-Image (T2I) models turn short text descriptions into images, and the leading models now produce images of comparable visual quality. Whether their outputs still carry model-specific characteristics matters for attribution, for model comparison beyond aggregate quality, and for evaluations that assume anonymity. We ask whether images from frontier T2I models can still be classified by source, and which visual properties make this possible. Here we show that a standard ResNet-50 identifies the source of an image among HiDream-O1-Dev, FLUX.2-dev, HunyuanImage-3.0, and Qwen-Image-2512, the four highest-ranked open-source models on the Text-to-Image Arena, with 100.00% accuracy on held-out prompts. Human participants reach only 62.61% on the pairwise version of this task, so the distinguishing cues are not visually obvious. They are also not tied to a particular classifier and survive severe image corruptions and out-of-distribution prompts. We trace them to visual idiosyncrasies at three levels. Low-level texture statistics alone let a lightweight multi-layer perceptron match ResNet-50. Object layout, depth, and albedo estimated from the images each carry a source signal. Image captions reveal model-specific preferences in style and background that the prompts never specify. A one-sentence hint about these cues raises human accuracy by 9.03 percentage points. These idiosyncrasies make source classification a tool for measuring similarity between models and between real and generated images, and they caution against assuming anonymity in human preference evaluation of T2I models.


### arXiv 2611.34841 - Knowledge-Base Gradient Descent: Self-Structuring Learning through Question Answering

- categories: cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-25T18:19:02Z
- authors: Masahiro Kato
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34841

We consider the problem of constructing knowledge bases for applications based on large language models, in which the structure of a knowledge base determines how an application can retrieve and connect the information needed to answer a question. We study Self-Structuring Learning, a setting in which questions and expected answers guide the construction of a persistent knowledge base whose editable structure can be a graph schema or the organization of linked knowledge documents. We propose Knowledge-Base Gradient Descent, which diagnoses discrepancies between expected answers and responses obtained through the current knowledge base. At each round, the method proposes a structural edit, rebuilds the knowledge base, and adopts the edit when the learning loss decreases. The retained structure is evaluated on other questions about the same sources. By randomizing over candidate edits, we express the loss change of an edit as a derivative with respect to the probability of selecting it, and we evaluate joint edits as single candidates because their changes interact. Schema Descent instantiates the method for graph schemas. In experiments on large documents, each a bundle of research papers, the updated knowledge cards and graph schemas obtain higher mean Answer F1 on held-out generated questions than their initial structures, with schema gains in every document. On human questions, mean F1 rises for schemas and falls for cards: the updated structures serve the kind of question they were learned from.


### arXiv 2611.34831 - Repairing the Trustworthiness of Distilled Language Models

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T18:12:20Z
- authors: Kumar Shubham, Devendra Vyas, Vidushi Agarwal, Jayin Khanna, Prathosh AP
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34831

Knowledge distillation enables the deployment of large language models through smaller student models, but the compression process can introduce regressions in trustworthiness even when downstream performance is preserved. Repairing such failures is challenging because the original distillation corpus is often unavailable, while direct preference optimization may alter capabilities inherited from the teacher. We propose a targeted repair framework based on the proximal Bregman response function (PBRF) that addresses both limitations. We estimate the curvature required for PBRF using a surrogate proxy dataset, removing dependence on the original transfer corpus, and extend the framework to preference-based supervision through an odds-ratio objective. This enables the use of preferred and dispreferred response pairs while constraining changes to the distilled model. Our results show that the proposed approach improves targeted trustworthiness behaviors for both pre-training and post-training models while largely preserving downstream performance, providing a practical alternative to re-distillation or unconstrained preference optimization. Our experiment results show that across different distilled models, the trustworthy regression can be improved by up to 8% with less than 1% decrement in perplexity.


### arXiv 2611.34830 - Not all world models can doubt: how training objective and architecture shape epistemic uncertainty in JEPA and RSSM world models

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T18:11:49Z
- authors: Simon Baur, Wojciech Samek, Jackie Ma
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34830

World models predict how an environment responds to actions, so that candidate actions can be evaluated before execution. Executed actions, or decisions, taken in this manner are only as reliable as the model's predictive capabilities, and predictions in turn are only reliable within the model's experience. Epistemic uncertainty (EU) is the signal that marks where that experience ends. In this work we offer an extensive empirical study on how training objectives and architectures shape epistemic uncertainty of world models. More precisely, we present a controlled comparison of post-hoc EU (last-layer Laplace and ensembles) between two dominant models of different paradigms: 1) LeWorldModel (LeWM): A joint-embedding predictive architecture (JEPA), which predicts the next embedding without a decoder, and 2) DreamerV3: A recurrent state-space model (RSSM), which reconstructs the observation from a recurrent latent state. In ten simulated visual-control environments, we follow a controlled state perturbation of the environment state through every stage of the models up to the final EU estimate. We find that the RSSM attenuates the state perturbation through two mechanisms before it reaches an estimator on the predictor feature. Its recurrent update averages over context, so a change in a single frame reaches the state at a fraction of its size and recovers only as the change persists. Its reconstruction objective weights an entity by the pixels it covers, so a small object can be lost due to the training objective. The JEPA has neither mechanism and passes the change through all stages to the uncertainty. We further show that this translates to gaps in five uncertainty related downstream tasks, from unfamiliar-state detection to uncertainty-penalised planning: the RSSM uncertainty misses deviations that its own posterior registers, whereas the JEPA uncertainty reports what its model sees.


### arXiv 2611.34816 - Transferring Architecture as an Optimization Prior

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T18:01:49Z
- authors: Vighnesh Subramaniam, Boris Katz, Brian Cheung
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34816

Initialization determines the optimization "fate" of a model, going far beyond just setting the scale of its weights. It establishes a fundamental initial geometry over the input data, dictating certain aspects such as which examples are considered close and which representations gradient descent will naturally refine first. In this paper, we view the initial geometry of a network as an optimization prior and ask whether a network can change its optimization prior by referencing a completely different architecture. To investigate this question, we use an initialization procedure where a target network is aligned to the representational geometry of a randomly initialized guide network before any downstream training occurs. Crucially, alignment to the guide network cannot transfer learned features, never receives gradients, and does not use labels. We demonstrate that this cross-architecture transfer of "fate" benefits the target network in two key domains: (1) instilling the optimization benefits of depth into shallow networks by aligning them to deeper networks, and (2) transferring representational granularity by using fine-resolution networks to shape the geometry of networks with coarser representations. In both cases, the alignment results in improved downstream performance of the target network. Ultimately, this demonstrates that a model's architectural destiny can be decoupled from its physical structure and explicitly modified at initialization.


### arXiv 2611.34813 - Solve Vision with Code: Coding Agents Are Stronger Visual Reasoners Than Video Models

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.LG, cs.MM
- published: 2026-11-25T18:01:22Z
- authors: Dezhi Luo, Fengyuan Yu, Haotian Zhang, Zehong Zhao, Ran Ji, Siyuan An
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34813

Video generation models are increasingly evaluated as visual reasoners: given a first frame and a natural-language prompt, can the model produce the video in which the specified task is carried out? The paradigm is considered advantageous because the reasoning is native to the visual medium, unfolding directly in the frames rather than through language or code. Whether this native route is in fact the more reliable path to visual reasoning has not been tested against a symbolic alternative under the same conditions. We therefore pose the same tasks, with identical inputs, output specification and evaluator, to both media: rather than denoising pixels, a coding agent writes a program that renders the answer video, which an unmodified rule-based evaluator scores. On VBVR-Pro-Bench (Xu et al., 2026), a 500-instance suite spanning five cognitive categories with symbolic ground truth, the agent receives the same first frame and prompt as the video model, with no access to ground truth, metadata or generation models, and one attempt per instance. Three closed-model agents (Codex with gpt-6-astra, Claude Code with Fable 5.1 and Gemini CLI with Gemini 3.1 Pro) score 0.923, 0.894 and 0.893, each more than 0.22 above the strongest video model. Among 23 runnable open-weight models, the best, GLM-5, reaches 0.572, above all video models except the RL-trained one. Together, we show that for tasks whose solutions can be computed from a first frame and a prompt, program synthesis is currently a substantially more reliable route to visual reasoning than video generation. We further distinguish depictive programs, which navigate the image to recover a transferable scheme, from propositional programs, which operate on hard-coded values, revealing how visual reasoning unfolds in agent-written programs and what it implies for its nature of reasoning in general.


### arXiv 2611.34811 - HOG-WAM: Improving Visual Robustness through Future Gradient Prediction

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.RO
- published: 2026-11-25T17:59:24Z
- authors: Boxuan Dong, Yupeng Qian, Ying Wu, Heshuai Li, Junhao Tan, Zhenxiong Tan
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34811

World-action models jointly predict future observations and actions, yet visual corruption can disrupt the resulting policies. We introduce HOG-WAM, which adds future Histograms of Oriented Gradients (HOG) as structural supervision derived directly from demonstration images. An auxiliary HOG diffusion transformer (DiT) module reads RGB features through asymmetric attention, allowing its objective to shape the visual representation used for control. The module is used only during training and removed at inference, preserving FastWAM's RGB-to-action architecture. We evaluate four controlled observation corruptions through a visual-corruption extension of RoboTwin, termed RoboTwin-Plus; HOG-WAM achieves the highest success among the compared policies under all four. Mixed-noise success increases from 78.86% to 82.86% relative to FastWAM, with comparable performance on clean scenes. On LIBERO Plus, overall success increases from 74.49% to 75.26%, with the largest gain under sensor noise, from 74.14% to 78.89%. Across four Franka tasks, average success increases from 40% to 59% under clean observations and from 17% to 24% under noisy observations. A representation ablation favors combining RGB observations with auxiliary HOG prediction over replacing RGB with HOG. These findings support future-gradient prediction as complementary supervision for visually robust control, with larger absolute gains on physical tasks than in simulation. Code is available at https://github.com/iPPLab/HOG-WAM.


### arXiv 2611.34785 - PersonaFactory: Stylistic User Prompt Augmentation with Synthetic Persona Bank

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-25T17:42:31Z
- authors: Bich Ngoc Doan, Hanna Yukhymenko, Imanol Schlag
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34785

Large language models increasingly replace real people in the AI development pipeline, as they are used to generate training dialogues at scale and to drive alignment and evaluation. However, out-of-the-box models produce monolingual and stylistically flat texts as synthetic user prompts, failing to replicate the real user-AI communication mechanics. We present PersonaFactory, an end-to-end pipeline that generates detailed user personas and uses them to augment existing user prompts from their point of view, producing a bank of over 740000 structured synthetic personas detailing their background and communication behaviors, and a corpus of roughly 1.24 million persona-styled synthetic conversations spanning 85 languages. We evaluate the effect of personal features on style adoption and intent preservation, using an LLM-judge and heuristic metrics to assess augmentation fidelity together with the downstream impact on assistant persona control through training data. Results show that our structured, attribute-based style representation enables per-axis control and better intent preservation at comparable style fidelity than free-text persona descriptions used in existing methods. A human expert evaluation found the augmented conversations plausible, with favorable ratings for intent preservation, style naturalness, and multilingual authenticity. Finally, PersonaFactory conversations achieve comparable performance to original training data on capability benchmarks, are preferred on real user prompts at equal reply length, and adapt their reply style more closely to users without explicit steering.


### arXiv 2611.34777 - When Every Rollout Fails: Splicing Learner-Conditioned Options into Long-Horizon Agent RL

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-25T17:38:00Z
- authors: Haowen Hou, Yicheng Liu, Zhen Huang, Jiaqi Wang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34777

Group-based reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards often yields all-fail groups on long-horizon agent tasks: every sampled trajectory receives zero reward, leaving group-relative methods without a reward-based policy gradient. A common remedy supplies complete solutions from a stronger model, but full trajectories are expensive and expose the learner to long off-policy histories. We introduce SPLICE, which constructs local training targets from failed current-policy trajectories online. For each all-fail group, a proposal model selects a splice point in a current-policy trajectory and generates several bounded options from that point. We estimate each option's value using repeated current-policy continuations, convert positive estimates into a return-weighted local target, and fit the current policy on the options. We evaluate SPLICE across two policy families on two long-horizon agent tasks, Software Engineering with SWE-bench Verified and Deep Research with BrowseComp-Plus. SPLICE outperforms GRPO and full-trajectory guidance baselines in all four settings, improving over GRPO by 2.8--9.8 percentage points. On initially failed SWE tasks, it raises GRPO's graduation rate by 10.8 percentage points. Further experiments show that SPLICE improves performance while reducing costly proposal-model computation.


### arXiv 2611.34770 - Make the Adversary Spend More: Stateful Deception as a Cost-Exchange Primitive for AI-Era Cyber Defense

- categories: cs.CR, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T17:35:27Z
- authors: Alessandro Cantelli-Forti, Isabella Marasco, Hosam Alamleh, Giada Boschi, Michele Colajanni
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34770

Large language models (LLMs) are transforming the economics of intrusion by reducing the attacker's cost to scan and adapt, while tempting defenders into unbounded compute. We argue that cyber deception should be judged by the cost exchange it imposes, not by realism or detection, and that the primitive making that exchange favorable is adversary-specific persistent state. Rather than a honeypot that resets or merges sessions, each recognized adversary re-enters the world it believes it has compromised, kept coherent with its own past actions and isolated from every other adversary, while the language model acts only as a constrained renderer of explicit state, never as its store. We formalize the trade-off as a Deception Exchange Rate (DER), relating mission-weighted adversary effort burned to defender resources spent, and instantiate it in an SSH honeypot pairing Cowrie with a local LLM. In this paper, we focus on the defender-side evaluation of that exchange, while specifying, but not yet measuring, per-adversary state and the adversary side of the DER. Across five open-weight models, deterministic-layer and prompt optimizations cut per-session defender cost by around 63% against a naive all-LLM decoy.


### arXiv 2611.34763 - InterConRank: Joint Node Centrality in Interconnected Heterogeneous Networks

- categories: cs.SI, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T17:32:23Z
- authors: Hao Ren, Jiaojiao Jiang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34763

Two nodes with identical connections within their own layer can differ in importance if their neighbors in another layer occupy different positions in the network. We introduce InterConRank, a centrality algorithm that computes a separate node ranking for each layer of an interconnected network. Directed dependencies specify which layers supply scores to each receiving layer. InterConRank normalizes each type of link separately and updates all layers together, so a score can cross into a layer in one iteration and propagate along its internal links in the next. We prove convergence of this joint update and evaluate the resulting rankings on four scholarly networks. On all four networks, InterConRank gives the highest rank correlations with both h-index and total citations for nodes in the collaboration layer.


### arXiv 2611.34762 - TASK Memory: Beyond Text-Only Memory with Activation Steering and Knowledge Editing against Memory Hallucination

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T17:30:37Z
- authors: Jinkun Zhao, Wenjun Wu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34762

LLM agents increasingly rely on external memory, yet they still hallucinate about what they remember. Often the correct memory is retrieved and placed in the prompt, but the answer contradicts it or the memory is applied to the wrong question. We call these use-time memory hallucinations. Across five open models, we find that they arise from the conflict between parametric knowledge and external memory and take three forms: memory-ignoring, where the model returns to a stale fact in its weights (up to 46% of queries); memory-misapplying, where it attributes the memory to unrelated entities (up to 24%, and 49% when retrieval is combined with weight editing); and version confusion, where 53% of answers about revised facts assert the superseded value when both versions remain stored. We propose TASK memory, which goes beyond text-only memory by combining a Text note with Activation Steering and Knowledge editing that share one in-model address. The text holds the content. A removable knowledge edit supplies the address through its subject key and supports multi-hop composition. A pair of steering vectors decides, query by query, whether the model adopts the memory or keeps its own answer; the vectors store no content and control how the text memory is used. On frozen models under a pre-registered, test-once protocol, TASK reduces memory-ignoring hallucinations to at most 1.5% in all ten model-dataset cells and cuts neighbour misuse from 39.2% to 7.5% (+10.8 pp composite on CounterFact). It improves a 300-memory library over BM25 RAG by 16.4 pp, an advantage that holds at 3,000 memories, resolves fact revisions without fact IDs, and adds 7.5 pp of multi-hop accuracy on MQuAKE. 21 of 23 cross-model comparisons (8B-27B, four families) are significant, and TASK beats three recent context-steering methods in 9 of 10 model-dataset cells.


### arXiv 2611.34760 - One Model, Many Modes: Evolving System Prompt Ensembles for Creative Generation

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-25T17:29:33Z
- authors: Daiwei Chen, Anda He, Binwei Yao, Junjie Hu, Ramya Korlakai Vinayak
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34760

Preference-aligned LLMs can exhibit mode collapse, concentrating their output distributions around a narrow set of high-reward responses. This limits creative generation, which requires quality, novelty, and diversity. Existing test-time methods often emphasize diversity, giving less attention to whether individual responses are both high-quality and novel. This distinction matters in practical use, where users may seek a single compelling, original response. We formalize creative generation as a multi-objective distributional optimization problem and introduce SEPO, a training-free framework for System Prompt Ensemble Optimization. The framework combines prompt evolution with greedy max-min selection to identify behaviorally distinct system prompts under a quality constraint, then uniformly samples one prompt per response at inference time. Across four creative generation tasks with increasing quality requirements and three open-weight models, SEPO ranks first in 8 of 12 settings on the harmonic mean of quality, diversity, and novelty, while no baseline leads in more than two. On average, it raises LLM-judged diversity from 1.92 to 3.43 and novelty from 1.00 to 3.34 over base sampling (1-5 scale), with quality decreasing from 3.72 to 3.30. We further show that expanding the optimized ensemble increases separation among prompt-conditioned response distributions. These results support system prompt ensemble optimization as a promising approach to improving LLM creativity at inference time.


### arXiv 2611.34758 - Reading Fine-Tuning Diffs Through Lenses: Model Diffing for Backdoor Detection

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-25T17:28:59Z
- authors: Maximilian Petrowitsch, Ben Connors, Chris Kapulkin
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34758

We introduce a common scoring system based on token readouts to evaluate weight, activation and output-based model diffing methods for backdoor detection in fine-tuning processes. We also improve existing weight and activation based methods by incorporating the $J$-lens and its refinement, the $J{+}{+}$-lens. We test our setup in experiments where we compare a backdoored model with the base model from which it was fine-tuned. Our results show that incorporating the $J{+}{+}$-lens improves the weight-based method in all our settings, while the $J$-lens improves its ranks within single layers. The strongest single method is $ΔW J{+}{+}$, the weight difference decoded through the $J{+}{+}$-lens of the base model, which ranks a token naming the backdoor among its first ten tokens in 11 of our 12 settings. Moreover, we show that output and activation based methods such as ADL and Diff Mining can easily break if the backdoored model is penalised during fine-tuning for giving the backdoor's words probability on clean inputs, whereas the weight-based methods are not affected. Conversely, permuting the hidden units or training only the layers that read the residual stream hides the backdoor from the weight-based methods but not from ADL and Diff Mining. No single method resists both attacks.


### arXiv 2611.34756 - Collect for Coverage, Replay with Evidence: Adaptive and Efficient On-Policy Distillation for Long-Horizon Agents

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-25T17:27:31Z
- authors: Haowen Hou, Gen Li, Ruilin Li, Yicheng Liu, Zhen Huang, Shuai Dong
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34756

Fully online on-policy distillation (OPD) is costly for long-horizon agents because each update depends on fresh environment rollouts. Prefix replay offers an efficient alternative by sampling current-student responses from stored histories, reducing idle time for long-tail rollouts and allowing each collected trajectory to support multiple updates. However, our analysis of the complete prefix replay pipeline identifies two limitations in long-horizon settings. In trajectory collection, teacher-only trajectories miss student-induced histories, while broader history coverage can reduce teacher reliability. In trajectory reuse, fixed position-based schedules cannot identify critical decisions that may occur at any trajectory depth. We introduce CARE-OPD (Collect for coverAge, Replay with Evidence) to address both limitations. Student-seeded teacher completions and response-level confidence gating expand history coverage while filtering unreliable updates. Hindsight replay allocation then uses downstream execution evidence to select consequential turns. Across long-horizon tasks Software Engineering and Deep Research, CARE-OPD outperforms online OPD and prior prefix replay baselines. On SWE-bench Verified, it raises task success to 59.5% while reducing end-to-end training time by 45% and environment actions by 44% relative to asynchronous online OPD.


### arXiv 2611.34751 - What Is a Theory Worth to an LLM Solver? Measuring, Inducing, and Merging Theories in Context

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T17:25:56Z
- authors: Jun Gao, Shuchao Bi
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34751

Mathematicians solve problems faster when they know the right theory. We ask how much a stated theory is worth to a large language model (LLM) solver, and whether a model can write that theory itself. In a length-matched protocol, adding the right theory to the context of Qwen3-14B raises pass@1 on pattern waiting-time problems from 0.37 to 0.83 and cuts the median tokens of correct solutions from 10.2k to 6.3k, while worked examples of the same length do not help. The gain shrinks as the solver and its token budget grow (+0.46, +0.26, +0.07), and at a long budget it comes almost entirely from finishing within budget: solutions that finish are correct 98--100\% of the time in every condition. To separate induction from recall, we plant unpublished theorems in custom probability families and measure how much of the oracle theory's gain an induced theory recovers. A strong open model, required to explore the training problems with code before writing, recovers 60\% of the gain on one planted family, 48\% on a second, and almost none on a third, where it finds the right form but not the condition that makes the form computable. No single induced note is complete, but a strong model that merges several notes recovers 61\% of the gain on the classic family, above the best single note (47\%). Theories are worth the reasoning they save, and models can find their pieces before they can assemble them.


### arXiv 2611.34745 - How Well Do Vision-Language Scene Graphs Serve as Memory for Embodied Question Answering?

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T17:23:01Z
- authors: Divyansh Pandey, Shubh Garg, Vedant Khandelwal, Niyati Rawal, Amit Sheth
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34745

Embodied QA needs memory, but answer accuracy mixes retained evidence, reading errors and question priors. For a simulated multi-room scene-graph pipeline we define answerability: the fraction of questions a fixed rule-based reader answers correctly from the graph alone. The baseline recovers 24.1% of visible object types and its room labels flicker. Four targeted repairs double recall, raise answerability from 27.2% to 37.4%, and improve retrained QA from 71.4% to 75.9%, yet better object F1 can lower answerability. A question-type prior beats this reader on every predicted graph. In one real-video test, graph memory lowers accuracy. These exploratory findings favour scoring retained evidence alongside accuracy.


### arXiv 2611.34708 - From Shared Patterns to Individual Evidence: Two-Stage Reasoning for Emerging Entities in Temporal Knowledge Graphs

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-25T17:01:07Z
- authors: Jinchuan Zhang, Lei Gao, Hongyu Jiang, Chong Mu, Chenang Cai, Ke Yan
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34708

Temporal knowledge graphs (TKGs) continually introduce emerging entities whose interaction histories evolve from zero-shot debuts into sparse, bursty few-shot trajectories. However, current paradigms are fragmented: pattern-transfer methods lack catalog-wide event memory, whereas online memory models suffer from endpoint-role asymmetry that degrades unseen-answer ranking. Furthermore, sparse early interactions exhibit both long-range dispersion and local redundancy under skewed distribution. To address these challenges, we propose INSPIRE (Integrating Shared Patterns and Individual Evidence for Reasoning), a two-stage forecasting framework over a known catalog. In Stage I, INSPIRE learns an availability-aware shared-pattern predictor that fuses event-updated memory across the full candidate catalog while applying coordinated query- and catalog-side masking. Subsequently, Stage II freezes this predictor and optimizes a lightweight individual-evidence adapter (EA) combining textual semantic priors with correlation-weighted long-range event statistics under support- and role-balanced co-answer supervision with prior-odds debiasing and soft residual alignment. In addition, we decompose evaluation by endpoint roles and track longitudinal emerging-answer cohorts via query-micro and answer-entity macro metrics. Across four benchmarks based on the ICEWS and GDELT event datasets, INSPIRE improves Emerging MRR by 16.73%-88.83% over the strongest baselines. Relative to paired Stage I predictors, EA further increases Emerging MRR by 8.28%-32.63%, Answer-new MRR by 7.81%-41.24%, and cohort macro MRR by 9.64%-39.94%, gains attributed to semantic priors and candidate-level score competition suppressing mismatched established competitors without zero-support prior shortcuts. The official code is publicly available at https://github.com/KcAcoZhang/INSPIRE.


### arXiv 2611.34707 - When the Proxy Is Wrong: Mismatch-Corrected Bandits for Adaptive Data Sampling

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-25T17:00:51Z
- authors: Amine Saadi, Elissa Mhanna, Raphaël Féraud, Gwénolé Lecorvé
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34707

Data allocation methods often use a small proxy model to select training data for a larger target model. However, this proxy can rank data differently from the target, leading to suboptimal training performance. To address this problem, we model the mismatch between the proxy and target models and incorporate it into the allocation decision. To do so, the problem is cast as a multi-fidelity bandit, where each arm is a data intervention. The proxy reward is observed every round, whereas the target reward is revealed only on rare audits of the same update. These paired observations identify arm-wise mismatches without assuming a shared offset or preservation of rankings. Mismatch-corrected bandits correct the proxy with paired residuals, audit only uncertain decisions, and pace the audit budget over training. From a theoretical viewpoint, we prove a regret decomposition and lower bounds under stationarity. These bounds show that proxy-only allocation can incur linear regret and that a minimum number of paired audits is necessary. Under abrupt changes, using the audit budget early can also incur linear regret. A sliding-window policy that spreads audits over training achieves regret of order $\sqrt{Γ_T T\log T}$ where $Γ_T$ denotes the number of change points. Experimentally, we test the approach in a closed-loop proof of concept on CivilComments-WILDS, where BERT-Small guides the training of BERT-Large. Mismatch correction improves worst-group accuracy over a target-only policy with the same target-model budget. Spreading audits over training improves worst-group accuracy by 6.3 to 10.5 percentage points at the same audit count.


### arXiv 2611.34703 - Maco-RL: Scaling Agent Capabilities with Mixed-Task Reinforcement Learning

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T16:59:27Z
- authors: Jiacheng Zhang, Qixing Zhou, Haiyang Wang, Qipeng Gu, Shuzhe Wu, Feiyang Pan
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34703

Mixed-task reinforcement learning (MixRL) directly trains a unified policy across domains to continually expand agent capabilities without training separate specialists or requiring an extra distillation stage. Despite its appeal, scaling MixRL poses significant challenges in achieving stable and efficient mixed-task learning, effectively allocating rollout compute, and supporting continual domain expansion. To address these challenges, we propose Maco-RL, an approach that effectively scales MixRL. Specifically, Maco-RL employs a quota-based scheduler for asynchronous rollouts across multiple task domains, thereby stabilizing training by balancing gradient contributions and mitigating off-policy effects. Along an orthogonal axis, Maco-RL further introduces Tri-Buffer Sampling (TBS) to allocate compute among samples with varying levels of mastery, promoting sustained frontier exploration while preserving acquired capabilities. Experiments on five benchmarks show Maco-RL raises Qwen3.5-27B's overall score from 43.6 to 54.2 and then to 60.1 with continual expansion, reaching the frontier held by Qwen3.8-27B, which is further pushed outward by +5.7. Maco-RL also outperforms single-task RL with less compute for policy updates. These results support MixRL as a practical and compute-efficient post-training approach with substantial potential to strengthen general agent capabilities.


### arXiv 2611.34698 - A Failure-Driven Data Flywheel for Post-Training Vision-Language-Action Models

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T16:54:15Z
- authors: Yuhao Luo, Zihao Sheng, Zilin Huang, Zhengyang Wan, Boyue Wang, Ruolin Yang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34698

Pretrained vision-language-action (VLA) models rarely satisfy all behavioral requirements of a new platform, and teams without a large deployed fleet lack the data to address the resulting failures. We propose a failure-driven data flywheel for post-training such models. It constructs target scenarios absent from the available data by inserting task-relevant entities into reconstructed real-world scenes, and evaluates the model against each behavioral requirement of the target task. Failure rates form a failure map across task conditions and difficulty levels, which is re-evaluated after each round and assigns larger data generation quotas to conditions where the model still fails. We instantiate the framework in autonomous driving for occluded-pedestrian emergence and yielding at stop-sign-controlled intersections, and evaluate it on two VLA models and one non-VLA planner in simulation and on a real vehicle. For occluded-pedestrian emergence, five rounds of post-training improve the critical-phase collision-free rate on the sealed test set from 0.261 to 0.579 for AutoVLA and from 0.390 to 0.765 for ReCogDrive, while the unnecessary braking rate in paired control scenarios never exceeds 0.013. On a full-scale vehicle without real-world training, post-trained ReCogDrive achieves 4 of 5 safe passages, versus 1 of 5 for the base model. Fine-grained evaluation reveals a pattern obscured by the collision-free rate alone: both VLA models learn anticipatory deceleration before reaching the occluding vehicle, while braking after pedestrian emergence remains limited. For stop-sign yielding, the stop-before-line rate rises from 0.100 to 0.897 for AutoVLA and from 0.000 to 0.667 for ReCogDrive. With sequential training from the pedestrian-capability models, it reaches 0.599 and 0.358, respectively, while the pedestrian collision-free rate decreases by at most 0.032.


### arXiv 2611.34687 - Evacuation Traffic Prediction for Dynamic Transportation Networks over Multiple Hurricanes: A Generalized Graph Learning Approach

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T16:48:09Z
- authors: Md Nafees Fuad Rafi, Samiul Hasan
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34687

High traffic demand during hurricane evacuations causes severe congestion on major highways, leading to delays and safety risks for evacuees. Predicting evacuation traffic in real-time can significantly enhance evacuation management efforts. However, evacuation traffic is highly dynamic and uncertain due to evolving hurricane characteristics and diverse population response. Each hurricane has unique characteristics and evacuation contexts, resulting in different spatial-temporal traffic patterns. Moreover, the structure of detector-based transportation networks varies across multiple hurricanes. Hence, a model trained on the data from one hurricane may not generalize well for others. This study proposes a generalized dynamic graph learning framework using traffic detectors and evacuation data from 12 historical hurricanes. Leveraging the dynamic nature of transportation network, we develop a Graph Convolutional Long Short-Term Memory (GCN-LSTM) model that predicts evacuation traffic up to 6 hours in advance. For an unseen hurricane (Milton, 2024), the model shows strong generalizability, achieving an RMSE of 280.6 and R2 of 0.96 for 1-hour prediction, and an RMSE of 459.8 and R2 of 0.89 for overall 6-hours prediction. We also introduce a modular training approach, where we evaluate performance improvements by sequentially incorporating data from multiple hurricanes. The proposed model outperforms state-of-the-art baseline models for overall 6-hour prediction horizons. Ablation experiments show an 8.4% improvement in RMSE for the proposed dynamic GCN-LSTM model compared to its static counterpart. Traffic management officials can use the developed model to understand future traffic conditions during hurricane evacuation in advance and take proactive measures to reduce delays during evacuation.


### arXiv 2611.34681 - Find the Clue, Not the Scene: Rethinking Reasoning for Entity Recognition in Vision-Language Models

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T16:44:26Z
- authors: Kyungmin Min, Soyeon Kim, Junseok Kim, Kyomin Jung
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34681

Answering a knowledge-based visual question requires first recognizing the entity in the image and then recalling knowledge about it. Thinking vision-language models (VLMs) reason for thousands of tokens before answering, yet it remains underexplored how well such thinking VLMs perform each of these two steps. We therefore analyze thinking VLMs on this task and make two key observations. (1) Recognition is the bottleneck, since 76.3% of errors across four models on ViQuAE stem from misrecognizing the entity, and 84.9% of these failures are resolved once the entity name is provided in the question. (2) Recognition is driven by entity-recall clues rather than by general descriptions of the image. An entity-recall clue is a distinctive visual detail that helps the model recall the entity, such as keys, a traditional attribute of Saint Peter. Based on these findings, we propose ClueRL, a reinforcement learning approach that extends Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) with an advantage localized to the entity-recall clue in the thinking trace. Rather than injecting new entity knowledge, ClueRL changes how VLMs think: it leads them to attend more closely to visual details and spot these clues, helping them use the knowledge they already have. Consequently, it outperforms both GRPO and a perception-reward baseline on three entity recognition benchmarks. Moreover, this clue-seeking behavior generalizes beyond entity recognition, the only task used for training. Since recognition is the bottleneck, the gains carry over to knowledge-based visual question answering. They even extend to general multimodal reasoning, raising Qwen3.5-4B by 4.4 and 4.0 points on the ScienceQA and MMMU subsets of MMStar, respectively.


### arXiv 2611.34675 - RoboEvo$^2$: Recursive Robot Self-Improvement via Harness-Policy Co-Evolution

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI, cs.CV
- published: 2026-11-25T16:39:10Z
- authors: Chenzhi Liu, Jiehong Lin, Wei Huang, Xuzhao Huang, Haoyuan Li, Yang Zhang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34675

Robots should adapt to unfamiliar environments and acquire new skills through interaction, then execute those skills efficiently with practice. Vision-language-action policies provide efficient control, but often need additional human demonstrations to handle unfamiliar settings; reasoning agents can discover solutions using broad prior knowledge and robot-control APIs, but repeated deliberation and repair are costly. Inspired by human learning through reasoning and practice, we propose RoboEvo$^2$, a framework for recursive harness-policy co-evolution. In Policy-to-Harness (P2H) evolution, policy failures guide a coding agent to search complementary Code, Code+ICL, and Code+Policy routes, consolidating verified solutions into reusable skills, routing strategies, and experience memory. In Harness-to-Policy (H2P) evolution, verified trajectories provide supervised training, while Counterfactual Preference Distillation (CPD) teaches the policy to favor successful corrections over failures from the same starting state. The improved policy provides stronger motor skills for subsequent search, while its remaining failures redirect harness evolution, closing the reciprocal loop. Through recursive co-evolution without additional human demonstrations, RoboEvo$^2$ improves standalone success from 11.0% to 84.1% and hybrid success from 30.4% to 90.5% on LIBERO-PRO, with similar gains and reduced agentic costs on RoboTwin C2R. Further experiments demonstrate autonomous task acquisition, cross-embodiment harness transfer, and 95.0% hybrid success on the Unitree G1-D. Our models and code will be publicly released.


### arXiv 2611.34671 - DemoFoundry: Toward Scalable Photorealistic Robot Data with Coding Agents and Generative Rendering

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T16:37:10Z
- authors: Ruixiang Wang, Xinyue Yao, Xuanning Zhou, Yueci Deng, Yongyi Su, Kui Jia
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34671

Scaling robot learning requires demonstrations that combine realistic visual observations with reliable action supervision. Simulation-based synthesis faces a substantial visual gap relative to real environments, while video-only generation struggles to maintain physically consistent interactions and provide aligned action labels. We present DemoFoundry, an automated framework that couples coding agents with generative rendering to synthesize photorealistic and physically grounded robot demonstrations. Coding agents both construct aligned real--proxy video pairs from existing robot episodes to train a video renderer and write executable interaction programs to generate new demonstrations. Given a scene reference and task instruction, an agent reconstructs task-relevant geometry and writes a control program without requiring target-task human demonstrations. Program execution in a physics simulator produces proxy videos with aligned robot states and actions. The video renderer translates these proxy videos into scene-consistent, photorealistic observations, reducing the need for detailed appearance modeling in each scene. Automated task-execution checks select successful trajectories for rendering and policy learning. Experiments show improved visual quality and action--video consistency. Policies trained on the generated demonstrations outperform raw-simulation training and approach the performance of training on an equal number of real demonstrations.


### arXiv 2611.34662 - Multi-Party Authorization of Maintenance Prescriptions on a Permissioned Blockchain

- categories: cs.SE, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T16:32:38Z
- authors: Siyuan Chen, Ebru Turanoglu Bekar, Anders Skoogh
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34662

In industrial prescriptive maintenance, an equipment supplier's model may recommend an intervention that a plant schedules, a contractor executes, and a quality or safety function approves. A shared ledger records who submitted the prescription, yet cannot check whether the agreed model and decision rule produced it. We require permissioned-blockchain endorsers to verify a succinct proof of that calculation before releasing a work order. On a four-organization Hyperledger Fabric testbed using a public engine benchmark, this stopped all nine contested or mis-operated prescriptions, whereas hash checks accepted four that either raised simulated maintenance cost or reintroduced late failures.


### arXiv 2611.34656 - Do We Still Need Reinforcement Learning When We Can Read the World Model?

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-25T16:29:37Z
- authors: Mingchao Zhang, Miduo Cui, Kangze He, Yuanjun Li, Dapeng Li, Ce Zhang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34656

Reinforcement learning has achieved remarkable success in sequential decision-making, but learning effective policies often requires substantial interaction experience. Model-based reinforcement learning alleviates this dependence by leveraging world models, which are typically used to support planning or policy optimization. In this paper, we investigate an alternative paradigm in which autonomous LLM-based coding agents directly synthesize control policies by reasoning over readable world models. By examining environment source code, these agents translate environmental knowledge into executable decision rules and iteratively refine their policies using online feedback or offline data, without task-specific RL training. Experiments across diverse decision-making benchmarks show that synthesized policies match or outperform conventional RL methods while requiring substantially less experience. Further analyses reveal interpretable decision rules and how they evolve through feedback-driven refinement. Comparisons with policies generated without source code access and experiments under challenging conditions further demonstrate the benefits of readable world models. These findings suggest that direct policy synthesis can reduce reliance on trial and error, challenging the necessity of task-specific RL training.


### arXiv 2611.34646 - REE: A Scientific Research Entrance Exam for AI Agents

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T16:25:19Z
- authors: Zeqi Zhou, Minghao Liu, Jiarui Liu, Qingcheng Zeng, Rui Yang, Tianyu Liu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34646

Autonomous research agents are emerging as a promising approach to scientific problem-solving across disciplines, owing to their growing ability to improve a solution through repeated experiments. Evaluating this ability requires benchmarks that pose open-ended research problems and measure improvements over established scientific methods. We introduce REE (Research Entrance Exam), a benchmark that tests AI agents on scientific research. It consists of 100 open-ended research problems spanning five broad scientific areas and 23 domains. To ensure high quality, every problem is drawn from active research in its field and individually reviewed by domain experts. Each problem is posed as a task of iterative improvement: an agent has up to 12 hours to improve a working starter solution, and REE scores the result on a common scale against an established expert solution evaluated under comparable conditions. By this measure, two of the four frontier agents we evaluate, Claude Opus 5.5 and GPT-6.1 Sol, outperform the expert solutions on 86% and 81% of problems, across all five scientific areas. Their runs also show how they do so: both agents surpass the expert solutions within the first two hours on average, and their best solutions mostly tailor or combine established methods of the field. REE thus provides a unified framework for evaluating autonomous scientific research, capturing both improvements over established methods and the research processes behind them.


### arXiv 2611.34644 - Motion Beyond Pixels: Learning Future Visual Representations for Object-Centric Dynamics

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.RO
- published: 2026-11-25T16:24:11Z
- authors: Chengzhi Zhao, Juelin Li, Zhennan Wang, Sheng Wang, Zhuoman Liu, Yuhao Lu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34644

Many embodied world models predict future states in latent spaces optimized for RGB reconstruction, which do not explicitly prioritize object-level semantics and geometry relevant to motion generation. We present MotionRep, a visual representation world model that jointly generates future DINO representations and 3D object flow, coupling semantic and geometric information with object-centric dynamics. To make high-dimensional DINO features amenable to generative modeling, we introduce DINO-VAE, which combines temporal and channel compression with latent regularization to produce compact latents while preserving semantic and geometric information. Conditioned on an initial observation, object query points, and a language instruction, a unified diffusion transformer predicts future visual representations and 3D object flow through joint attention. MotionRep employs asymmetric modality-specific noise schedules to keep the visual stream ahead of 3D object flow during joint denoising, allowing progressively refined future visual representations to guide flow generation. Experiments on simulated and real-world manipulation benchmarks demonstrate that MotionRep outperforms pixel-based baselines in 3D object flow accuracy and generalization to unseen scenes, while requiring only three denoising steps for inference. These results support predictive visual representations as an effective future-state space for object-centric dynamics modeling.


### arXiv 2611.34637 - Carrying Feature Ablations Across Sparse Autoencoder Seeds

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-25T16:20:40Z
- authors: Karl Li
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34637

A vision encoder turns an image into one vector per patch, refined layer by layer. A sparse autoencoder (SAE) writes each vector of one layer as a sum of learned directions, most with a coefficient of zero. Researchers call each direction a feature and edit the encoder by removing one feature. An SAE trained from another random seed learns different features. Earlier work matches the two SAEs' features one to one by how closely their directions agree. Matched features can point alike yet respond to different image parts, so removing them may change the encoder differently. We remove a feature and, separately, its match, then compare the changes in the encoder's output on the same held out images. When the second change nearly reproduces the first, we say the removal carries across seeds. A setting fixes a DINOv2 or SigLIP layer and its SAE feature count. At our tolerance, the removal carries for 12% to 70% of matched pairs across three settings. The share is 0% in two controls: an SAE whose directions stay random and, post hoc, matches shuffled among trained features. Post hoc, predicting which removals carry is more accurate with layer contributions than with directions. Some incorrect predictions remain. One persists on independent images. So pairs can be screened at the layer. Measuring the output detects errors this screen misses.


### arXiv 2611.34635 - ZeroHero: Training Small Language Agents from Scratch

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T16:20:13Z
- authors: Nikita Sorokin, Zoya Volovikova, Alexey Skrynnik, Aleksandr Panov
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34635

Large pretrained language models provide capable policies for interactive tasks, but how much model capacity is necessary for a specialized language agent? We introduce ZeroHero, a framework for training small language agents (SLAs) from scratch. In each environment, a large language model acts as the expert, and we collect its successful trajectories. We pretrain each SLA, which has fewer than 50 million parameters, to predict the expert's responses given the interaction history. We then post-train the agent on its own interactions using proximal policy optimization (PPO). During post-training, reasoning alignment supplements environment rewards with expert supervision on the language the agent generates alongside its actions. We evaluate the agents in three multistep environments where acting requires both understanding and generating language. In Crafter, agents interpret textual observations and generate reasoning before acting. In WebShop, they translate shopping instructions into search queries. In Alfworld-Dialogue, they ask clarifying questions and use the answers to guide household actions. The agents approach expert performance on WebShop and surpass their respective demonstration experts on Crafter training rollouts and held-out Alfworld-Dialogue tasks. An interactive demo is available at https://zoyav.github.io/-ZoyaV-zerohero-demo.


### arXiv 2611.34634 - LatentRL-QAS: Transfer via Latent Reinforcement Learning for Quantum Architecture Search

- categories: quant-ph, cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-25T16:20:02Z
- authors: Maria Ilektra Plakitsi, Sebastian Feld, Akash Kundu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34634

Reinforcement learning (RL) emerges as a prominent approach to quantum architecture search (QAS), the automated design of quantum circuits. However, transferring what an agent has learned from one number of qubits to another is still weakly explored: the state, the action space and the neural network of a state-of-the-art RL-QAS agent all grow with the number of qubits, which makes transfer learning across qubit counts prohibitive. We make this transfer possible by introducing LatentRL-QAS, a latent space transfer and refresh framework for RL-based QAS. At its core, a circuit variational autoencoder encodes a circuit as one token per gate and maps circuits on any number of qubits into the same latent space. An RL agent navigates this space instead of adding gates, so its state, actions and network have the same size at every qubit count. The latent space is periodically refreshed with the agent's new discoveries and, for a larger system, grown from the solutions of the smaller one, so what is transferred is the representation of circuits, not network weights or past experience. To show transfer, we consider absolutely maximally entangled (AME) states, a resource for quantum error correction. LatentRL-QAS discovers the five- and six-qubit AME states without being shown them and prepares them in 89-99% of evaluation episodes, whereas the baseline RL-QAS fails to find them with the same budget, even with a network 10 to 17 times larger. LatentRL-QAS remains robust under depolarizing noise with single-qubit gate errors amplified 10x and two-qubit gate errors 5x compared with IBM quantum hardware. By decoupling the RL agent from the number of qubits, LatentRL-QAS lets quantum architecture search reuse circuit representations learned on smaller problems, providing a foundation for the transferable design of circuits across quantum optimization problems.


### arXiv 2611.34627 - DecisionReplay: A Corpus and Offline Simulator for Ensembling Decision Models

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.DB
- published: 2026-11-25T16:13:45Z
- authors: Dean Ariel, Seffi Cohen
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34627
