Comparing ensembles, routers and cascades requires the output of every model on every item, and its cost. Stored collections built for routing keep no per-option probabilities. We provide a dataset on which ensemble methods, routers and cascades are simulated quickly, without new model calls. DecisionReplay holds the probability vectors of 17 commercial decision models, endpoints that return a probability for every answer option, on 35 benchmarks, with the token counts, billed cost and measured latency of every call, all released. A deterministic simulator replays any policy that selects, combines or cascades them. One well-chosen model per benchmark is hard to beat: pooling all models loses 2.7 accuracy points to it, and stacking gains 1.0 points at 3.4 times its cost. The models are wrong together: two that are both wrong choose the same wrong option in 72% of cases. The best router matches that model at 64% of its cost, and certified early exit cuts the cost of a stacked ensemble by 43% without changing an answer. DecisionReplay makes it cheap and fast to test whether a new method changes these results.


### arXiv 2611.34624 - HoMe: Toward Practical Harness Optimization of Memory Systems for AI Assistants

- categories: cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-25T16:11:53Z
- authors: Yifei Xu, Li Yu, Zhaoyang Liu, Ce Wang, Sen Huang, Yanyan Shen
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34624

The behavior of memory systems for long-running AI assistants is governed by a harness, which consists of the code, prompts, and configurations for memory construction, maintenance, retrieval, and answer generation. However, optimizing the harness of a memory system remains largely manual, and unrestricted editing alone does not ensure structural improvements. In this paper, we introduce HoMe, an evaluation-driven framework that iteratively evaluates candidate harnesses and derives each new candidate from any previously evaluated harness by editing any part of it. HoMe combines session-level memory versioning with execution traces to identify shared failure mechanisms across cases and localize them to the responsible sessions and harness components. It encourages early structural exploration through candidate gating and reduces evaluation cost through smoke tests, adaptive sampling, and the reuse of constructed memory. We validate HoMe with the open-source memory system ReMe on two large-scale long-horizon memory benchmarks, LongMemEval-S and BEAM. With six formal candidate evaluations per benchmark, after a full initial evaluation, HoMe discovers harnesses that improve end-task performance over the originally strong harness while consuming far fewer query-time tokens. The code is available at https://github.com/agentscope-ai/ReMe.


### arXiv 2611.34623 - From Models to Services: Systematizing Security Assurance for Model-as-a-Service

- categories: cs.CR, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T16:11:04Z
- authors: Jianming Zhou, Yang Luo, Guangpu Chen, Zhaoyu Chen, Yifeng Gao, Qingni Shen
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34623

Large language models are mainly delivered as cloud services, in which the assets of different principals are coupled within a single service pipeline, while the related security research is scattered across several fields with differing threat models. This paper systematizes this research from a service perspective. We decompose Model-as-a-Service (MaaS) into a five-layer stack and five trust boundaries, define six adversary classes and six security goals, use them to code 120 studies, and evaluate 50 defenses under a unified set of criteria. The results show that 44.2% of the studies concentrate on behavioral integrity, while service integrity, availability and cost, and accountability together account for only 13.3%. Defenses are evaluated on production services at less than half the rate of attacks, and none of the cryptographic or hardware mechanisms that protect against the provider can be adopted unilaterally by customers. Finally, we propose a research agenda by comparison with current standards and regulations.


### arXiv 2611.34612 - Do We Really Need an LLM for Weight-Space Programming? A Deterministic Forward-Only Paradigm and Its Capability Boundaries

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T16:01:05Z
- authors: Lin Yao
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34612

Recent work on Weight-Space Programming uses LLMs to edit neural weights with forward passes only, conflating LLM scripting with the underlying algebra of forward-only editing. We show that for core structured editing regimes, an LLM optimizer is unnecessary, and introduce Forward Weight-Space Programming (FWSP): a deterministic pipeline combining probing, localization, stratified operators (O1-O4), automatic blind routing (83.3% vs. 33.3% for a 27B LLM with the same tool library), and paired verification with zero additional per-token computation. For reasoning LLMs, we propose Protected FisherGate, a closed-form covariance-whitened readout edit orthogonal to normal-reasoning states: on DeepSeek-R1-0528-8B, a single frozen math-calibrated patch achieves 84.2% HumanEval pass@1 (1K budget; 75.4 +/- 9.4% across 3/5 Stage-4-passing subsets), beating 512-token-reserved budget forcing by +14.6 pp (-23.4% tokens) while transferring across HF-to-vLLM backends on full GSM8K and MATH-500. Across five modalities and thirteen models (50K to 27B dense and FP8 MoE), we further prove and verify two operator boundaries, subspace entanglement and frozen representation span, where FWSP-localized LoRA bridges forward-only and gradient adaptation.


### arXiv 2611.34603 - VisualMontage: Manipulating the Beliefs of Vision-Language Agents with Authentic Image Sequences

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.MA, cs.SI
- published: 2026-11-25T15:57:55Z
- authors: Jinwei Hu, Lai Wei, Zhuoyun Li, Youcheng Sun, Yi Dong, Xiaowei Huang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34603

As agents based on vision-language models (VLMs) increasingly interpret social-media content, errors in their analysis may lend credibility to rumors and amplify their spread. In this paper, we study the problem of visual cognitive collusion, in which coordinated posts combine authentic images and locally faithful captions to induce VLM agents to infer and accept attacker-chosen target claims through their own reasoning. To enable systematic evaluation of this cognitive threat, we introduce a new benchmark comprising 544 target cases and 33.2K candidate images across eight application domains. We further propose VisualMontage, a multi-agent framework for simulating such attacks through narrative planning, image selection and ordering, and caption assignment under iterative review. We evaluate this threat through extensive experiments across 12 vision-language models, examining modality effects, production-team components, and downstream defenses. Using identical attack feeds across models without victim-specific optimization, we demonstrate cross-model transferability, achieving mean attack success rates of 61.93% for proprietary models and 68.71% for open-weight models. Moreover, 92.65% of generated feeds pass independent model-based validity auditing. Even after downstream review, residual attack success rates range from 35.3% to 76.7% across the evaluated configurations. Our additional controlled comparisons further reveal that captions can improve model-rated posting naturalness while reducing attack success. Together, these findings reveal a novel cognitive threat in social-media interpretation and motivate safeguards that assess how authentic posts jointly shape agents' reasoning and conclusions.


### arXiv 2611.34579 - Tune for Retention, Not Just Gain: SFT Forgets Far Less than You Think

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T15:43:38Z
- authors: Nirmit Joshi, Nathan Srebro, Eran Malach
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34579

Catastrophic forgetting, the drastic loss of previously acquired capabilities when learning new ones, is a central challenge in language model fine-tuning. Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) is widely believed to be prone to such forgetting. Recent works have attributed this to the off-policy nature of SFT, arguing that on-policy learning is key to continual learning. In this paper, we challenge this premise. We show that while maximizing new-task performance can induce catastrophic forgetting, alternative hyperparameter choices (lower learning rate and batch size) can yield similar gains with little to no forgetting. With hyperparameters selected jointly for gain and retention, vanilla SFT retains prior capabilities far better than previously suggested and can be competitive with Self-Distillation Fine-Tuning (SDFT), an on-policy approach designed to mitigate forgetting. While SDFT can be more robust to hyperparameter choices, it also requires a retention-aware criterion to identify hyperparameter configurations that avoid catastrophic forgetting. Our results challenge the view that on-policy training is what enables learning with retention and raise broader questions about what drives catastrophic forgetting during fine-tuning.


### arXiv 2611.34576 - Procedure Inference as Memory: Learning Recruiter Decision Procedures from OKF-Structured Episodes in Multi-Session Hiring Assistants

- categories: cs.AI, cs.HC, cs.IR
- published: 2026-11-25T15:41:52Z
- authors: Praveen Kumar Bodigutla, Yizhi Zhang, Sai Vivek Kanaparthy, Arup De
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34576

Multi-session hiring assistants must infer evolving recruiter preferences and decision patterns across projects and time. We introduce Open Knowledge Format (OKF)-indexed episodic memory to organize interactions and retrieve related events through their lineage. We also introduce Procedure Memory, which learns reusable decision procedures from these episodes through prompted reflection or reinforcement learning. We compare these approaches with no memory, full-history context, and conversation search, and evaluate Jev-class models for index navigation and GPT-5.6 Luna as a lower-cost alternative to GPT-5.4. In our evaluation, OKF-indexed retrieval achieves low-priority preference F1 scores of 0.93-0.95 at medium and high preference overlap between hiring projects. It significantly outperforms full-history context and conversation search in both settings, and no memory at high overlap, while using 69% fewer context tokens than full history. Procedure Memory reduces conversational turns by up to 39% relative to no memory. Qualitative and long-horizon evaluations provide evidence of temporal reasoning, with RL-learned procedures achieving 60% on both correct abstention and exact-field identification. Jev-class models improve conversational efficiency but more frequently miss temporally relevant evidence, highlighting the importance of evidence selection. On PersonaMem-v2, Procedure Memory with OKF retrieval achieves 50.2% accuracy versus 48.6% for full-history context and 29.0% for no memory, using 90.5% fewer reader input tokens than full history. GPT-5.6 Luna performs comparably to GPT-5.4 at lower cost. These findings highlight the value of structured episodic memory and learned decision procedures for efficient personalization and temporal reasoning.


### arXiv 2611.34575 - Sage-Med: Scaling Medical Multimodal Intelligence

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T15:39:45Z
- authors: Sage-Med Team,  :, Ming Hu, Cheng Tang, Wei Li, Rongbin Li
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34575

Establishing effective scaling laws for medical multimodal large language models (MLLMs) remains a persistent and fundamental challenge. Our systematic evaluation shows that existing medical MLLMs tend to underperform frontier general-purpose MLLMs across a range of medical tasks, while scaling model size primarily improves text capabilities rather than multimodal perception, and enabling reasoning modes fails to close this visual gap. To investigate how medical multimodal intelligence scales in practice, we introduce Sage-Med-Preview, a 35B-parameter medical multimodal model with 3B activated parameters, built by combining stage-specific curation over 322.7 million candidate records with a progressive training curriculum spanning medical pretraining, staged supervised fine-tuning, and adaptive reinforcement learning. Across this curriculum, progressive supervised refinement and adaptive reinforcement learning yield consistent stage-wise gains, substantially advancing both specialized visual understanding and medical language capabilities. Evaluated against 77 competing models across 238 clinical tasks spanning 61 public and 13 private multi-center benchmarks in more than 40 modalities, Sage-Med-Preview achieves an average score of 58.26% on the 31 public multimodal benchmarks. It outperforms the state-of-the-art open-source medical model Hulu-Med-235B-A22B at 50.99% and the general scientific model Intern-S2-Preview at 52.24%, and approaches the advanced proprietary model GPT-6.2-Sol across multiple tasks such as comprehensive multimodal understanding, report generation, and visual grounding. Although substantial challenges remain in complex medical reasoning and multi-step clinical workflows, this report provides empirical evidence, actionable data and training recipes, and a rigorous benchmark toward scalable clinical multimodal intelligence.


### arXiv 2611.34563 - On Utilization of Edge-AI for condition monitoring towards predictive maintenance: A use case in dairy processing using explainable AI

- categories: cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-25T15:33:26Z
- authors: Rahul Umesh Mhapsekar, Norah O'Shea, Omer Ali, Steven Davy, Lizy Abraham
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34563

The Industrial Internet of Things (IIoT) and Artificial Intelligence (AI) are driving Industry 4.0, enhancing productivity, automation, and efficiency in manufacturing systems. This study develops an Edge-AI-based condition monitoring framework, as a step towards predictive maintenance, for a rotary valve in a dairy processing pilot plant, using vibration data collected via a low-power IIoT sensor node and processed on an edge device.


### arXiv 2611.34550 - An Information-Theoretic Analysis of Information Hiding in Semantic Communication

- categories: cs.IT, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T15:26:33Z
- authors: Yanhao Huo, Shijun Xiang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34550

Semantic communication shifts the transmission goal from the accurate recovery of syntactic symbols to the reliable conveyance of semantic content, yet a systematic information-theoretic characterization of information hiding at the semantic layer is still missing. Building on the semantic information theory of Niu-Zhang and the classical information hiding theory of Moulin-O'Sullivan, this paper establishes an information-theoretic framework for information hiding under semantic communication. The classical hiding model is extended to a two-layer structure (semantic host, syntactic host, syntactic composite signal, syntactic received, semantic received) bridged by synonymous mappings f_s, f_x and the inverse mapping f_y^{-1}, subject to four distortion constraints: syntactic transparency, semantic fidelity, syntactic perturbation, and semantic destruction. The capacity is cast as a Stackelberg game with the hider moving first and the payoff J(Q,A) = I(U~; Y~ | K) - I(U~; S~ | K), yielding the single-letter maximin C^s = max_Q min_A J(Q,A), whose achievability and converse follow from random coding and joint typicality decoding. Relative to the classical capacity, comparison payoffs built from the up/down semantic mutual information give the endpoint sandwich C^s_end(0,1) <= C^s <= C^s_end(1,0) with the classical value C in the same interval, while closing semantic tolerance yields C^s_no-tol <= C_cl under a fair comparison. These results clarify that semantic hiding is not unconditionally superior to classical hiding. Binary, ternary, and Gaussian examples confirm the degeneration under one-to-one mappings and the capacity loss under coarsening.


### arXiv 2611.34545 - Recalling the Past to Answer the Present: Context-Aware Multi-Evidence Reasoning for Streaming Video Question Answering

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T15:24:02Z
- authors: Cui Yakun, Tiantian Geng, Huixiang Kong, Xingqun Qi, Sirui Han, Yike Guo
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34545

Vision-language models (VLMs) have achieved remarkable progress in video understanding, yet existing video question answering (VQA) benchmarks mainly evaluate models in offline settings. Real-world streaming scenarios instead require models to answer context-dependent queries by retrieving and reasoning over evidence distributed across the video history. We introduce StreamRecall, a benchmark for context-aware Multi evidence reasoning in streaming video QA. StreamRecall requires models to retrieve, associate, and reason over temporally scattered evidence to answer queries at the appropriate moment. It contains 4,983 questions over 462 videos, with context-aligned query times and fine-grained temporal evidence annotations. We further propose the Evidence Utilization Cascade to diagnose model reasoning through evidence localization, grounding, chain completion, and answer derivation. To enable efficient long-term streaming reasoning, we present EviStream, a training-free causal visual memory mechanism that preserves reasoning-critical evidence under a fixed KV budget. Experiments reveal substantial gaps between current VLMs and realistic streaming requirements, establishing StreamRecall as a foundation for developing evidence-aware streaming video intelligence.


### arXiv 2611.34544 - HAS: Hierarchical Adaptive Frame Sampling for Video Understanding

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T15:23:58Z
- authors: Yuyao Sun, Daiqi Shi, Shuang Li, Liao Zhang, Yang Cai, Deqing Wang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34544

Due to context-length constraints, most MLLMs cannot process full-length videos and therefore rely on sampling a subset of frames as input. However, existing sampling methods, ranging from uniform sampling to relevance-based selection, are often driven by a single sampling principle and thus struggle to accommodate the heterogeneous evidential requirements of different queries: some demand a holistic understanding of how the video unfolds over time, while others hinge on fine-grained events within a short temporal window. To address this limitation, we propose \textbf{Hierarchical Adaptive Frame Sampling (HAS)}, a two-stage frame sampling framework. In the first stage, \emph{Backbone Frame Construction}, we apply a Determinantal Point Process (DPP) to sample frames that are both query-relevant and non-redundant. These selected frames capture key moments across the video and form the backbone of the sampling set, providing a foundation for subsequent enrichment. In the second stage, \emph{Adaptive Contextual Enrichment}, we analyze the temporal distribution of the backbone frames to infer the query's requirements and adaptively allocate the remaining frame budget between Local and Global Context. The Local Context enriches the backbone with fine-grained temporal dynamics and short-range causal relations, whereas the Global Context connects temporally isolated evidence and provides a holistic view of the entire video to support global understanding. Through such a hierarchical two-stage process, HAS effectively addresses diverse query requirements. Incorporated into three leading MLLMs, it demonstrates consistently superior performance across the Video-MME, LongVideoBench, and MLVU benchmarks.


### arXiv 2611.34539 - EPPC-CARE: Staged Hierarchical Annotation of Communication Behaviors in Secure Patient-Provider Messages

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T15:21:01Z
- authors: Samah Fodeh, Sreeraj Ramachandran, Ganesh Puthiaraju, Sarah Schellhorn
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34539

Objectives: Patient portal messages provide invaluable information about communication dynamics between patients and healthcare providers, but extracting it manually is labor-intensive. We developed and evaluated EPPC-CARE, an automated framework for identifying communication behaviors together with the supporting text for each classification. Materials and Methods: From 867 messages, we obtained 5,516 reference standard annotations. Each annotation is a triplet of communication-related text (span), a broader communication category (Code), and a specific subcategory (Sub-code). We evaluated EPPC-CARE's identified triplets across six open-weight large language models (LLMs) against supervised fine-tuning and communication-based models such as EPPC-OASIS. We also compared EPPC-CARE with human expert annotations on 100 messages. Results: EPPC-CARE improved performance over EPPC-OASIS across all six models. EPPC-CARE's Triplet F1 improved by 2.90 percentage points on average, with a best score of 64.4%. On the human expert evaluation subset, EPPC-CARE and the human expert showed comparable agreement with the reference standard, with differences favoring EPPC-CARE. Discussion: These findings suggest that identifying triplet components (span, code, subcode) sequentially can improve performance across model families while preserving the supporting text behind each identification. EPPC-CARE links the communication code and subcode with the source text (span), supporting human verification of automated annotations in clinical informatics. EPPC-CARE may therefore make larger-scale studies of patient-provider communication more feasible and provide a foundation for human-in-the-loop annotation workflows. Conclusion: EPPC-CARE offers a more reliable and reviewable approach for automated characterization of communication behaviors in secure patient-provider messaging.


### arXiv 2611.34521 - PURA: Video Token Pruning via Unified Spatiotemporal Redundancy Assessment

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T15:08:53Z
- authors: Yuyao Sun, Hongli Yuan, Shuang Li, Tao Deng, Deqing Wang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34521

Video large language models incur substantial inference costs from long visual token sequences. Existing video token compression methods mitigate these costs by exploiting spatial redundancy within frames and temporal redundancy across frames. However, many approaches impose predefined processing orders or fixed allocation ratios for spatial and temporal compression, limiting their ability to adapt to the varying balance of spatiotemporal redundancy across videos. To address this limitation, we propose PURA, a training-free method that prunes visual tokens through unified spatiotemporal redundancy assessment. For each token, PURA constructs spatial and temporal reference candidates and selects the reference with the lowest representation error across the two dimensions. The representation error is combined with token importance to determine each token's removal cost. To avoid the time overhead of removing the lowest-cost token one by one, we introduce Coordinated Pruning, which iteratively groups mutually compatible low-cost removals into batches for simultaneous execution and updates the pruning state after each batch until the target budget is reached. Extensive experiments on three video large language models and five video understanding benchmarks demonstrate that PURA achieves a favorable trade-off between task performance and inference efficiency.


### arXiv 2611.34509 - Long-Horizon Planning with Latent World Models via Graph Anchors

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI, cs.CV
- published: 2026-11-25T14:58:31Z
- authors: Syed Nazmus Sakib, Abdul Monaf Chowdhury, Peizhen Li, Nafiul Haque, Chandak Chakma, Md Mehedi Hasan
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34509

Latent world models plan in a compact latent space by predicting the outcomes of actions. Their planners judge a plan by the latent distance between its predicted end state and the goal image. We show that this distance fails in two distinct ways. First, it loses directional signal a few steps from the goal, causing distant goals to fail. Second, it weighs the robot arm over the manipulated object, causing manipulation to fail at contact. We measure both failures directly on recorded data by asking whether a lower latent distance corresponds to true progress toward the goal. Across five environments, six visual representations (including raw pixels and V-JEPA 2), and nine Meta-World tasks, this ranking test cleanly separates the two failures, and the graph distance to a goal predicts where closed-loop planning breaks down. Both failures share a single remedy: replacing the distant goal with a nearby target that lies on a recorded trajectory. We realize this principle with Graph Anchors, a training-free planner for frozen latent world models. It builds a sparse graph once over the model's own training frames and aims each planning step at a node a few moves ahead along the shortest path to the goal, keeping paths on recorded transitions wherever contact matters. On goals up to 400 steps away, Graph Anchors raise LeWM's success from 13% to 85% on PointMaze-Medium, from 8% to 87% on PointMaze-Large, and from 5% to 71% on Push-T, where stronger search toward the goal fails and where prior training-free planners break down under missing step horizons, subsampling shortcuts, or fragmented contact.


### arXiv 2611.34508 - Angular Interpolation in Sparse-View Low-Dose CT Reconstruction: Placement Effects and Data-Consistency Step-Size Scaling

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T14:57:39Z
- authors: Yitian Tang, Yuncheng Zhang, Hualiang Wang, Zuozhu Liu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34508

Dual-domain networks combine projection and image processing for sparse-view low-dose computed tomography (CT). We quantify the contribution of angular interpolation before learning and distinguish its roles in sinogram initialisation and the image residual base. On synthetic parallel-beam data, parameter-free interpolation reduces noise-free missing-view mean absolute error by 99.7% relative to zero filling at 60 views. Full-view filtered back-projection (FBP) of the jointly learned completed sinogram adds 1.27 dB of peak signal-to-noise ratio (PSNR) over interpolated FBP. In a data-consistency dual-domain network (DC-DuNet), a 2x2 configuration ablation controls branch width, depth and training schedule while varying these placements and their associated input channels. After 20 epochs, the PSNR contrasts are +1.98 dB for sinogram initialisation and -2.16 dB for the interpolated image base; the image-base ranking also reverses during training. The sinogram-initialised variant exceeds an image-only U-Net by 0.96 dB at 60 views and 10% dose, and by 1.28 dB on independently generated synthetic data. The U-Net leads at higher view counts and with a shared interpolated image base. Without retraining, the variant retains a 0.12 dB advantage on held-out CHAOS abdominal CT under simulated degradation; the image-base contrast rounds to zero. The implemented projection/backprojection pair has empirical dominant gain of approximately 0.85NK, where N is image-grid width and K acquired view count. These results distinguish interpolation placement from network architecture and support NK normalisation for step calibration.


### arXiv 2611.34474 - Noise threshold for learning with quantum memory

- categories: quant-ph, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T14:40:44Z
- authors: Miaojiang Chen, Wenjing Xiao, Haiqiang Chen, Jiarong Liang, Anfeng Liu, Zhenrong Zhang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34474

A quantum memory can reduce exponentially the number of experiments needed to learn a physical process, but real memories are noisy. Here we show that, for Pauli memory noise with eigenvalues $λ_X,λ_Y,λ_Z$, the advantage is governed by $λ_X^2+λ_Y^2+λ_Z^2$. We prove that no protocol with an entangled probe gains an exponential advantage when this sum is at most one, reduce the general case to the additivity of a Rényi-2 coherent information, and find coded memories that retain a growing advantage within 1% of this threshold. On a superconducting processor, nine simultaneously operated Bell pairs show an advantage that grows or shrinks exponentially with system size, reversing where a parameter-free model predicts. A three-qubit repetition code turns a shrinking advantage into a growing one with all processor and readout errors included, and under dephasing its memory performs better than Bell sampling in any memory frame on a perfect processor.


### arXiv 2611.34466 - EgoUseBench: Benchmarking Computer-Use Agents under Evolving First-Person Contexts

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T14:36:38Z
- authors: Yurun Chen, Biao Yi, Qihang Yu, Keting Yin, Shengyu Zhang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34466

AI wearables, such as smart glasses, extend the context available to agents beyond digital environments to users' physical surroundings. With these devices, computer tasks can originate from users' ongoing activities rather than solely from explicit instructions. Through first-person observations, agents can identify objects and activities relevant to user requests and ground digital tasks in the surrounding context. We call this interaction paradigm Ego Use. However, first-person observations are limited by the wearer's viewpoint and may provide incomplete or ambiguous evidence of ongoing activities. As new observations arrive, agents may need to revise their understanding of the physical context and user intent, and reassess whether prior digital actions remain valid. Whether current agents can reliably handle these changes remains insufficiently evaluated. We introduce EgoUseBench, a benchmark for evaluating Ego Use across three interaction settings with different levels of first-person context. Computer-Use relies on explicit instructions, Offline Ego Use provides prerecorded first-person context before execution, and Online Ego Use provides continuous observations as user intent evolves. Based on over 74 hours of real-world first-person recordings, we construct 693 tasks across the three interaction settings. To capture task evolution, we track first-person observations, user requirements, and website state separately. We further group tasks by how information changes during execution, resulting in three types: Graft (revised user requirements), Refresh (new first-person observations), and Clarify (agent-initiated information seeking). We evaluate advanced models and find that average success rates in Online Ego Use are 26.3% lower than in Computer-Use. This result reveals the difficulty of keeping task execution aligned with evolving first-person context and user intent.


### arXiv 2611.34457 - ProxyGenBench: Benchmarking Proxy-Guided Video Generation

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T14:31:23Z
- authors: Zhenchen Tang, Haolin Lu, Songlin Yang, Xiaotong Zhao, Ran Zhang, Bo Peng
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34457

Recent video generators produce realistic videos, yet controlling how scenes unfold remains challenging. Structural proxies, such as animated 3D blockouts, guide generation by specifying scene layout, motion, camera behavior, and event order. However, these coarse inputs leave details unspecified and may contain artifacts that should not be reproduced. Successful proxy-guided generation must therefore Preserve intended structure, Complete missing content and correct proxy artifacts, and Bind text, image, and audio conditions to the right entities and events. We introduce ProxyGenBench to study these interacting requirements, with 312 evaluation cases from 60 scenes across three scene tasks and controlled variations in proxy fidelity, text detail, and multimodal inputs. We evaluate five video generators using verified, case-specific checklists. Experiments show that proxies improve spatiotemporal control, but greater proxy detail can coincide with poorer completion, while strong structural adherence does not ensure correct binding.


### arXiv 2611.34440 - vSFT: Learning to Verbally Tune Agents at Test Time

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-25T14:22:22Z
- authors: Qishuai Wen, Rui Chen, Haiwen Li, Zhiyuan Huang, Qiwen Gu, Bingjie Gao
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34440

Large language model agents are increasingly improved via agentic reinforcement learning, which can be on-policy but suffers from expensive rollouts and sparse rewards, limiting its application at test time. In this paper, we present a verbally Supervised Fine-Tuning (vSFT) framework that enables the conjunction of dense feedback in natural language and weight updates at test time. Specifically, our vSFT forms a closed loop between an agent and a supervisor via a hypernetwork, in which the supervisor generates verbal supervision from the agent's failed trajectories, and the hypernetwork converts that supervision into low-rank weight updates to tune the agent. We validate that this test-time in-parameter evolving process outperforms both its in-context counterparts and prevalent reinforcement learning methods. In particular, our vSFT using Qwen3-4B outperforms GRPO and Reflexion by 8.7% and 13.9% in success rate on ALFWorld, and by 4.8% and 18.2% on WebShop, respectively. We believe that learning how to adapt a model, rather than an adapted model, offers a promising path towards more capable agents that improve themselves.


### arXiv 2611.34435 - Most of the Way There: Turning a Strong Video Prior into an Action-Conditioned World Model

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T14:19:27Z
- authors: Zanyi Wang, Xia Chen, Liu Liu, Xianglong Guo, Dengyang Jiang, Haodong Li
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34435

Modern video generators excel at producing visually plausible futures guided by text and image prompts. Yet their emphasis on semantic plausibility does not guarantee fidelity to the physical consequences of specific actions. Action-conditioned world models (ACWMs), in contrast, aim to simulate how the world responds to robot actions, enabling scalable policy evaluation and improvement without costly real-world rollouts. The challenge is not merely to generate a plausible future, but to predict what follows from a specific physical action. As video priors grow stronger, we ask how little adaptation is truly needed to bridge this gap. We introduce Dianjing, a minimal recipe that preserves and unlocks the capabilities of strong video priors through unified physical action conditioning, training concentrated at the high noise levels where motion and layout, and thus the consequences of actions, are decided, and native history conditioning. Built upon MiniMax H3, a 33B video foundation model, Dianjing freezes the pretrained backbone and trains only 0.34% of its parameters using approximately 65 hours of robot video across two embodiments, without additional robot-specific pretraining or camera calibration. Controlled ablations show that where training is allocated across noise levels matters more than how actions enter the network. On WorldOracle, which evaluates predictions against paired real-world executions, Dianjing achieves the best scores on four of the five evaluation tracks and ranks second on AgiBot-Long. Our findings suggest that strong video priors already bring us most of the way to world modeling; lightweight physical action grounding can unlock their potential for real-world prediction.


### arXiv 2611.34432 - Evaluating Aviation Safety Knowledge Extraction with SmolLM2: From Zero-Shot to Retrieval-Grounded Prompting

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T14:18:25Z
- authors: Aziida Nanyonga, Mustafa Tahsin Yilmaz, Amir Hussain, Ugur Turhan, Graham Wild
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34432

Aviation accident narratives contain safety knowledge that is difficult to capture using structured occurrence fields alone. This study evaluates prompting-based knowledge extraction across four dimensions: concept accuracy, verbatim evidence grounding, structural compliance, and directed relation extraction. SmolLM2-1.7B-Instruct was evaluated under zero-shot, one-shot, fixed few-shot, and retrieval-grounded prompting using 300 National Transportation Safety Board narratives from 2000-2020, containing 3,763 reference concepts and 467 directed relations. Evaluation combined structural checks, verbatim evidence matching, expert-reviewed semantic matching, and paired bootstrap confidence intervals. No prompting strategy performed best across all dimensions. Retrieval-grounded prompting achieved the highest concept micro-F1 of 32.27%, although its advantage over fixed few-shot prompting was uncertain (95% CI: -0.92 to 2.40 percentage points). One-shot prompting achieved the highest relation F1 of 6.95%, identifying directed relation extraction as the principal SmolLM2 bottleneck. Compared with fixed few-shot prompting, retrieval improved verbatim grounding (59.45% vs. 54.02%) but reduced complete structural compliance (33.3% vs. 42.7%). Verbatim matching establishes source presence rather than evidential relevance. An exploratory comparison using 35 benchmark narratives showed that Qwen3-8B achieved higher concept micro-F1 across all prompting conditions and higher relation F1 under demonstration-based prompting, reaching 51.37% and 34.62%, respectively, under one-shot prompting. The small subset limits generalization. These findings support evaluating semantic correctness, source traceability, structural reliability, and directed relation accuracy separately.


### arXiv 2611.34430 - One Representation to Rule Them All -- Open-Vocabulary Change Detection from Text or Visual Examples

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-25T14:16:57Z
- authors: Blaž Rolih, Matic Fučka, Filip Wolf, Luka Čehovin Zajc
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34430

Open-vocabulary change detection (OVCD) aims to localise user-specified changes without retraining for predefined categories. Existing approaches typically rely on textual descriptions and combine separate models for region proposal, semantic recognition, and temporal comparison. This limits how target concepts can be expressed and makes detection dependent on several intermediate predictions. We introduce OVTSID3, a training-free framework that unifies region grouping, concept identification, and temporal comparison in the representation space of DINOv3. OVTSID3 supports two complementary modes of concept specification: zero-shot textual queries or one-shot visual prompting through a masked reference pair. OVTSID3 outperforms all prior training-free OVCD methods on two building and two natural-disaster benchmarks, raising the average F1 by 8.9 points. OVTSID3 also supports semantic change detection and achieves state-of-the-art on SECOND. These results demonstrate the flexibility of a unified representation for text- or example-guided change detection without task-specific model training.


### arXiv 2611.34428 - Calibration of Neuromusculoskeletal Models Using Differentiable Physics: Recommendations for Improving Model Accuracy

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T14:15:32Z
- authors: Philip Sanderink, Yingfan Zhou, Shuzhen Luo, Cheng Fang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34428

Differentiable physics has shown potential to usher in a new paradigm in human neuromusculoskeletal (NMS) model calibration. Early results from pioneering works have demonstrated that auto-differentiable NMS models can greatly reduce NMS model calibration time while achieving comparable accuracy compared to traditional calibration methods. The efficient gradient-based optimizers have enabled calibration of increasingly sophisticated human models with a large number of parameters, which seek to narrow the gap between simulation and reality. Several modules have been evaluated separately in different studies, but have never been directly compared within the same study. In this work, these modules are compared on the same differentiable upper limb model with a 3-DoF shoulder, a 2-DoF elbow, and 11 superficial and 5 deep muscles in the calibration to find an optimal pipeline for improving model accuracy. The considered modules include muscle synergy, learnable bone parameters, optimizable geometry of wrap objects, and constraint torque of the differentiable arm model. To evaluate the effect and interplay between the modules on calibration accuracy, one or more modules were used in each of 16 calibration configurations, which were compared in terms of the error between two joint torques calculated by the forward and inverse dynamics with the calibrated model on 9 distinctive testing movements across 4 subjects. Results showed that torque loss was reduced by 23.6% on average using muscle synergy extrapolation to estimate deep muscle activities, and by 10.9% on average through bone dynamic parameter optimization, which are both recommended to be incorporated in the calibration.


### arXiv 2611.34416 - CHASE: Curiosity-Guided Skill Acquisition for Embodied Task Execution

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T14:08:37Z
- authors: Zixuan Wang, Wei Zhang, Chenlei Zhu, Naiyu Chen, Xihua Wang, Jie Chen
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34416

Robots executing tasks in partially observed environments must resolve uncertainty while operating with incomplete skills. Inspired by human curiosity, we investigate how robots can formulate task-relevant hypotheses, seek evidence, and acquire missing skills during task execution. We propose CHASE, a Curiosity-guided Harness for Acquiring Skills from Evidence, which operationalizes curiosity as selecting investigations that resolve task uncertainty and expand executable capabilities. Four modules connect dynamic scene understanding, hypothesis-driven planning, on-demand skill acquisition, and verified execution. The planner balances information value, skill reuse, and acquisition/execution cost. Missing skills trigger bounded exploration, learning from verified segments, and held-out admission during acquisition; separate checks verify physical effects and acquired evidence. Newly admitted skills expand the executable investigation set, closing the loop between resolving uncertainty and acquiring capabilities. Simulation and real-robot experiments evaluate investigation, skill acquisition, and task execution. CHASE achieves 50-80% task success under combined information and capability gaps, with 70-90% evidence resolution.


### arXiv 2611.34414 - SemProxy: Semantic 3D Proxies for Controllable Agentic 3D Modeling

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T14:08:05Z
- authors: Shuyuan Zhang, Changjian Li
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34414

Coding agents are increasingly capable of generating 3D models, yet enabling users to effectively control the modeling process remains challenging. Existing workflows rely primarily on text prompts and reference images, which often struggle to convey precise spatial intentions and require iterative corrections. We introduce SemProxy, a lightweight intermediate representation that bridges intuitive human interaction and agentic 3D modeling. Instantiated as labeled 3D bounding boxes, semantic 3D proxies allow users to directly specify object parts and spatial arrangements while leaving geometric details to coding agents. These proxies can be serialized into structured text and rendered as images, making them directly accessible to existing coding agents without additional training or modifications. We further develop ProxyStudio, a user interface that integrates text, image references, and interactive 3D proxy editing into agentic modeling workflows. We demonstrate the versatility of SemProxy across object- and scene-level modeling tasks with different coding agents, and evaluate its effectiveness in supporting controllable human-agent collaborative 3D modeling through a user study.


### arXiv 2611.34407 - Agentic Waterfall: An Intent-Driven Information Discovery Framework for Reclaiming User Control in the Attention Economy

- categories: cs.AI, cs.IR
- published: 2026-11-25T14:04:25Z
- authors: Yige Luo, Shining Zhang, Jikun Liu, Hongjiao Qiang, Ran Guan
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34407

We formalize Agentic Waterfall Feed, a new problem of personalized information discovery in which users actively direct recommendations and the system adapts to evolving needs inferred from both explicit requests and browsing behavior. We address this problem with the Agentic Waterfall framework, whose language-model controller maintains persistent user state and coordinates content retrieval, ranking, and state updates. To evaluate systems on this task, we contribute the Agentic Waterfall Dataset and a replay-based evaluation protocol. The dataset combines real content metadata with simulated interaction histories. The protocol compares systems under shared histories using relevance scores and blind judgments of how well recommendations satisfy current user goals and constraints. Agentic Waterfall remains competitive with strong baselines on conventional recommendation tasks across five public benchmarks. On the Agentic Waterfall Dataset, it outperforms the strongest baseline in both evaluations, with relative improvements of up to 16.1%.


### arXiv 2611.34400 - EvoLink-Bench: A Benchmark for Evaluating Self-Evolving Agents in Long-Horizon Interdependent Task Streams

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T14:00:22Z
- authors: Wei Chen, Xiaodong Lu, Weihao Shen, Gongxun Li, Xingyu Guo, Qihang Sun
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34400

Large language model (LLM) agents are evolving from passive chatbots into autonomous systems that operate continuously in dynamic environments. Such agents need not only solve current tasks but also learn from past interactions to improve future performance. However, existing streaming benchmarks often rely on short task sequences or largely independent tasks, providing limited evidence of whether agents can effectively reuse experience when later tasks depend on earlier outcomes. To address this gap, we introduce \textbf{EvoLink-Bench}, a benchmark for evaluating self-evolving agents in long-horizon, interdependent task streams. It covers daily office work, multi-turn dialogue, and AutoResearch-style workflows, with persistent workspaces and dependencies across tasks. The benchmark includes two complementary levels: \textbf{L1}, which evaluates the acquisition and revision of language-expressible knowledge, and \textbf{L2}, which assesses the development of task-level capabilities through execution and feedback. Its evaluation further distinguishes within-task recovery, persistent-state updates, and experience reuse in later tasks. We evaluate five agent frameworks across three backbone configurations under three persistence conditions, covering 19,635 scored task slots from selected evaluation cohorts. Results show that no framework consistently performs best across both levels, retries recover only a limited proportion of failed tasks, and persistent-state updates do not necessarily improve subsequent performance. Furthermore, the effects of evolution differ between knowledge- and capability-level tasks, suggesting that these two forms of evolution require different learning mechanisms. Performance gains also vary across domains and task streams, with improvements on some tasks not necessarily extending to others.


### arXiv 2611.34398 - When Skills Fail to Compose: Compatibility and Retention in Language Models

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T13:59:48Z
- authors: Yan Lin, Zhongliang Guo, Jingyu Sun, Qiyan Zhao, Ruihong Zeng, Ying Chen
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34398

Skill acquisition alone does not establish a reusable skill library: task proficiency does not specify a compositional interface. Motivated by agents that acquire and refine skills, we study independently learned soft-prompt skills on frozen language models, with skill identities and execution order supplied. Across four digit-task bases and three record-editing models, mean composition accuracy in one call is at most 7.6%, compared with 88.2% to 100% over two calls that add computation and an explicit intermediate output. Neutral-insertion training improves robustness but leaves substantial interference, which block length and per-token norm alone cannot explain on two of four digit bases. Blocks of the same operation need not be interchangeable: after training next to frozen learned partners, a post hoc test on two Qwen bases shows that replacing those partners with independently learned blocks of the same operations lowers median composition accuracy from 98.0% and 95.3% to 12.7% and 0.5%, although every replacement scores at least 90% alone. Repair can improve a composition while impairing the original skill, and rehearsal improves retention while transfer remains limited on Qwen2.5-0.5B. Internal interventions constrain explanations without identifying a shared failure mechanism. These findings motivate evaluating compatibility, retention and transfer alongside acquisition when building skill libraries for self-evolving agents. Code is available at https://github.com/YanLin-Quinne/SkillCompatibility.


### arXiv 2611.34366 - ConTra: A Training Framework for Effective Contextual Retrieval via Prefix Generation

- categories: cs.IR, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T13:30:46Z
- authors: Jiajun Hou, Jingbang Chen
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34366

Data management systems organize large text collections for tasks such as finding relevant studies and extracting contract clauses. Systems make long documents searchable by splitting them into independently indexed chunks. Chunking can separate text from its identifying details. The indexing challenge is to keep each chunk independently searchable while preserving the context needed to find and rank it. Existing methods either encode that context in chunk vectors or attach it as a text prefix. Context encoded only in vectors is unavailable to text rerankers. Prompted prefixes serve both vector search and reranking, but are costly to generate at indexing time and can underperform contextual encoders. We propose ConTra (Context That Travels), a reinforcement learning framework for training lightweight prefix generators. Its reward combines feedback from frozen embedding and reranking models with factual verification and length control. ConTra generates one prefix per chunk during index construction and stores the augmented text and its embedding for reuse across queries. To evaluate contextual retrieval across workloads and under interference, we introduce RealContextBench, a benchmark with five application collections and three controlled conditions that isolate the effect of distracting text. On ConTEB and RealContextBench, ConTra-2B outperforms the prompted 9B baseline using Anthropic's method and the PPLX-4B contextual encoder, with a margin of up to 3.15 points over their best overall scores, while indexing short documents 7.74 times and 2.15 times as fast as those baselines, respectively. Post-trained only on ConTEB, it leads the standalone methods on RealContextBench after reranking and is less sensitive than the prompted 2B baseline to random and topically similar distractors. Code, datasets, and trained models are available at https://github.com/SStan1/ConTra.


### arXiv 2611.34351 - Inverse Reinforcement Learning with State-Dependent Foresight for Human Decision-Making

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T13:20:03Z
- authors: Jinil Kim, Jinwoo Jeong, Hyeongjun Yun, Min-hwan Oh, Woo-Young Ahn
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34351

Computational modeling of behavior represents individuals with a model fitted to their observed behavior and characterizes them through its parameters. Naturalistic tasks incorporate the complexity of real environments and yield high-dimensional behavioral trajectories. Inverse reinforcement learning (IRL) infers the latent reward function underlying these trajectories. Standard IRL, however, assumes effectively infinite-horizon planning, whereas human foresight is both bounded and state-dependent. Here we introduce SC-AIRL (State-Conditional Adversarial IRL), which builds these two cognitive properties into the planner. Per-depth policies share one reward but differ in how far ahead they sum it explicitly. A state-conditional router mixes them according to the current state. Across held-out episodes in pedestrian crossing and highway overtaking tasks, SC-AIRL improves imitation over Standard AIRL, raising top-1 accuracy from 0.549 to 0.647 and from 0.629 to 0.718, respectively, while more closely reproducing participant behavior in rollouts. Inferred planning depth correlates with self-reported anxiety and motor impulsivity. SC-AIRL thus reproduces human behavior more closely than Standard AIRL while providing an interpretable, model-derived measure of state-dependent foresight.


### arXiv 2611.34331 - Puzzling-Bench: Evaluating Language Models on 23,777 Human-Written Puzzles with Community-Verified Answers

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-25T13:10:49Z
- authors: Abhinav Sharma, Li Li, Chien Van Nguyen, Sai Karthik Navuluru, Bo Ni, Xiangbo Gao
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34331

We introduce Puzzling-Bench, a benchmark of 23,777 puzzles with community-verified answers drawn from the Puzzling Stack Exchange archive (2014-2026): riddles, ciphers, logic grids, lateral-thinking problems, wordplay and recreational mathematics, a third of them with images. Every puzzle carries the answer accepted by its author, a short reference answer taken from it (LLM-extracted for the evaluation sets and checked on a 50-puzzle human review), the author's own hints, community tags and a creation date, so that the benchmark supports hint ablations, category analysis and a rolling post-cutoff slice (254 puzzles posted after the training cutoff of six of the nine models we evaluate, including the best one) as a contamination check. We evaluate nine frontier and open models with a two-tier scorer (normalized exact match, then an LLM judge) and validate the judge with three judges from two model families: they agree on 95-97% of verdicts (kappa 0.82-0.96), move no model by more than 1.6 points, and show no own-family preference. The best model, GPT-6.1, reaches 74.8% on a shared 255-puzzle set; a hard core of 17 puzzles defeats all nine models; model failures are strongly correlated, and the second-best model contributes a single puzzle to a greedy ensemble, yet an oracle over all nine reaches 91%. On a 100-puzzle multimodal pilot, withholding the question's image costs the two best models 24 and 35 points. We release the dataset, the scoring pipeline and all per-row provenance.


### arXiv 2611.34328 - LUDIUS: A Benchmark for Embodied Social Intelligence in Everyday Human-Robot Interactions

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T13:09:34Z
- authors: Chaewon Kim, Sangheon Lee, Seyeon Choi, Kangwook Lee, Yea Seul Kim
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34328

Social intelligence in a robot requires interpreting a situation from the perspectives of the people involved and using that understanding to decide when and how to act. We introduce LUDIUS, a benchmark for evaluating how vision-language models exercise this judgment through a robot's verbal responses and actions, organized around 15 social intelligence skills drawn from social psychology, sociology, and cognitive science. LUDIUS comprises six editions, each a collection of episodes spanning one simulated week. Three editions each cover all 15 skills in a different household. The other three focus on interactions involving multiple people, safety, and context-specific norms, respectively. We evaluate frontier models and a smaller model finetuned on social-interaction data we generated, using a common evaluation harness. The harness supplies each model with the robot's camera views and residents' reactions, retrieves relevant interaction history, and converts the model's tool calls into robot verbal responses and actions. Models tend to perform worse when they must reason about what a resident saw or heard earlier, reconcile competing interests, or fulfill a request within safety constraints and household rules. Beyond the home setting, LUDIUS's design pipeline offers a way to construct social intelligence benchmarks by translating social intelligence skills into context-specific judgments and observable robot behavior.


### arXiv 2611.34327 - MeterRouter: Budget-Aware Dynamic Routing for Multi-Agent Systems

- categories: cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-25T13:09:13Z
- authors: Yue Xu, Leikun Yin, Ruiming Min, Yufeng Lai, Justin Andrew Johnson, Sitao Luan
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34327

Multi-agent systems (MAS) built on large language models (LLMs) outperform single-agent systems, but inter-agent communication incurs substantial token overhead. While assigning a large LLM to every agent is common, it results in inefficient resource utilization, leaving the system far from the Pareto frontier. The cost of answering a query is set jointly by how many agents are recruited, the role and LLM assigned to each, and communication topology among them. Most existing routers treat cost only implicitly rather than as an explicit optimization constraint. A fixed accuracy-cost preference also cannot express how much a caller is willing to pay for a particular query. We introduce MeterRouter, a budget-aware dynamic router that incrementally synthesizes a collaboration graph by selecting each agent's role, LLM, incoming connections, and termination conditioned on the query and a per-query monetary budget. We initialize the router by imitating successful execution graphs, then jointly refine its decisions with a variant of Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) whose reward favors correct answers and penalizes spending and budget overruns. Across five benchmarks and a three-tier LLM pool, MeterRouter is (1) accurate, achieving performance comparable to the strongest MAS; (2) economical, reaching that accuracy at 82% lower cost than the strongest MAS router and extending the accuracy-cost frontier beyond the ceiling of single-agent systems; and (3) controllable, adapting its spending to the specified budget while rarely exceeding it.


### arXiv 2611.34322 - The cost of being seen as AI in group bargaining

- categories: cs.CY, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T13:07:08Z
- authors: Tahmineh Godazgar, Nico Mutzner, Taha Yasseri
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34322

As artificial intelligence (AI) becomes embedded in collective decision-making, people's perceptions of AI may shape who reaches agreement and what they receive. We studied this in a repeated three-player majoritarian bargaining game with 2,234 participants. Players saw each opponent identified as Human or AI. The instructions described AI as acting either independently or on behalf of a human. Human participants made every decision. In mixed groups, players presented as AI received fewer points in opening offers, were included in fewer final agreements and earned about 18% less per round than those presented as Human. Smaller payoffs in agreements that included them accounted for most of the observed gap. Further analysis found a payoff gap even when all three players agreed. Social perceptions of AI may therefore shape who benefits from collective decisions, with potential costs for people represented by AI.


### arXiv 2611.34298 - RAP: Radial-Angular Random Projection for Hyperbolic Representations

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T12:50:23Z
- authors: Jiahong Liu, Wenhao Yu, Yifan Li, Yanyu Chen, Rex Ying, Menglin Yang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34298

Random projection is a basic compression primitive for Euclidean representation learning, with the Johnson--Lindenstrauss lemma giving a target dimension $k=O(\varepsilon^{-2}\log n)$ independent of the ambient dimension. As hyperbolic representations gain increasing attention in modern Transformers, vision-language models, and large language models, the same need for oblivious dimensionality reduction arises, but no analogous primitive yet exists for hyperbolic representations. We study this gap. Two natural attempts fail in dual ways: a tangent-space scheme gives a clean local guarantee but is chart-bound and pays a curvature penalty, while a fully hyperbolic projection in the hyperboloid model is geometrically native but mixes radial and angular error before feeding them into the conditioning of $\operatorname{arcosh}$. We propose radial-angular random projection (RAP), which preserves the radial coordinate exactly and applies a Euclidean random projection only to the angular direction, lifting the result back to the target hyperboloid. RAP is hyperbolic-to-hyperbolic by construction, costs one Gaussian matrix-vector product, and reduces hyperbolic compression to a normalized angular JL question on $\mathbb{S}^{d-1}$. We prove a finite-set RAP guarantee with explicit $\sinh(\sqrt{c}\,r_x)\sinh(\sqrt{c}\,r_y)$ conditioning, complemented by a local tangent-space JL theorem and an arcosh-conditioning lemma for the fully hyperbolic comparator. Across an origin-centered broad-radius point cloud, a balanced tree, a clustered Gaussian, and the WordNet noun hierarchy, RAP attains the lowest mean relative distortion in every $(n,k)$ cell, reducing distortion over the strongest hyperbolic baseline by approximately $48\%$--$49\%$ on broad-radius data. RAP also scales to million-point datasets and improves retrieval on vision--language representations over hyperbolic projection baselines.


### arXiv 2611.34292 - WebVibe: Multimodal Interactive Website Generation via Reverse Synthesis and Visual Self-Distillation

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T12:46:28Z
- authors: Tianxiang Jiang, Sheng Xia, Yilin Long, Kanghui Tian, Linquan Wu, Yongheng Zhang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34292

Although multimodal large language models translate single screenshots into static HTML, generating interactive functional websites from design specifications still struggles with multi-page routing and stateful widget transitions. Examining existing training pipelines reveals two bottlenecks: (1) in forward data synthesis (X -> Y), writing the specification X before code execution leaves unimplemented or altered requirements in X as supervision noise; and (2) in reinforcement learning, scalar rewards discard the rich state transitions observed during replay. Based on these findings, we introduce WebVibe and Interactive-SDPO to train InteractiveCoder. WebVibe reverses synthesis into Y -> X by verifying cross-page and in-page interactions in the browser before deriving multimodal inputs from replay-confirmed states to build the 31,542-site WebVibe-32K corpus with reusable execution contracts. Interactive-SDPO then conditions a weight-sharing self-teacher on the replay trace and a 2x2 state-transition montage to provide token-level supervision at zero inference-time overhead. On the public Vision2Web L2, one-shot InteractiveCoder-9B nearly 3x its base model's functional score (20.2 to 55.2 FS) and matches a 1T-parameter coding agent in a single pass with 111x fewer total parameters and 78x fewer tokens. On our WebVibeBench, InteractiveCoder-4B and 9B further outperform all evaluated baselines by over 23 points.


### arXiv 2611.34290 - StateWeaver: Multi-Agent Coordination of Story-State Transitions for Long-Horizon Video Generation

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T12:43:37Z
- authors: Modi Jin, Xuying Zhang, Yijing Guo, Haoran Li, Yanwen Ma, Jing Wang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34290

Modern video foundation models generate high-quality short clips, but preserving long-video consistency and correctly depicting event-driven state changes remain challenging. Existing agentic workflows face these challenges because continual planning and repair accumulate context, while reference updates remain limited. We introduce StateWeaver, which combines Active States and Hierarchical Collaboration to connect review-driven visual reference updates with cross-shot state inheritance. Active States translate state discrepancies identified during review into concrete visual asset requirements that guide reference construction, validation, and regeneration, preserving identity and already-correct content while repairing target attributes. Confirmed generation results are linked to the corresponding asset states, story time, and reference versions for verification and reuse by subsequent shots. Hierarchical Collaboration isolates execution contexts by task, clip, and repair round, and uses hierarchical memory to organize this process across multiple sets. Across multiple benchmarks, StateWeaver outperforms existing methods and produces long videos with sustained cross-shot consistency under predefined and autonomously planned storyboards.


### arXiv 2611.34278 - Latent-in-the-Middle: On the Security of Cross-Model Latent Communication in Multi-Agent Systems

- categories: cs.CR, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T12:35:33Z
- authors: Manit Baser, Vyoman Jain, Rachel Yeo Hui Min, Xianglin Yang, Meenatchi Sundaram Muthu Selva Annamalai, Dinil Mon Divakaran
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34278

As large language models increasingly collaborate in heterogeneous multi-agent systems, latent communication has emerged as an efficient alternative to exchanging natural-language messages. By sharing internal representations, agents can avoid repeatedly processing the same context, reducing communication overhead and prefill computation. One such mechanism is cross-model Key-Value (KV) cache transfer, which uses a learned mapper to translate a source model's KV cache into one a target model can use. However, this handoff means the target model consumes context it did not encode itself, thus calling into question whether its safety alignment still holds. In this work, we investigate the security implications of latent handoffs by introducing Chasm, a framework characterizing cross-model handoff attacks across three new attack surfaces: the source model, mapper, and handoff mechanism itself. Our experiments reveal that latent handoffs substantially undermine safety alignment and prompt-injection defenses. Notably, a target model that follows only 0.4% of benchmark prompt injections natively, follows up to 44.2% when transferred through a mapper from a source model. On the other hand, for jailbreak attacks, transfer alone raises attack success by up to 24.6 pp within a model family and zero-shot attacks by up to 69.2 pp, whereas white-box optimization through the mapper boosts the attack success rate to 92.5%, against 37.5% for ordinary GCG on the same target. To address these vulnerabilities, we propose a lightweight target-side cache guard that corrects transferred caches to restore the target model's refusal, considerably mitigating these attacks.


### arXiv 2611.34275 - LatentCP-DPO: Conformal Pessimism for DPO with Finite-Sample Guarantees

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T12:34:48Z
- authors: Hongwei Xu, Jingyi Liu, Vincent Y. F. Tan, Zai Yang, Yulian Wu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34275

Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) learns a response policy from human preference data, but with a limited dataset it can overfit to noisy or weakly informative preference labels. We introduce LatentCP-DPO, a framework that combines conformal prediction with pessimistic preference optimization for Bradley-Terry (BT) pairwise feedback and Plackett-Luce (PL) ranking feedback. Building on LatentCP's construction of uncertainty sets for unobserved model parameters, we address how to incorporate this uncertainty into preference optimization and characterize its effect on policy performance. Under exchangeability and a correctly specified preference model, we construct uncertainty sets for latent relative rewards with finite-sample marginal coverage from conformal prediction sets for observable preferences. We then learn a response policy by maximizing the worst-case expected preference log-likelihood over these uncertainty sets, using an independent collection of prompts and candidate responses without preference labels. Under suitable assumptions, we establish finite-sample upper bounds on regularized policy suboptimality that separate policy-class approximation error, conformal uncertainty, and statistical estimation error. For BT feedback, we further establish a worst-case lower bound for LatentCP-DPO that matches the upper bound's reciprocal dependence on the number of unlabeled optimization examples. The lower bound also shows that the uncertainty sets can induce a bias that persists as the optimization sample grows. Together, these results characterize both the statistical accuracy and potential limitations of preference optimization using conformal uncertainty sets. Experiments support the benefits of the proposed framework in finite preference datasets.


### arXiv 2611.34270 - LLM-coupled interpretable tool wear monitoring with multi-evidence reasoning in CNC machining

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T12:32:30Z
- authors: Yuntong Chen, Yingqi Li, Qiwen Shen, Guobin Zhao, Xitian Tian, Lijiang Huang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34270

Interpretable tool wear assessment in CNC machining is challenging. Existing diagnostic models have high accuracy but provide limited guidance for tool replacement. This study proposes a two layer framework combining deep learning wear classification and LLM assisted reasoning. The first layer uses PCA Loading feature selection and optimized GRU to recognize three wear states, achieving 98.38 percent macro F1 score on the PHM2010 dataset. The second layer uses LLMs to generate natural language reports and replacement suggestions from classification results and feature trends. Engineer evaluation shows the reports outperform rule templates. Cross dataset tests on NASA milling data support the generalizability of this framework.


### arXiv 2611.34254 - Crucible: Evaluating LLM Agents in Executable Chemistry Research Environments

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T12:18:29Z
- authors: Yinjun Liu, Xinye Li, Ruisen Peng, Lang Gao, Zixiang Xu, Zirui Song
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34254

Large language models (LLMs) perform well on chemistry knowledge benchmarks, which mainly assess question answering and isolated tasks. Chemical research, however, requires using specialized tools and interpreting their outputs to obtain chemically valid results. To address this gap, we introduce Crucible Bench to evaluate this ability across all four stages of the design-make-test-analyze (DMTA) cycle. The benchmark contains 1,078 tasks in 12 task families curated from 18 published datasets, covering molecular design, synthesis planning, experimental execution, closed-loop optimization, simulation, and analytical characterization. Agents access 75 domain-specific tools through the Model Context Protocol in seven isolated environments. Sandboxed graders verify the resulting synthesis routes, experimental protocols, and other outputs. Laboratory tasks use robotic simulators or previously measured experimental data. We evaluate 14 LLMs as agents and find that the best achieves an overall score of 0.545, with synthesis planning and closed-loop optimization emerging as the hardest domains. Overall performance does not predict optimization performance well: the model ranked twelfth overall achieves the highest score in closed-loop optimization. Error analysis also shows that agents often submit chemically invalid results despite running their tools successfully. Stronger models benefit more from tool access, but reliable use of chemical software remains a challenge that Crucible Bench can track as models improve.


### arXiv 2611.34250 - MedSkill-E$^{2}$: Evaluating and Evolving Skills for Medical Agents

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-25T12:13:27Z
- authors: Jingjing Hua, Shaocong Wei, Jianghan Shen, Junzhi Ning, Min Cen, Cheng Tang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34250

Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly use skills to access domain knowledge and complete real-world tasks. We observe that even when a skill specifies the rules and procedures needed for a task, agents may fail to apply them effectively during autonomous execution, a phenomenon we call skill Enable But Underuse (EBU). This issue is particularly consequential in medical tasks, where overlooking a single critical constraint can yield a plausible but incorrect result. To evaluate and address EBU, we introduce MedSkill-E$^{2}$, a framework for evaluating and evolving skills for medical agents. We construct EBU-Bench, a challenging benchmark covering 12 medically relevant task groups and associated skills, to evaluate agents' ability to use available skills autonomously. We quantify the EBU gap as the difference in skill-use accuracy between autonomous use and explicitly guided reading and application of the same skill. On EBU-Bench, all evaluated model-harness combinations exhibit EBU, with a mean gap of 43.94 percentage points (pp). For the same model, skill-use accuracy under autonomous use differs by up to 38.96 pp across harnesses. Motivated by these observations, we propose EBU-Evolve, which uses execution feedback from autonomous and explicitly guided skill use to distill effective guidance into reusable skill instructions. Relative to their pre-evolution versions, the revised skills improve skill-use accuracy during autonomous execution by 33.33 to 55.17 pp and continue to yield improvements when transferred across multiple models and harnesses. Our results show that autonomous skill-use accuracy varies across harnesses with the model and skill held fixed, and model rankings can change. EBU can persist even after a skill is opened. EBU-Evolve improves autonomous skill-use accuracy on unseen tasks within the same skill, with gains transferring across models and harnesses.


### arXiv 2611.34249 - Navigent: A Geometry-Grounded Agentic Framework for Vision Language Navigation

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T12:12:31Z
- authors: Xinyu Zhou, Weiren Cai, Yitong Chen, Shenghai Yuan, Zihang Mu, Boyu Ma
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34249

Vision-and-Language Navigation (VLN) with general-purpose multimodal large language models (MLLMs) offers a flexible alternative to task-specific navigation policies, while reliable long-horizon execution remains challenging. Agents must track navigation progress, detect discrepancies between commanded and actual motion, and recognize when movement has passed relevant landmarks or entrances. We present Navigent, an agentic VLN framework built around a frozen MLLM and a Geometry-Grounded Execution Critic (GEC). GEC performs two complementary checks: action-to-motion consistency, which evaluates measured displacement against commanded movement, and motion-to-landmark consistency, which tracks agent-selected visual anchors and entrance candidates in world coordinates. Detected discrepancies interrupt the remaining action sequence and trigger reobservation, local adjustment, or node-based recovery. Historical visual memory supports progress tracking across extended routes. An offline self-improvement procedure further analyzes successful and failed training trajectories to refine navigation prompts and GEC logic while keeping model weights fixed. On the R2R-CE validation-unseen split (1,839 episodes) and RxR-CE validation-unseen split(3,669 episodes), Navigent achieves 84.0%/86.0% SR and 72.9%/69.1% SPL, respectively, demonstrating the value of geometry-grounded execution verification for reliable agentic navigation. Project Page: https://marslab-navigent.github.io/navigent.github.io/ Code: https://github.com/MarsLab-Navigent/Navigent


### arXiv 2611.34248 - EXPOSURE: Explaining Sources of Uncertainty in Reinforcement Learning

- categories: cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-25T12:11:44Z
- authors: Kiana Farhadyar, Yannick Vogt, Philipp Laur, Ayça Çelik, Iman Nematollahi, Gabriel Kalweit
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34248

Shapley values in reinforcement learning are used to explain which observation features drive an agent's behavior and expected return, but not its certainty. Explaining certainty requires understanding how observation features influence different sources of return uncertainty, and Shapley values need the agent's prediction for every subset of visible features. We propose EXPOSURE, which uses one mask-aware belief network for both action selection and Shapley explanations. It explains the variance of the return, split into an observational part due to missing features and an inherent part due to the environment's own randomness, and the entropy of its belief over the masked features. In tic-tac-toe and Minesweeper, where exact Shapley values are computable, EXPOSURE gives more accurate explanations of expected return and its uncertainty, while uniquely explaining belief uncertainty.


### arXiv 2611.34247 - Did the Model Forecast It, or Already Know It? Evaluating Large Language Models for Novel Drug-Target Prioritisation with a Temporal Knowledge-Graph Oracle

- categories: cs.AI, q-bio.QM
- published: 2026-11-25T12:10:57Z
- authors: Pui Chung Siu, Yuan Liang, Claudia Cabrera, Mani Mudaliar, Arkaitz Zubiaga
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34247

AI systems increasingly help decide which drug targets enter costly clinical trials. We recast each target-disease hypothesis as a dated event, the pair's first clinical trial. This lets us test whether a language model can forecast such events after its training cutoff, rather than recall events before it. We rebuild a dated biomedical knowledge graph as it stood at the end of each year from 2020 up to its newest version. We then ask six open language models, with and without this graph, which targets will enter a first trial for a disease the next year. Without retrieval, the models rank about as well as trial history, a count of each target's past trials in the same therapeutic area, also after their training cutoffs. Evidence dated before the decision adds at most a small gain, before or after the models' training cutoffs. The newest graph raises five of six models clearly above that baseline, but its paths usually include the answer: the pair's own trial, dated after the decision. Evaluations of target prioritisation should date both features and labels, with evidence from before the decision and outcomes after it.


### arXiv 2611.34230 - Weakly Conditioned Diffusion Model Augmentation for Prostate Segmentation
