- authors: Jaemin Kim, Christian Simon, Dongseok Shim, Wei-Yao Wang, Qiyu Wu, Masato Ishii
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33957

Online forward matching aligns flow-based generative models by repeatedly generating, rewarding, and fitting model-produced endpoints. However, this behavior creates an on-policy feedback loop: as high-reward modes become increasingly favored, alternative modes become less likely to appear in subsequent training pools, progressively reducing visual diversity. We introduce RefMix, a reference-assisted endpoint proposal strategy that addresses this limitation by injecting alternative candidates into the training pool while retaining the original task reward and forward matching objective. In particular, RefMix uses a reference model during early high-noise steps to expose alternative visual modes, followed by the current reward-tuned model for endpoint completion. Across state-of-the-art online forward matching methods, RefMix consistently improves visual diversity while preserving task reward performance. Controlled inference experiments further demonstrate that training-time endpoint exposure is critical to improve reward--diversity trade-offs, beyond the impact of reference-assisted inference. By rethinking how training endpoints are proposed, RefMix achieves up to 44% higher diversity over forward matching baselines at comparable reward levels.


### arXiv 2611.33952 - DuoMI: Dual Mutual Information Regularization for Expert Reuse in Looped Transformers

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T08:13:44Z
- authors: Xinlai Kang, Zhenhe Wu, Haoyu Wang, Weihua He, Yaping Jin, Jie Hu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33952

We introduce Dual Mutual Information Regularization (DuoMI), a method for regulating expert routing across rounds in looped mixture-of-experts (MoE) Transformers. When two rounds of computation share an expert pool, their routers may favor different expert subsets, limiting the reuse of individual expert parameters across rounds. Even when aggregate expert usage is identical across rounds, we would still prefer each token to retain some experts while switching to others. DuoMI addresses both aspects of routing: it uses round-level marginal mutual information to limit the dependence of aggregate expert usage on the round, and token-level conditional mutual information to penalize average cross-round routing changes that fall outside a target interval. During training, DuoMI computes differentiable penalties from soft routing distributions over all experts. It adds no model parameters and leaves sparse Top-$k$ routing strategy unchanged at inference. On a looped OLMoE backbone in which paired adjacent layers share experts, DuoMI improves the average score across $15$ downstream tasks from $50.08\%$ to $51.82\%$, a gain of $1.74$ percentage points over the looped baseline.


### arXiv 2611.33946 - Should Service Requirements Be Scalarized or Constrained When Minimizing Costs? A Case Study on HVAC Control

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T08:07:35Z
- authors: Tai-You Chen, Mihael Mohorcic, Blaz Bertalanic, Carolina Fortuna
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33946

Automated decision systems, from learned policies to model-based optimizers, often minimize costs such as energy or resource use while meeting a service requirement, for example the quality of service (QoS) specified in a service level agreement (SLA), a comfort band, or a safety margin. The requirement can be scalarized with the costs into a single objective, with a fixed or an adaptively tuned (Lagrangian) weight, or it can be imposed as a constraint. We study this choice in the Smart Buildings Challenge, in which a single policy controls the heating, ventilation and air conditioning (HVAC) of previously unseen buildings and is evaluated by a weighted sum of comfort, energy cost and carbon emissions. With all other components fixed, we compare the two treatments in model-based optimization, represented by model predictive control (MPC) with the requirement as a hard or a soft (l1-penalized) constraint, and in learned policies, represented by proximal policy optimization (PPO) with a fixed or a Lagrangian penalty in the reward or a projection layer on the action. In MPC, the soft constraint reproduces the hard one once its weight exceeds the requirement's shadow price, which the challenge's comfort weight exceeds approximately fourfold; below this weight, comfort is sacrificed, and under a metric that values comfort below its shadow price the soft constraint outperforms the hard one by up to 28%. A weight tuned on the training buildings is 3.9% worse than the hard constraint on 48 unseen buildings, because the shadow price varies across buildings. In PPO, the projected policy satisfies the requirement from the outset and leads early in training; with longer training, the Lagrangian policy overtakes it while the projected policy deteriorates, and the fixed penalty does not learn to satisfy the requirement.


### arXiv 2611.33943 - What Does Skill Lift Actually Measure? A Source-Code-Level Audit and Noise-Aware Reanalysis of SkillsBench

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.SE
- published: 2026-11-25T08:02:04Z
- authors: Haozhe Zhang, Zhen Long
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33943

Agent skills are directories of instructions, reference materials, and scripts loaded on demand by coding agents. According to SkillsBench, although human-curated skills can raise the passing rate by about 16.6 percentage points (pp) on average, they have actually reduced the results for some tasks. Later research has started to gate or eliminate skills according to the above per-task gains. We have audited the above benchmark at the source-code level and analysed 7,554 public trials to form 1,421 paired "(configuration, task) cells". Source-code audit identified two controlled-verified cases of grader dependence and one operational definition gap; the average lift on these three tasks was 74.6 pp, and the overall average lift for the full set was 18.7 pp. The audit also found that four tasks contained placeholder skill directories, and a certain execution harness additionally added 48 built-in skills in most runs. No task-level reduction achieved statistical significance in the pooled reanalysis with multiple-testing correction. As there were at most three runs per condition for each cell, the observed negative differences are very susceptible to run-sampling noise, and arithmetically, 58% of the cells could not have shown a decrease. Therefore, the raw numbers of negative cells cannot show that skills cause harm. A gating strategy based on the skill-free score seems to improve by 2-3 pp in the logs; however, even after shuffling condition labels to remove any skill effect, it still achieves an increase of 4-7 pp. On the held-out runs not used for fitting and decision-making, there was no statistically significant improvement. The audit ledger and analysis scripts have been published by us.


### arXiv 2611.33937 - InstructTTS-OPD: On-Policy Distillation for Multi-Objective Instruction-Following TTS

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, eess.AS
- published: 2026-11-25T07:56:16Z
- authors: Haitao Li, Tian Tan, Yuguang Yang, Guanrou Yang, Wenhao Guan, Chenglin Li
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33937

Instruction-following text-to-speech (Instruct-TTS) renders a target transcript in the manner described by a free-form natural-language instruction, and a good rendition must succeed on three fronts at once: following the instruction, pronouncing the transcript correctly, and sounding natural. Reinforcement learning has become the standard way to post-train such generative models toward human preferences, and TTS systems increasingly adopt it. When the three objectives are optimized together under a single scalar reward, typically with group relative policy optimization (GRPO), training tends to be unstable: the objectives compete, and the shared update drifts toward whichever one is easiest to improve, producing a seesaw among them. We instead adapt on-policy distillation (OPD), which fuses several single-objective teachers by matching their velocity fields on the student's own rollouts. Realizing this for Instruct-TTS first calls for a sound reward on each axis, which we analyze carefully: a distilled Qwen-Omni proxy for the costly Gemini judge, dialect-free data for the WER reward, and DNSMOS in place of the language-biased UTMOS. We then train three single-objective teachers with GRPO and fuse them with OPD, routing each rollout to the instruction-following or WER teacher by its data domain and letting every rollout additionally follow the MOS teacher for quality. This lets instruction following and intelligibility improve together without a trade-off, but the quality target conflicts with instruction following, so following it additively reintroduces the seesaw. We therefore propose conflict-aware velocity fusion, which projects the quality target off the direction that opposes the primary objective before fusing, and show that it markedly reduces the instruction-following/quality trade-off. The fused model improves over scalar-reward GRPO on all three objectives at once.


### arXiv 2611.33934 - Towards 360$^\circ$ Embodied UAV Tracking Without Turning

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T07:54:11Z
- authors: Bingyu Li, Wu Du, Zhiyuan Zhao, Junyu Gao, Xuelong Li
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33934

Conventional embodied UAV tracking couples target observability with heading control because targets may leave the field of view of forward-facing cameras. We investigate a complementary paradigm that uses full-spherical perception to follow designated targets without active heading adjustment. To support this setting, we introduce 360UAV-ETrack, a simulation benchmark comprising 2,652 sequences, over 1.73 million synchronized observations, eight tracking tasks, and 150 held-out closed-loop scenarios. We propose 360UAV-TVLA, a panoramic vision--language--action (VLA) framework integrating persistent visual referring (PVR), compressed temporal memory (CTM), and geometry-aligned trajectory supervision (GTS) for instance identification, motion reasoning, and three-dimensional control. Experiments across two VLA backbones show consistent gains over standard fine-tuning, with normalized average tracking frames increasing from 22.56\% to 35.28\% for $π_0$ and from 25.68\% to 33.64\% for $π_{0.5}$. Our benchmark further reveals a substantial gap between offline imitation accuracy and closed-loop tracking reliability, establishing a challenging testbed for future research https://libingyu01.github.io/360UAV-ETrack_360UAV-TVLA/


### arXiv 2611.33926 - Connecting AI Agents to Live Discrete Element Simulations via the Model Context Protocol

- categories: cs.CE, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T07:46:35Z
- authors: Yusong Han, Ryosuke Uzuoka, Kyohei Ueda
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33926

This paper describes a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server through which a general-purpose AI agent operates a discrete element engine, commercial or open-source, while the simulation runs. The agent submits a long simulation to the engine and, while it computes, reads the running model, changes it, or stops it. The human modeler stays at the engine's console and graphical interface throughout, and the agent sees what the modeler does there. The tools are derived from what the agent needs to do at the engine and are held to four principles: correctness, coverage, orthogonality and simplicity. Two documentation tools let the agent search the engine's manual and browse it level by level. Five execution tools let the agent run code in the engine, submit long simulations in the background, check on them, read the running model, and stop them. A bridge inside the engine serves these tools in a commercial DEM engine, PFC, and an open-source one, YADE. In a case study, a general-purpose agent calibrated a drained triaxial model of dense Toyoura sand against laboratory data in both engines, while the modeler worked at the same engine; the agent noticed the modeler's actions at the console and acted on them. The same calibration on YADE was then given to seven language models, three sessions each and without human intervention. Twenty of the twenty-one sessions reached the targets, and nineteen of the parameter sets lie in one small region of the admissible ranges. The server and the bridge are released as two open-source packages, itasca-mcp, which also serves FLAC, 3DEC, MPoint and MassFlow, and yade-mcp.


### arXiv 2611.33925 - Beyond the Happy Path: Generalizing Robot World Models from Simulated Failures

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T07:45:51Z
- authors: Zhiyue Zhao, Mingyu Liu, Bolin Zhang, Liyang Li, Guangyu Huang, Canyu Zhao
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33925

Robot world models are increasingly used to evaluate and plan policies, yet robot training data are dominated by successful demonstrations. Off the happy path, when a grasp is misaligned or mistimed and the object is pushed, tilted, or slips, their predictions break down. Across five pretrained action-conditioned models, object-motion error is at most 10.4% lower than that of a static baseline that copies the first frame, and two models are worse than this baseline. We present WorldReforge, which teaches world models what happens when manipulation goes wrong. We construct the Reforge dataset of 62k simulated trajectories by perturbing reference grasps in position, orientation, and closure timing, recording each resulting push, slip, or failed pickup with ground-truth object pose, contact, and surface points. WorldReforge turns this ground truth into two training signals without additional inference cost. Interaction Alignment focuses reconstruction on the small object and contact regions that a global loss underweights, and Surface Correspondence trains backbone features to follow the same physical surface points across frames. On Cosmos3 Nano, WorldReforge reduces object trajectory error by 28.2% overall and by 35.0% on unseen objects, and it lowers the error in predicting how two actions' outcomes diverge by 43.3%. Although trained only in simulation, it also improves reconstruction on DROID and on real failure videos. Controlled data studies show that failed contact and successful holding teach complementary physics: failed contact teaches how objects respond, and holding teaches how a grasped state persists. Our code, data, and models are available at aim-uofa.github.io/WorldReforge/.


### arXiv 2611.33919 - DiCALM: Converting Autoregressive Convolution-Attention Language Models into Discrete Diffusion Language Models

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T07:38:13Z
- authors: Phong Le, Ngoc-Quan Pham, Dat Quoc Nguyen
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33919

Existing methods for adapting autoregressive (AR) language models to diffusion generation focus mainly on full-attention architectures. More efficient hybrid architectures remain underexplored. We introduce DiCALM, a recipe for converting the hybrid convolution-attention models of the LFM2 family (LFM2 and LFM2.5) from AR to masked-diffusion generation. DiCALM progressively extends LFM2's causal gated convolution so that it captures local context in both directions. It combines this extension with a masking warm-up and joint AR--diffusion training. In controlled experiments, DiCALM performs comparably to the corresponding fine-tuned LFM2.5-based AR models and delivers consistent speedups over them at both the 350M and 1.2B scales: over 1.4x on average and up to 1.79x.


### arXiv 2611.33909 - EGSE: Evidence-Grounded Skill Evolution for Medical Reasoning Agents

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T07:28:21Z
- authors: Mingyuan Li, Tong Jia, Ziyuan Yang, Wenbo Qi, Haoran Wang, Jie Gui
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33909

Medical reasoning agents combine foundation models with specialized tools to interpret medical images, yet reusing their historical experience remains challenging. A correct final answer can conceal flawed reasoning or tool use, synthesized advice may lack source support, and superficially similar cases may require different diagnostic procedures. In this paper, we propose EGSE, an evidence-grounded skill evolution framework that distills historical dialogues from similar cases into reusable conditional workflows and progressively refines a multi-faceted skill library through supported and validated updates to improve medical agents. EGSE evaluates intermediate reasoning and tool use and contrasts repeated successful and failed rollouts to extract useful practices and evidence-supported avoidance guidance. These experiences are synthesized into workflows with applicability conditions and checks traceable to source dialogues. Library additions and merges require source-grounded review and execution validation. At inference time, EGSE routes queries to skill nodes organized by imaging modality, question task type, and disease category, then retrieves and selects applicable workflows based on their conditions, the current images, and the question to guide evidence acquisition and interpretation. Experiments across four chest X-ray benchmarks show that EGSE outperforms representative medical agents and skill evolution methods in reasoning accuracy while reducing tool calls. It also delivers consistent accuracy gains across seven foundation models and supports skill transfer across datasets.


### arXiv 2611.33900 - Correlation-Aware Evidential Fusion for Trusted Multi-View Classification

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, stat.ML
- published: 2026-11-25T07:18:37Z
- authors: Tianyang Wang, Mengchi Zuo, Cai Xu, Yilin Zhang, Ziyu Guan, Wei Zhao
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33900

Trusted multi-view classification aims to make accurate and reliable predictions by integrating information from multiple views. Existing methods typically estimate class evidence and uncertainty for each view and combine the resulting opinions through aggregation rules. However, they generally treat different views as independent information sources, overlooking the correlations among them. Highly correlated views tend to produce similar evidence, and treating them as independent sources may repeatedly count consistent information and give it disproportionate influence, while weakening complementary evidence that provides additional information. Consequently, such correlation-unaware fusion may not only limit classification performance but also produce miscalibrated uncertainty estimates. To address this issue, we propose Correlation-Aware Fusion of Evidence (CAFE), a framework for trusted multi-view classification. CAFE applies complementarity-based trust discounting to the evidence produced by view-private experts and introduces a correlation-aware constraint to reduce the repeated contribution of similar evidence. It then learns sample-specific weights from the global multi-view context to adaptively combine the correlation-adjusted private evidence with cross-view collaborative evidence. Extensive experiments on six real-world datasets demonstrate that CAFE consistently improves classification and calibration performance in general settings, with particularly significant gains when strong correlations exist among views.


### arXiv 2611.33897 - EATS: Scaling Tool-Use Experience for Medical Reasoning Agents

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T07:17:18Z
- authors: Mingyuan Li, Tong Jia, Haoran Wang, Wenbo Qi, Ziyuan Yang, Bowen Ma
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33897

Medical reasoning agents combine large language model (LLM) reasoning with clinical workflows and specialized neural tools. However, general-purpose LLMs receive limited training exposure to domain-specific medical tools, and the uncertainty and distributional shifts of neural tool outputs make selecting tools from simple descriptions significantly more challenging than in deterministic settings such as calculators or dictionary lookups. In this paper, we propose EATS, an experience-augmented tool selection strategy that leverages accumulated tool-use trajectories to improve tool selection in medical reasoning agents. EATS constructs a memory of past interactions, including queries, tool choices, arguments, and self-evaluated feedback, and retrieves relevant experiences to guide tool selection for new queries. As memory construction does not require ground-truth supervision, EATS scales naturally with test-time experience. We evaluate EATS on tool-augmented medical reasoning tasks, including closed-ended visual question answering and free-form report generation. Experiments on four public datasets, ChestAgentBench, CheXbench, MIMIC-CXR, and SLAKE-VQA, across three multimodal LLMs, Qwen3-VL 2B, Qwen3-VL 32B, and GPT-5.5, show consistent gains in tool selection behavior and downstream medical reasoning performance. Code is available at https://github.com/Limingyuan001/EATS.


### arXiv 2611.33893 - When Are Parallel Subagents Effective for Coding? An Empirical Study on Repository Construction

- categories: cs.MA, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T07:11:29Z
- authors: Zhiheng Lyu, Chengyu Yin, Lingming Zhang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33893

LLM-based coding agents are increasingly used for long-horizon tasks such as building an entire program from a specification. Products like Codex and Claude Code let an agent delegate work to parallel subagents, multiplying run cost, yet it remains unclear whether and when delegation produces a better program. We compare a single agent with a main agent delegating to subagents on repository-construction tasks from NL2Repo and ProgramBench (which also provides an executable reference program). We compare scores at equal time and equal cost, run intervention experiments, and analyze fine-grained behavior. On randomly drawn tasks, parallel subagents raise hidden-test pass rates by only about 1 to 5 percentage points at up to 3.8x the cost of a single agent. Their gain is larger on hard tasks, where we find evidence of three mechanisms. Self-checking: with GPT-5.6 Sol, one sentence telling a single agent to probe the reference program first recovers most of this gain at a fraction of the cost; from GPT-5.6 Sol to GPT-6.1 Sol, the gain shrinks as single agents check their own work more. Fresh context: in extended runs, a single agent that periodically starts a new session with an empty conversation makes faster early progress in every comparison than one that resumes its conversation. Modularity: multi-agent runs produce more modular code (more files and shorter functions), closer to the upstream source. We observe similar patterns in a smaller replication with Opus 5.5 on Claude Code. Overall, subagents help when (i) a single agent stops checking too early, (ii) a long session keeps editing with little improvement, or (iii) modular code helps on the task; otherwise, they mainly add cost.


### arXiv 2611.33879 - FORK: Co-Evolving Safe Disclosure and Decision Skills Against Attackers Who Read Every Refusal

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T06:57:56Z
- authors: Hieu Man, Chao Shang, Rui Dong, Devang Kulshreshtha, Yanjun Qi, Sandesh Swamy
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33879

When a guardrail rejects an LLM agent's action, its message reaches any attacker observing the trajectory: a fixed refusal leaves benign users unable to recover, whereas an explanation can tell the attacker what to try next. We study this as safe disclosure: explaining rejections so that authorized tasks recover without increasing adaptive attack success. Safe disclosure depends on both the decision and the message: what the guardrail blocks and what it reveals to the agent and the attacker. Because hand-written rules alone cannot anticipate how adaptive attackers probe decisions or use messages, we introduce FORK, which co-evolves a guardrail's natural-language decision and disclosure skills with an attacker that reads every public message. Each exploited message and permissive decision gives FORK evidence for a gradient-free revision of its natural-language skill library. We evaluate FORK on four benchmarks. Averaged over three agents on FORK-World, FORK cuts attack success rate (ASR) under adaptive attack from 78.4% to 14.1% and keeps utility above 85%, outperforming baselines on both ASR and utility. Notably, on MT-AgentRisk, whose environments and tasks are unseen in training, FORK lowers ASR from 70.6% to 9.1% with 93.1% benign utility.


### arXiv 2611.34754 - AutoRSP: An AI Agent for Radar Sensing Pipeline Design

- categories: cs.NI, cs.MA, eess.SP
- published: 2026-11-25T17:26:36Z
- authors: Zhi Zheng, Xincheng Xie, Avery Gump, Sacha Jungerman, Hongyang Li, Yin Li
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34754

Radar sensing demands a pipeline that is well-thought-out across hardware and software. We ask whether an AI agent can automatically design radar sensing pipelines at a fraction of the time, cost, and expertise that current practice needs. We present AutoRSP, an agent that embeds radar-system-level structure through sub-agents mapped to five stack layers, a code-based representation of every layer with explicitly modeled cross-layer dependencies. Feedback for the agentic search process comes from a pipeline of realistic RF simulation, on-sensor emulation, and execution on real edge devices, rather than from an LLM judge. Because such feedback can be slower than an LLM call, we present methods to efficiently optimize agentic loops. We evaluate AutoRSP on robotic imaging, automotive perception, and vital sign sensing. It produces executable pipelines with performance comparable to or better than human-expert pipelines and closed-source agents. AutoRSP's harness, tools, and benchmarks will be released to bridge the rising gap in agentic research abilities outside of frontier labs.


### arXiv 2611.34293 - Energy-Aware Distributed Task Allocation via Local Submodular Maximization

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-25T12:46:38Z
- authors: Jing Liu, Fangfei Li, Xin Jin, Yang Tang, Ruggero Carli, Luca Ballotta
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34293

This paper studies distributed task allocation under time-varying agent sets, task sets, and communication graphs. At each decision epoch, the stage allocation problem is formulated as monotone submodular maximization over a time-varying, energy-filtered admissible ground set subject to a partition-matroid constraint. We develop a Distributed Adaptive Greedy Bidding Algorithm (DAGBA) that combines local admissible-set construction, conditional marginal bidding, and neighbor-based conflict resolution under limited communication. We prove that DAGBA guarantees per-epoch feasibility, finite-round convergence, and residual-energy safety. Theoretically, we identify a sequential-greedy regime where DAGBA recovers the classical $1/2$-approximation guarantee. For general executions with partial information, we derive a local-information-factor approximation bound and conservative bounds computable from the execution trace that quantify the degradation caused by local estimates and parallel fixing. Simulations on cooperative observation missions demonstrate that DAGBA achieves task-completion progress and utility close to centralized and complete-communication references, while improving communication efficiency and maintaining strict energy feasibility.


### arXiv 2611.33793 - LSRP*-LA: Scalable and Anytime Multi-Agent Path Finding for Large Agents with Asynchronous Actions

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-25T05:19:43Z
- authors: Dachuan Liu, Shizhe Zhao, Zhongqiang Ren
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33793

Multi-Agent Path Finding (MAPF) seeks collision-free paths for multiple agents from their start locations to their goal locations while minimizing path costs. Most MAPF algorithms assume that each agent occupies a single vertex and that the actions of all agents are synchronized. These assumptions may limit the use of MAPF planners in practice. Existing planners that get rid of both assumptions either handle only a few agents or cannot improve their solutions. Recently, a planner called LSRP* was proposed, which is scalable and anytime with asynchronous actions, but still limited by that each agent occupies a single vertex. This paper develops a new planner called LSRP*-LA for MAPF for large agents with asynchronous actions (LA-MAPF-AA), where an agent can occupy several cells. LSRP*-LA is scalable and complete, and it keeps improving solution quality after the initial solution is obtained until the runtime budget depletes. We analyze the properties of LSRP*-LA and test it against several baselines with up to 800 agents on various maps. LSRP*-LA can handle twice as many agents as baseline methods and its solution cost is about half that of recent synchronous planners for large agents.


### arXiv 2611.33627 - Welfare-Enhancing Mechanism Design for Agent Ecosystems

- categories: cs.MA, cs.GT, cs.IR
- published: 2026-11-25T01:57:22Z
- authors: Zitian Guo, Chen Xu, Beibei Li, Chenyan Xiong
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33627

The emerging Agentic Web forms a multi-agent ecosystem: a decentralized service market where the user agent selects which content agents to query from their submitted profiles, before seeing any documents. Profiles therefore become a new target of optimization. Existing Generative Engine Optimization studies how a single supplier rewrites documents the engine can already retrieve, to improve their citation. In the Agentic Web, multiple content agents optimize profiles to compete for limited opportunities to deliver documents. To study this repeated interaction, we build WildRegistry, a simulation framework and benchmark based on a real agentic-search request stream, and model it as a dynamic game under private monitoring. Long-horizon experiments show that this competition lowers website-selection quality and settles at a harmful stable point, which our empirical criterion identifies as an approximate rewriting equilibrium. We therefore design a welfare-enhancing mechanism, DEC (Delivery-Evidence Credit), a platform-side policy that uses recorded delivery outcomes to reallocate later opportunities and reduce information asymmetry. DEC improves this endpoint, raising website-selection quality while preserving most of providers' exposure gains, for a Welfare Gain of +2.01 points and a healthier long-horizon outcome. More broadly, our findings highlight the importance of mechanism design in agent ecosystems: turning recorded delivery outcomes into evidence that endogenously shapes later user-agent selection and content-agent adaptation can steer long-horizon adaptation toward delivered capabilities.


### arXiv 2611.33373 - From Conversations to Commitments: A Design Study of Shared Records for Agent-to-Business Coordination

- categories: cs.CR, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-24T20:02:56Z
- authors: Carlos Eberhardt, Mansura Habiba
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33373

Business commitments require accepted terms to be linked to delegated authority, reserved resources and durable evidence. We examine how a shared, attributable interaction record can support these links across independently operated organisations. Talea combines signed AT Protocol records with A2A task execution. We use its coordination sandbox and a proposed commercial extension in a design study of an airline booking disrupted passengers into three competing hotels. Four observations follow. First, AT Protocol provides mechanisms supporting five of six coordination requirements, while record-level access and release controls require additional enforcement. Second, the case's confidentiality constraints restrict each hotel's authorised view to its bilateral exchange; only the airline can correlate all three deals. Third, this leaves a possible benefit for late-joining participants inside the airline's trust boundary, but does not establish an advantage over an equally instrumented point-to-point design. Fourth, a deterministic release interface constrains fixed, approved payloads but cannot bound disclosure when negotiated values depend on private state. Finite-model explorations and deterministic interface checks reproduce the specified behaviours and counterexamples when selected controls are removed. They do not measure comparative benefit or validate a deployed commercial system. The remaining hypothesis is that, within one trust boundary, shared records improve decision reconstruction and recovery under changing participation relative to point-to-point exchange with equivalent authority, reservation, retention and correlation evidence. We specify an experiment to test it.


### arXiv 2611.33318 - What Must LLM Agents Communicate? From Information Loss to Communication Failures

- categories: cs.MA, cs.IT, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-24T19:07:21Z
- authors: Yi-Yun Chu, Xiaobin Shen
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33318

When evidence is distributed across LLM agents, a team may collectively possess all the information needed for a decision yet fail to use it effectively. What must agents communicate for accurate joint predictions, and when does interaction help? We study three requirements for effective communication: sufficiency of the information conveyed, selection of relevant evidence, and utilization of received information. Drawing on information theory and communication complexity, we develop a framework that combines theoretical benchmarks with controlled experiments and message-level analyses. We find that local probability reports can discard essential information, with theoretical information loss closely matching observed performance penalties. Query-first interaction improves predictions when relevant evidence depends on a private query, but compact one-shot representations can eliminate this advantage. Even when sufficient information is communicated, agents may fail to select or use it correctly. Together, these findings distinguish fundamental communication limits from failures in how LLM agents communicate and use information.


### arXiv 2611.33242 - CypherMAS: Benchmarking Routing Reliability in LLM-Based Multi-Agent Systems

- categories: cs.MA, cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-24T14:39:07Z
- authors: Danil Kostromin, Andrey Sakhovskiy, Semen Budennyy
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33242

Multi-agent systems (MAS) built on large language models (LLMs) distribute reasoning across specialized agents that must exchange messages and invoke tools reliably. Existing evaluations typically couple communication with open domain tasks, making it difficult to determine whether a failure arises from reasoning, malformed tool use, lost messages, or forgotten requests. Prior benchmarks do not isolate communication execution or measure routing redundancy against a known optimal path. We introduce CypherMAS, a synthetic benchmark that isolates communication and routing in fixed multi-agent graphs. Agents in chain, star, bipartite, or ring graphs route cyphered messages to remote peers holding the matching decoders; since the optimal route is known, redundant LLM calls can be measured and routing failures may be easily tracked. Across eleven open-weight models from 4B to 753B, small models fail at routing and tool use, dense models above 27B degrade with diameter, and successful runs exceed minimum LLM calls. These findings identify communication reliability as a distinct scaling bottleneck for multi-agent systems and provide a controlled diagnostic for complex multi-agent systems. The source code is freely available: https://github.com/Andoree/CypherMAS.


### arXiv 2611.33228 - Soft Decentralized Expected SARSA: Equilibrium Selection via Gradual Adaptation

- categories: cs.MA, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-24T09:07:39Z
- authors: Giuseppe Martucci, Janusz M. Meylahn, Gurdal Arslan, Serdar Yuksel
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33228

We introduce Soft Decentralized Expected SARSA (SDES), a modification of Decentralized Q-learning in which agents maintain persistent exploration and update their acting Q-values gradually rather than switching directly to newly learned best responses when updating. We prove convergence to $ε$-greedy Nash equilibria in weakly acyclic stochastic games for sufficiently large batch sizes. We then show, in the repeated Prisoner's Dilemma with memory, that soft updating can reshape basins of attraction without changing the equilibrium set. In the deterministic limit, we derive analytical basin bounds governed by the Q-value differences between actions in the target policies and show that reducing the policy learning rate can favor either cooperative or competitive equilibria. These results suggest that soft updating is not merely a stabilization device but also a mechanism of equilibrium selection in decentralized learning.


### arXiv 2611.34925 - One Ocean, Many Tasks: A Data-Grounded Simulation Suite and Benchmark for Marine Robotics

- categories: cs.RO, cs.DB
- published: 2026-11-25T18:58:56Z
- authors: Shuaijun Liu, Qifu Wen, Xingwei Chen, Xinwen Hu, Feiyang You, Sijia Chen
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34925

Simulation is essential for marine robotics, yet existing platforms often trade off between visually realistic worlds, high-throughput experimentation, and ocean-condition realism grounded in real data. We present OneOcean, a data-grounded simulation suite and benchmark built around a unified spatiotemporal ocean-environment product that harmonizes bathymetry with data-driven currents and tides, with optional pollution fields for cleanup-related evaluation. On top of this environment representation, OneOcean defines a task ladder spanning navigation and station-keeping under currents, waypoint and route following, depth-profile tracking, area scanning, pipeline inspection, and multi-agent coordination, including formation transit, fish protection and patrol (herding), and surface pollution localization, containment, and cleanup. The suite supports multi-agent settings with 2-10 vehicles. Our simulator supports three to six Degrees of Freedom (DoF) and produces standardized metrics and reproducible run manifests, enabling consistent aggregation across tasks, difficulties, and scaling settings. Experiments across dataset variants and scenes systematically stress planning and control under data-grounded disturbances and reveal trade-offs among heuristic baselines, Behavior Cloning (BC), and a Large Language Model (LLM)-based planner.


### arXiv 2611.34907 - Agentic LongLive: Asynchronous Agentic Memory for Long Video Generation

- categories: cs.CV
- published: 2026-11-25T18:54:03Z
- authors: Shaoteng Liu, Shuai Yang, Wei Huang, Luozhou Wang, Song Han, Yukang Chen
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34907

Real-time autoregressive video generators stream minutes of video and accept new prompts on the fly, but struggle to keep people, objects, and places consistent over a long interactive story. Agent systems provide such memory for offline multi-shot generation, yet they read the whole script in advance, plan each shot before generating it, and regenerate failed shots, which a live stream does not allow. We present Agentic LongLive, an asynchronous agentic memory for long video generation. A memory agent on separate GPUs reads only the prompts revealed so far, never the generated video, and keeps a text state and evolving images for every person, object, and scene. From this entity memory it composes a global image and a per-shot memory image, which an auxiliary GPU prefills into the generator's attention sink before the next shot starts, and writes entity cards for the prompts that name them, so the video never waits for memory and video errors are not written back. A small language model, trained with reinforcement learning and an image reward calibrated using downstream video outcomes, decides what to remember before the next prompt is known. On LongStory-Online, a new benchmark of 25 stories (857 shots) revealed one shot at a time, Agentic LongLive improves persistent-state accuracy of LongLive-2.0, its base generator, by 26.3 points, protagonist identity by 44.9, and revisited-place consistency by 40.4, at about 1% extra generation time on H100. The full system streams faster than real time on one 8xB200 node with the generator in BF16 (34.3 FPS), keeps image quality stable over 14-minute stories, and improves identity on two MiniMax-H3 generators, one of which, LongLive-H3, also runs in real time with it. To our knowledge, it is the first real-time video generator with text and image memory kept by an LLM agent.


### arXiv 2611.34818 - Deep Reinforcement Learning for Multi-UAV Data Harvesting Under Variable Team Sizes and Agent Loss

- categories: cs.LG, cs.DC
- published: 2026-11-25T18:03:00Z
- authors: Thiago de Souza Lamenza, Bruno José Olivieri de Souza, Markus Endler
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34818

Collecting data from wireless sensors can be challenging when limited communication infrastructure prevents them from offloading their measurements. In such settings, unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs) can exploit their mobility to harvest data locally, while coordinated UAV teams can provide efficient and redundant coverage of remote areas. Multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) enables such teams to learn decentralized coordination policies. However, training separate policies for different team and sensor populations is costly, and fixed-population policies may be vulnerable to agent failures. This work proposes F-MADDPG, a flexible variant of Multi-Agent Deep Deterministic Policy Gradient (MADDPG) that supports data harvesting with varying numbers of wireless sensors and UAVs. The framework also accommodates unexpected catastrophic failures that permanently remove agents during a mission. We compare F-MADDPG with a MADDPG baseline that uses multilayer perceptrons (MLPs) and evaluate training strategies based on team-size sampling and exposure to agent terminations. The results show that F-MADDPG learns across the evaluated population sizes, completes missions faster than the baseline on average, benefits from inverse-cardinality team-size sampling compared with uniform sampling, remains effective after permanent agent losses, and gains no consistent benefit from exposure to failures during training.


### arXiv 2611.34591 - ADClaw: A Programmable Visual Agent for Industrial Anomaly Detection and Understanding

- categories: cs.CV
- published: 2026-11-25T15:51:47Z
- authors: Xinyi Wu, Rongxin Gao, YuHui Lin, Yonglin Hong, Chu Li, Wei Li
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34591

Industrial anomaly detection (IAD), the task of identifying and localizing deviations from normal product conditions, is essential to automated quality inspection in manufacturing. Many vision-language models for IAD rely on large domain-specific datasets for fine-tuning and make predictions in a single pass, leaving no opportunity to re-examine ambiguous regions. Although tool-using agents can revisit these regions, predefined tool interfaces limit how they can combine intermediate results in a follow-up check. When such checks are insufficient, models may miss subtle defects or misidentify the defect type. To address this limitation, we introduce ADClaw, an inspection agent that uses executable code to test anomaly hypotheses without task-specific fine-tuning. For each hypothesis, a Structured Normality Prior identifies the applicable rules and retrieves normal visual references for the inspection target. Guided by this prior, the agent writes and executes code in a persistent Inspection Workbench to combine masks, anomaly maps, and measurements from earlier steps into a new test of the suspected defect. After each check, Hypothesis-Guided Evidence Reasoning assesses whether the evidence supports the suspected defect and its location, using gaps or inconsistencies in that evidence to guide the next code action. ADClaw achieves competitive results in detection and localization across 2D and 3D industrial anomaly detection benchmarks and in anomaly understanding on MMAD.


### arXiv 2611.34586 - Geometric Acceleration Obstacles: Physical Boundary Structure and Affine Safety Certificates

- categories: cs.RO
- published: 2026-11-25T15:45:49Z
- authors: Xuheng Gao, Yuhan Pang, Bi-Ke Zhu, Ronghao Chen, Lingxiao Meng, Jiankun Wang
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34586

Safe robot navigation in dynamic environments requires collision avoidance that respects motion constraints while remaining computationally efficient. Conventional velocity-obstacle (VO) methods characterize collision-inducing motions in velocity space. Although some formulations account for acceleration-limited velocity transitions, their collision constraints remain tied to specific velocity-tracking models. Acceleration obstacles (AO) address this limitation by representing collision-inducing inputs directly in acceleration space. However, existing AO methods either rely on conservative approximations or derive analytical structure expressions without verifying their geometric correspondence. To address this gap, this paper presents Geometric Acceleration Obstacles (GAO), which provides, to the best of our knowledge, the first unified and physically validated characterization of AO geometry and its supporting structure. Specifically, we derive configuration-dependent half-planes that globally support the collision set and establish their tightness, thereby separating certified collision-free acceleration inputs from collision-inducing parts. These geometric results yield unified affine safety constraints. Combined with actuator limits, these constraints define a quadratic program (QP) for real-time navigation. Extensive experiments demonstrate that GAO achieves competitive success rates and the lowest control effort among the evaluated methods, with millisecond-level computational latency. Further circle-interchange simulations demonstrate scalability to 1,000 agents. Physical experiments validate the approach in pedestrian encounters, reciprocal multi-agent navigation, and high-speed maneuvering.


### arXiv 2611.34536 - Kant-Q: A Q-Learning Algorithm for Kantian Equilibrium in Symmetric Markov Games

- categories: cs.LG, cs.GT
- published: 2026-11-25T15:19:10Z
- authors: Rishi Bharadwaj, Sujit Gujar, Yadati Narahari
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34536

AI agents increasingly interact with one another, and many of these interactions are mixed-motive games, which have posed a persistent challenge for learning algorithms. Most multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) algorithms are designed to reach Nash equilibria, yet in many such games these equilibria are Pareto-inefficient. We instead study Kantian equilibria, an economic solution concept in which each agent plays the action it would want all agents to adopt. In symmetric games, this corresponds to an optimal common policy. We propose Kant-Q, a Q-learning algorithm in which each agent learns action profiles where all agents play the same action. Each update is then a sample from a single-agent MDP, so Kant-Q inherits the convergence guarantees of standard Q-learning. We show that every symmetric Markov game has a pure Kantian equilibrium. We also prove that all agents' Q-tables converge to the same limit, whose greedy policy is such an equilibrium, and that greedy play converges whenever the optimal common action is unique at every state. We further show that in a subclass of symmetric monotone games, the Kantian equilibrium is Pareto-efficient among pure strategy profiles and strictly individually rational. Empirically, on the iterated Prisoner's Dilemma and the GovSim fishery with up to 200 agents, Kant-Q learns the Kantian equilibrium, while the baselines underperform. Since copies of the same AI system naturally face one another in symmetric games, these results suggest universalization as a design principle for cooperative AI-AI interaction.


### arXiv 2611.34211 - Compute-Aware Submodular Online Learning in Open Multi-Agent Systems

- categories: cs.LG, eess.SY
- published: 2026-11-25T11:35:43Z
- authors: Jing Liu, Luca Ballotta, Fangfei Li, Yang Tang, Ruggero Carli
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34211

This paper studies policy learning for cooperative task allocation in open multi-agent systems, where submodular utilities model diminishing marginal returns due to overlapping agent contributions. While standard methods evaluate every candidate action to update the policy, this may incur an excessive computational cost online. Hence, we impose a budget on these evaluations and analytically study the effect of query budget on online learning. Our algorithm samples a prescribed number of agent-action pairs and applies inverse-probability weighting to their marginal gains, yielding an unbiased gradient estimate of the expected stage utility for a Kullback-Leibler (KL) mirror-ascent update. We derive a $1/2$-approximate regret bound that elegantly splits into a tracking error between optimal and learned policies, quantified by their KL divergence, and a query-dependent feedback term. Experiments on cooperative information coverage show that our approach retains high performance with significantly less computation online.


### arXiv 2611.34132 - Agent-as-a-Verifier: From Scores to Evidence for General Verification

- categories: cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-25T10:31:08Z
- authors: Yuxuan Sun,  Hao,  Chen, Nagarajan Natarajan, Sushrut Karmalkar, Niranjani Prasad
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34132

Verification is increasingly critical across various inference and learning paradigms, such as test-time scaling, recursive intelligence and automated research, where feedback can improve efficiency and optimization. Common score-based verifiers, or discriminative reward models, predict a scalar reward for an output. While useful for filtering, rewarding, and ranking, such scores provide no explicit, auditable evidentiary basis for the judgment. In parallel, although existing generative process reward models offer text feedback, most designs do not fully exploit agentic capabilities, such as tool use and iterative reasoning, which are essential for fact-checking and fine-grained claim verification. In this paper, we address these limitations by introducing Agent-as-a-Verifier, an agentic, certificate-producing verification system that places role-separated LLM agents inside a formal-methods-inspired claim-obligation-certificate protocol. Given a generation candidate, Agent-as-a-Verifier produces a certificate by extracting a verdict-blind dependency graph of claims, independently compiling the task's acceptance contract, and deriving checkable obligations. On Terminal-Bench 2.1, Agent-as-a-Verifier achieves higher accuracy than the state-of-the-art approach. Across different downstream applications, the augmented agent also outperforms the plain agent on FrontierMath and CyberGym. More importantly, Agent-as-a-Verifier is able to identify reward-hacking solutions that deceive the benchmark evaluator, highlighting its broader potential for iterative and recursive agent optimization. Code is available at https://github.com/sunyxedu/General-Verifier


### arXiv 2611.34027 - ContextHands: Completing Hand Motion with Learned Context and Joint-Specific Body Kinematics

- categories: cs.CV
- published: 2026-11-25T09:13:29Z
- authors: Taewoo Kim, Seunghun Koh, Hyosup Shin, Sungho Jo
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34027

Detailed hand motion contributes to the realism and expressiveness of generated human motion. Completing these details from body motion and language requires connecting the context of an action to the articulation of the hands. We introduce ContextHands, which completes hand motion from an observed body sequence and text descriptions assigned to hands and time intervals. Our approach learns probabilistic hand features from the descriptions and body motion, using auxiliary hand prediction to connect these features to hand articulation. A body encoder preserves joint and body-part features across multiple temporal scales, allowing each hand joint to query these features for joint-specific body information. The diffusion generator combines this joint-specific body information with the learned hand context. Both encoders and the generator are trained jointly, so the final generation objective also shapes the context features. Experiments on the OakInk2 dataset show that ContextHands generates more accurate hand motion and achieves lower Fréchet Hand Distance than existing hand-motion and general human-motion generation methods adapted to the task. In qualitative examples of grasping and tool use, ContextHands more closely reproduces the detailed hand poses in the reference motion. We will release our code and pretrained models to support further research.


### arXiv 2611.33913 - PerovAgent: Autonomous Ligand Design for Halide Perovskite Nanocrystals

- categories: cond-mat.mtrl-sci
- published: 2026-11-25T07:33:00Z
- authors: Seungjun Cha, Yoo Jae Jeong, Amanda Conde Del Moral, Juan-Pablo Correa-Baena, Seth R. Marder, David S. Ginger
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33913

Designing surface-capping ligands for high-performance halide perovskite nanocrystals requires navigating a vast molecular space while discovering design rules that govern ligand-surface interactions. Here, we introduce PerovAgent, an autonomous multi-agent framework that combines large language model (LLM)-driven scientific reasoning with computational tools for atomistic model construction, machine-learned interatomic potential evaluation, and statistical validation. Across ten independent campaigns on CsPbBr3 surfaces, PerovAgent adaptively explored and refined diverse ligand chemistries while progressively improving the binding-energy objective. Beyond optimization, the framework converts recurring chemical observations into explicit design principles that can be statistically evaluated. Of 35 formulated principles, 19 were prospectively supported, of which seven also passed the confound audit, highlighting the need to systematically validate plausible LLM-generated hypotheses during autonomous discovery. A recurrent alkynyl-phosphonate principle was further examined using density functional theory, which reproduced the predicted energetic preference. These results demonstrate an autonomous discovery framework that not only searches molecular space and optimizes the scalar objective but also transforms its search trajectory into systematically testable design principles. The framework can also be extended to more complex ligands, including zwitterionic, multidentate, and polymerizable ligands, to explore a wider range of ligand-surface interactions and uncover new design principles for halide perovskite nanocrystals.


### arXiv 2611.33808 - EvoVAU: Tool Evolution for Video Anomaly Understanding

- categories: cs.CV
- published: 2026-11-25T05:38:46Z
- authors: Benfeng Wang, Chao Huang, Yudong Cheng, Chengliang Liu, Wei Wang, Wenqi Ren
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33808

Video anomaly understanding (VAU) requires reasoning about event progression, interactions, and the contextual conditions that distinguish abnormal behavior from common activity across diverse scenes. Although video agents enable multi-round analysis, the predefined analytical operations may not adequately satisfy these diverse reasoning demands. In this paper, we present EvoVAU, a framework that evolves a reusable tool library for a frozen video solver. Guided by task outcomes and execution trajectories, an external proposer identifies potential limitations in the library and refines it, retaining task experience as analytical operations that can be reused across questions. Specifically, Cross-Version Contrastive Feedback guides tool refinement by combining current errors with paired execution trajectories from historical corrections and regressions. Adoption-Aware Selection accepts a candidate library only when overall development-set accuracy improves and corrections outnumber regressions among questions on which the solver invokes new or modified tools. Using a small development set, EvoVAU alternates tool refinement with candidate evaluation and selection. At test time, the evolved library remains fixed, with no further involvement from the proposer. Experiments on several VAU benchmarks demonstrate strong performance and the flexibility of the evolved library across anomaly domains.


### arXiv 2611.33706 - EVA: Evidence-seeking Visual Agent for Hallucination-Resistant Multimodal Reasoning

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T03:19:42Z
- authors: Ting Lei, Yu Chen, Yang Liu
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33706

Hallucinations in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) often arise from visual assumptions that go unchecked. Self-reflection, multi-agent debate, and counterfactual verification can reinforce erroneous conclusions when they rely on the same unverified visual assumptions. External visual tools can provide verifiable evidence, but models may select the wrong tools, supply incorrect parameters, or ignore or contradict the evidence they obtain. In this work, we propose EVA, an Evidence-seeking Visual Agent that reframes multimodal reasoning as an explicit process of evidence acquisition and verification. At the core of EVA is a unified evidence-grounded execution pipeline designed to address the above challenges through two tightly coupled mechanisms. First, we introduce Iterative Tool Replanning, which dynamically refines tool selection and parameters based on intermediate observations, improving the reliability of tool usage. Second, we develop Evidence Consistency Verification, an answer validation mechanism that improves alignment between the final prediction and the collected evidence, mitigating evidence--reasoning inconsistencies. In addition, to balance robustness and efficiency, EVA incorporates Selective Evidence Routing, an adaptive fast--slow reasoning strategy that determines when external evidence is necessary. Visually straightforward queries are handled via direct reasoning, while complex or high-risk cases trigger tool-based evidence acquisition. By jointly addressing how to reliably acquire evidence and how to use it consistently, EVA transforms multimodal reasoning from passive answer validation into explicit evidence-backed decision making. Experimental results demonstrate that EVA achieves significantly improved reasoning reliability over direct VLM inference and multi-agent methods, while maintaining efficiency through selective tool invocation.


### arXiv 2611.33676 - Interactive Proofs as a Unified Framework for Evaluating and Leveraging Verifiable Proof Generation by LLM Agents

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T02:42:59Z
- authors: Yankai Shu, Xingyu Zhao, Chuan Mao, Congchi Yin
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33676

Recent advances in LLM-assisted theorem proving and program verification have attracted widespread attention. Beyond determining whether a proposition is true, LLM agents must produce evidence that can be independently checked by humans or formal verification tools. To evaluate the underlying, protocol-agnostic ability of LLM agents to construct verifiable proofs-rather than their proficiency with any particular proof language or verification tool, we introduce Merlin-Bench. Drawing on interactive proof systems from computational complexity theory, we cast an LLM agent as the prover: under a designated protocol, the agent interacts with a formal verifier to substantiate its claimed answer to a problem instance. We develop an automated framework for constructing test cases and use it to create 100 cases across 15 problem families, each formulated within this prover-verifier framework. Experiments with 10 frontier LLMs across multiple agent harnesses show that reliably producing verifiable proofs in accordance with specified protocols remains a substantial challenge. Our case studies further provide actionable insights into improving agents' ability to generate verifier-accepted proofs: tool use is essential for completing practical proof tasks; different forms of guidance, ranging from engineering instructions to problem-specific knowledge, substantially affect success rates on interactive-proof tasks; and proof-solving experience distilled from abstract tasks can transfer to concrete, real-world verification tasks.


### arXiv 2611.33648 - PRIOR: Pre-Call Reasoning in SFT and Its Influence on Tool-Use Retention under RL

- categories: cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-25T02:14:13Z
- authors: Kaiwen Hu, Hyunin Lee, Joseph Suh, Serina Chang, Shuai Shao, Yong Zhang
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33648

Tool use learned during supervised fine-tuning (SFT) is not always retained during reinforcement learning (RL). We study how properties of SFT data may affect this retention by constructing Low, Medium, and High SFT datasets with approximately matched token budgets but different tool-call depths. Under the same RL recipe, the three initializations reach similar RL training accuracy yet exhibit markedly different tool-use dynamics: tool initiation increases in Low, declines in Medium, and collapses in High. We observe that these datasets also differ substantially in the amount of reasoning preceding each tool call. In particular, High trajectories contain considerably less pre-call reasoning, motivating the hypothesis that pre-call reasoning may affect how well tool use is calibrated and, consequently, whether the corresponding behaviors are retained during RL. An idealized theoretical analysis provides a selective-retention view of this process: advantages in overall and tool-conditioned accuracy can be preserved under outcome-only optimization, while tool initiation increases only when tool-using trajectories are more successful than the tool-free alternative. Guided by this analysis, we rank the original High trajectories by average pre-call reasoning length and retain the highest-ranked examples while approximately matching cumulative SFT token exposure. This simple filtering strategy improves post-RL accuracy and tool-conditioned final-answer success without consistently increasing tool initiation across multiple benchmarks. These results suggest that pre-call reasoning is a useful dimension of SFT supervision for understanding and improving tool-use retention under downstream RL.


### arXiv 2611.33612 - Failure Broadens, Success Sharpens: Continual Adaptation of Tool-Use Agents Across Diverse Tasks with Dual-Polarity Memory

- categories: cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-25T01:38:15Z
- authors: Tianmai M. Zhang, Hoda Shajari, Amr Hendy, Xiaohu Liu, Piyush Behre, Omar Zia Khan
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33612

Large language model agents can self-improve over time by utilizing procedural memory derived from prior task trajectories, yet the distinct utilities of memories derived from local successes and failures remain underexplored. This work studies this question in a realistic setting with heterogeneous streams of multi-domain tool-use tasks and various environment constraints common in real-world scenarios. We develop \emph{Dual-Polarity Memory with Periodic Maintenance} (DualPM), a simple, retrieval-free memory workflow characterized by (1) customizable reflection and procedural memory generation from both local successes and failures within each trajectory, and (2) periodic memory consolidation over long task streams under a given memory capacity, preventing memory explosion. Online prequential evaluation with fine-grained metrics reveals that failure memory more strongly broadens coverage, success memory better streamlines execution, while combining both polarities effectively integrates both benefits. Further studies demonstrate that DualPM enables cross-domain memory generalization and continual memory adaptation, while external evaluation on EvoMemBench reveals the portability and effectiveness of DualPM as a general long-term procedural memory method.


### arXiv 2611.33563 - FIG-MAC: A Fine-grained Inspiration Graph Empowered Multi-Agent Collaboration for Automated Scientific Hypothesis Generation

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-25T00:25:29Z
- authors: Siyuan Chen, Yuping Gao, Tai Huang, Yuling Zhou, Dongyu Su, Tongguan Wang
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33563

Automated Scientific Hypothesis Generation (ASHG) leveraging large language models (LLMs) aims to harness their strengths in knowledge integration, logical reasoning, and text generation for efficiently producing innovative, verifiable scientific hypotheses. However, existing approaches face three critical limitations: (i) insufficient fine-grained knowledge representation, as unstructured context impairs LLM comprehension and reasoning; (ii) lack of knowledge evolution traceability, constraining the generation of innovative cross-domain conjectures; (iii) absence of human-inspired and role-specialized multi-agent collaboration, failing to leverage the efficient innovation mechanisms arising from cognitive diversity and division of labor among expert roles to complete the full ASHG-evaluation loop. To address these limitations, we propose the Fine-grained Inspiration Graph-empowered Multi-Agent Collaboration framework (FIG-MAC) including: (i) Fine-grained Inspiration Graph module (FIG), to obtain fine-grained structured knowledge representations, where nodes represent academic semantic units and edges encode heuristic relationships among them; (ii) ontology-guided hybrid reasoning module, which follows the FIG ontology to construct structured reasoning paths and uses vector search to enrich them with semantic evidence, to obtain the traceable scientific conjecture paths; (iii) role-specialized Multi-Agent framework, inspired by real scientific teamwork, enabling a closed-loop process of scientific conjecture generation, screening, and validation. Experimental results demonstrate that FIG-MAC significantly outperforms state-of-the-art methods on source diversity, provenance-adjusted novelty, and raw novelty.


### arXiv 2611.33453 - SecID: An LLM-Guided Framework for Threat-Aware Secondary Asset Identification with Quantitative RTL Analysis

- categories: cs.CR, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T21:20:28Z
- authors: Md Ajoad Hasan, Dipayan Saha, Upoma Das, Sujan Kumar Saha, Mark Tehranipoor, Farimah Farahmandi
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33453

The growing complexity of modern hardware designs is making security assurance increasingly difficult. One of the foundational steps in pre-silicon security verification is identifying security assets. While primary assets are often evident from design functionality, the internal signals that can facilitate their compromise are much less apparent. Complex data- and control-flow dependencies create many such internal security-critical locations, whose exploitation depends on the threat-specific attack mechanism---these are the secondary assets. To precisely identify these, we propose SecID, a multi-agent framework that combines deterministic RTL analysis with bounded dynamic evaluation and LLM reasoning to identify threat-specific secondary assets. Each asset decision is supported by functional influence, threat features, and explicit attack mechanisms. We demonstrate SecID on seven open-source SoC and IP designs, including openMSP430, NEORV32, and CV32E40P. The experimental results are corroborated through comparison with prior work, fault simulation, and formal analysis, and further evaluated through pipeline- and final-stage ablation, threshold-sensitivity studies, and final-stage repeatability analysis. Together, these evaluations show that SecID can identify highly threat-relevant secondary assets with respect to a given primary asset, providing a scalable path toward hardware-security verification.


### arXiv 2611.33407 - An Adversarial Multi-Agent Pursuit Game for Locomotor Agility Pretraining

- categories: cs.RO, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-24T20:33:56Z
- authors: Nathan A. C. Hogg, Matt Hyatt, Neil Klingensmith, George K. Thiruvathukal
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33407

Agile humanoid locomotion requires controllers that can adapt to rapidly changing motion demands while remaining stable and robust. Recent locomotion controllers are typically designed with either a dense reward that is carefully shaped to encourage steps and penalize falling, or by using a reference tracking reward which teaches the controller to follow predetermined motions. Once a behavior that maximizes the reward is discovered, the controller ceases to learn. While generally stable, reference tracking methods focus on motor control separately from motion generation. Self-play has been proven as an effective technique for superhuman performance on several multiagent games like Go, Starcraft, hide-and-seek, and capture-the-flag. We train neural networks to play the game of multiagent tag in a physics simulator to study if new motion generation and control strategies can emerge through sparse feedback and competitive self-play. We present MJTag, a task suite for role-conditioned locomotion during competitive pursuit-evasion. Our model is a role-conditioned policy for a low-level locomotion controller and optional planar velocity commands. MJTag's league-based play with sparse feedback enables a locomotion controller to adopt new strategies, including dodging, sprinting, and tackling.

- 1.0.6 @ 2026-11-18T08:06:30Z :: ## What's Changed ### v1.x security fixes 1.0.6 is a security release for the deprecated v1 line. Upgrade with `npx @modelcontextprotocol/inspector@v1-latest`, or move to v2 (`@latest`). * **A malicious MCP server can no longer crash the proxy by streaming an unbounded server-sent event.** The proxy now caps how much of an upstream SSE stream it will buffer. A single line, or a single event, longer than 16 MiB closes that upstream connection with an error instead of growing the proxy's heap until it runs out of memory and drops every session. This covers the `sse` and `streamable-http` transports, including a standalone GET stream with the wrong content-type. The cap sits well above any legitimate message (four times the SDK reference servers' 4 MB inbound limit). ([GHSA-3xgv-mvj6-g78r](https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/inspector/security/advisories/GHSA-3xgv-mvj6-g78r)) by @cliffh

### anthropics-anthropic-sdk-python

- v2.14.0 @ 2026-11-24T21:00:57Z :: ### Features * **api:** add Google-managed data controls for Google Cloud projects * **api:** add Google-managed data controls for user profiles * **api:** add support for compliance review cases, notices, and items ### Bug Fixes * **client:** honor proxy and trust_env when proxy variables are set `DefaultHttpxClient` and `DefaultAsyncHttpxClient` read `HTTP_PROXY`, `HTTPS_PROXY`, `ALL_PROXY` and `NO_PROXY` even when the caller passed `proxy=` or `trust_env=False`, and the variables won. Now `proxy=` is used for every request and the variables are not read, and `trust_env=False` ignores them, as in plain `httpx2`. What you may notice: with `proxy=` set, a host listed in `NO_PROXY` used to be reached directly and now goes through your proxy. To keep a host off the proxy, pass `mounts={"all://that.host": None}`. ### Chores * **docs:** drop the provisional naming note from the Google Cloud 

- v2.13.0 @ 2026-11-23T18:34:26Z :: ### Features * **api:** add an error code to Message Batches creation * **api:** add External Storage to the Admin API beta * **api:** add webhook events for external storage failure and recovery ### Bug Fixes * **client:** build the right class for tool results with unknown values ### Chores * **api:** mark Claude Opus 4.5 as deprecated (end-of-life January 22, 2027) * **client:** read deprecated models from generated data and stop warning on retired ones * **docs:** clarify when plugin archive downloads are recorded and who gave a share * **docs:** correct organization-type notes in usage and cost reports * **docs:** describe the user profile endpoints and link to the guide * **docs:** use realistic example values in more request bodies ### Documentation * **api:** shorten the human_review description

- v2.12.0 @ 2026-11-20T15:56:21Z :: ### Features * **api:** add the image_dimensions_too_large error code ### Bug Fixes * **bedrock:** send the user profile ID in the request body Amazon Bedrock reads `user_profile_id` from the request body and ignores the `anthropic-user-profile-id` header, so a profile ID given to `AnthropicBedrock` or `AsyncAnthropicBedrock` was not applied. Both clients now move it into the body of Messages requests and no longer send the header. A `user_profile_id` already in the body takes priority over the header. Some models on Bedrock do not support user profiles. A request to one of them with a profile ID now fails with a 400 error; before, the ID was ignored. Leave the ID off requests to those models. ### Chores * **docs:** clarify billing_policy in batches and two billing errors

## The Decoder - After OpenAI and Anthropic shut the door on their data, a Chinese AI developer is turning to Google and Meta instead (2026-11-26)

After OpenAI and Anthropic shut the door on their data, a Chinese AI developer is turning to Google and Meta instead Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe See plans DE Switch to German Open menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Open menu See plans Sign In Subscribe Now The Decoder Discord LinkedIn Maximilian Schreiner View the LinkedIn Profile of Maximilian Schreiner Nov 26, 2026 Short News After OpenAI and Anthropic shut the door on their data, a Chinese AI developer is turning to Google and Meta instead Chinese AI companies Z.AI and Moonshot AI have to delay their next flagship models and are looking for new sources of training data. The effects now reach beyond previously known delays , after the US closed the cloud loophole for Nvidia chips , Nvidia halted shipments to Southeast Asia , and US companies cracked down on model distillation. As a result, Z.AI's roughly 3-trillion-parameter GLM-5.5 model, planned for November, is likely to slip into the first quarter of 2027, Reuters reports, citing sources. To bridge the gap, the company released the 750-billion-parameter GLM-5.4 on Monday and says a new design lets it keep up with systems that have 2 to 3 trillion parameters. Without foreign computing power, Moonshot AI's development cycle could double from six to 12 months, likely delaying its next major model, Kimi K4, until mid-2027. With OpenAI and Anthropic blocking data scraping for distillation , Moonshot is now turning to the latest models from Google and Meta for training and stepping up its search for compute capacity in Europe, according to the sources. FCC clears SpaceX to deploy up to 1 million Starmind satellites for orbital AI data centers by 2040 Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe See plans DE Switch to German Open menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Open menu See plans Sign In Subscribe Now The Decoder Discord LinkedIn Maximilian Schreiner View the LinkedIn Profile of Maximilian Schreiner Nov 26, 2026 Plus AI in practice FCC clears SpaceX to deploy up to 1 million Starmind satellites for orbital AI data centers by 2040 Nano Banana Pro prompted by THE DECODER SpaceX has won approval from US communications regulators for its Starmind constellation. Up to 1 million satellites would process AI data in orbit. The Federal Communications Commission (FCC) has authorized SpaceX to deploy up to 1 million satellites for orbital data centers, according to Reuters . The Starmind constellation of orbital compute modules is intended to meet part of the rapidly growing demand for AI computing power on Earth. The agency also approved radio links that let SpaceX monitor and control the satellites if their optical connections fail. <!-- Like KI PRO: the box follows the text, a white gradient above it covers the end of the blurred rest.
