## The Decoder - Microsoft details how state-backed hackers use the ARTEX AI framework to automate cyberattacks (2026-11-26)

Microsoft details how state-backed hackers use the ARTEX AI framework to automate cyberattacks Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe See plans DE Switch to German Open menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Open menu See plans Sign In Subscribe Now The Decoder Discord LinkedIn Maximilian Schreiner View the LinkedIn Profile of Maximilian Schreiner Nov 26, 2026 AI and society Microsoft details how state-backed hackers use the ARTEX AI framework to automate cyberattacks Nano Banana Pro prompted by THE DECODER Ask AI Easy Perspectives Terms Own question Key Points According to a Microsoft analysis, state-backed hackers and criminal groups are increasingly using the AI framework ARTEX to run fully automated cyberattacks through autonomous agents and existing tools like Metasploit. The system distributes attack tasks on its own, switches between language models when something fails, and operates at machine speed, often leaving defenders with just minutes to respond. To counter these adaptive agents, Microsoft recommends targeted deception tactics like decoy systems and faked success signals ("fake wins") that trick the AI into calling off attacks on its own. The AI framework ARTEX automates cyberattacks from reconnaissance to data theft. A Microsoft analysis shows how state-backed groups and cybercriminals use its specialized agents at machine speed, and how defenders can respond. Since its release in July 2026, the AI-powered penetration-testing framework ARTEX has become a go-to tool for cyberattacks. The software was recently suspected of being used in an intrusion into an analytics system run by the CIA, NGA, and ODNI (Link nicht geprüft). That breach surfaced shortly before US financial crimes agency FinCEN reported a security incident. The FBI and the US Department of Agriculture had reported incidents earlier. A report from Microsoft Threat Intelligence now details how ARTEX works and how widely it has spread. GitHub user "Autumn-27" published the source code in July and deleted it in October, though other developers still distribute copies. Chinese security firm ThreatBook also sells a commercial Pro version. Microsoft says state-backed groups from China and North Korea have integrated ARTEX into their operations, including Salt Typhoon, Crystal Typhoon, Onyx Sleet, and Storm-0940 . Financially motivated cybercriminals also use the system, the company says, with both state-backed and criminal groups deploying it in operations such as supply chain attacks. Ad Specialized AI agents run existing security tools with minimal oversight Microsoft points out that ARTEX doesn't invent new attack techniques. Instead, the framework operates existing security software with almost no human intervention, including tools such as Nmap, Sqlmap, Metasploit, and automated headless browsers. Attackers can connect any large language model, including modified versions without safety guardrails. A built-in pooling feature automatically switches models or API keys on errors or usage limits, making attacks hard for any single AI provider to track. Ad ARTEX's architecture splits tasks across specialized AI agents. | Image: Microsoft According to Microsoft, the system is built on a multi-agent architecture. A goal model breaks an attacker's instructions into verifiable subgoals. From there, a planning agent continuously reviews a knowledge graph, a dynamic map of discovered systems and vulnerabilities, to assign short attack tasks. Worker agents carry out those tasks via system commands, including deploying web shells on web servers, reading password files, or hijacking cloud accounts. Each new finding feeds back into the map and prompts the planning agent for the next step until a reporting agent documents all exploited flaws. Machine-speed attacks push Microsoft to recommend active deception Automation cuts defender response times to just a few minutes, the company found. In one case, an AI agent took just five minutes to search for accidentally exposed credentials on GitHub and pull data from Azure Key Vault, a cloud key store. During a Storm-0940 campaign, the company logged more than 151,000 failed login attempts from automated password guessing, peaking at 2,900 per minute. In that same campaign, Microsoft observed some compromised apps granting themselves top administrator privileges. Ad Conventional rule-based filters often fail against ARTEX because they rely on fixed markers like known IP addresses or the browser identifiers "artex-enrich" and "norma." Agents and their human operators constantly change those markers and reroute blocked traffic through rotating VPNs, proxy servers, or Tor. Even when security teams spot and isolate a breach, that is often not enough. Because ARTEX usually runs two to three attack chains simultaneously, its agents adapt immediately and shift to new network paths at machine speed. The Decoder newsletter The AI week in five minutes Every week, our editors pick the AI news that matters. No hype, no spam. Enter your email Sign up free Free and weekly. Unsubscribe any time. ARTEX still gives itself away with huge bursts of traffic, because the framework doesn't hide its activity by default. Microsoft recommends deception technologies tailored to how AI agents operate, alongside strict access controls, rate limits, and automated real-time isolation for compromised devices. Ad Honeypots present autonomous agents with attractive but worthless decoy systems to pull traffic away from real targets. Artificial delay loops, known as tarpits, slow down requests and drain an agent's built-in time and compute budget. Fake wins, Microsoft says, are especially effective because ARTEX's goal model looks for verifiable proof of a breach. When a trap returns convincing fake success messages, the AI considers its task complete and stops the attack by itself. Ad Decoy credentials, called honeytokens, trigger earl


## The Decoder - Nvidia halts server shipments to Malaysia, Singapore, Thailand and Indonesia after tighter US export controls (2026-11-26)

Nvidia halts server shipments to Malaysia, Singapore, Thailand, and Indonesia after tighter US export controls Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe See plans DE Switch to German Open menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Open menu See plans Sign In Subscribe Now The Decoder Discord LinkedIn Maximilian Schreiner View the LinkedIn Profile of Maximilian Schreiner Nov 26, 2026 AI and society Nvidia halts server shipments to Malaysia, Singapore, Thailand, and Indonesia after tighter US export controls GPT-Image-2.6 prompted by THE DECODER Ask AI Easy Perspectives Terms Own question Key Points The Financial Times reported that Nvidia told partners including Dell and Supermicro to halt server shipments to Malaysia, Singapore, Thailand, and Indonesia. New US export controls are designed to stop Chinese companies from bypassing sales bans through foreign cloud providers. Dealers have listed an unusually large number of Nvidia GPUs for sale as a result of the rules, CNBC reported. The supply includes thousands of B300 chips originally intended for data centers with Chinese rental customers. Cloud providers in Southeast Asia now have 90 days to apply for a license, a requirement one executive said has led to "chaos." Banks have already halted about $500 million in loans to Singapore-based provider Aolani for a planned deal with ByteDance, the Financial Times reported. The US is closing cloud loopholes that give Chinese companies access to AI chips. Nvidia is responding by halting shipments to four countries, while thousands of high-performance processors are looking for new buyers on the secondary market. Nvidia has told manufacturing partners such as Dell and Supermicro to stop shipping servers to Malaysia, Singapore, Thailand, and Indonesia. The Financial Times reported the halt, citing three people familiar with the matter. It isn't clear whether the pause is only temporary, according to the report. The move follows tighter US export controls from the Commerce Department's Bureau of Industry and Security. The rules aim to stop Chinese companies from getting around existing sales bans by renting computing power from foreign cloud providers. US Trade Representative Jamieson Greer told CNBC the goal was to block indirect access, including smuggling through Southeast Asia and remote access via data centers in third countries. Ad Thousands of B300 chips hit the market as cloud providers face chaos The controls are already affecting the hardware market, CNBC reported. Since the announcement, dealers have listed an unusually large number of Nvidia GPUs for sale. Jack Duhamel, president of the AI infrastructure marketplace GPU Mart, and two other insiders confirmed to CNBC that the surge is a direct result of the new rule. According to the report, sellers are looking for new buyers for hardware, including thousands of B300 chips, one of Nvidia's most advanced models. Those chips were originally intended for data centers with Chinese rental customers. Ad The rollout has created significant uncertainty in Southeast Asia, the Financial Times reported. Cloud providers have 90 days to apply for a license and prove that no restricted companies are among their end users. One cloud provider executive described the situation to the paper as chaos, saying there was no guidance on how to apply. The fallout is also hitting major infrastructure projects. Banks have halted about $500 million in loans to Singapore-based cloud provider Aolani, which planned to rent computing power to TikTok parent ByteDance, the Financial Times reported. ByteDance could now pull out of the deal. ## The Decoder - US chip-access curbs and anti-distillation measures are reportedly already slowing Chinese AI labs (2026-11-25)

US chip-access curbs and anti-distillation measures are reportedly already slowing Chinese AI labs Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe See plans DE Switch to German Open menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Open menu See plans Sign In Subscribe Now The Decoder Discord LinkedIn Matthias Bastian View the LinkedIn Profile of Matthias Bastian Nov 25, 2026 Short News US chip-access curbs and anti-distillation measures are reportedly already slowing Chinese AI labs The US is tightening its grip in the AI race with China, and the push is already having an effect, according to The Information. Chinese AI developers including Deepseek, Moonshot AI, and Alibaba have been under pressure since September, squeezed by restrictions on Chinese firms' remote access to advanced Nvidia chips in foreign data centers and by successful anti-distillation measures from OpenAI and Anthropic . As a result, many ongoing projects are facing delays, the report says . Last month, Deepseek researchers internally estimated that their gap to top US models could grow from three to six months to nine months if distillation disappears completely . Moonshot AI has already canceled a $150 million deal for Nvidia Blackwell chips in Indonesia . Chinese firms are now looking for new workarounds, the publication reports. Some are trying to rent computing power through non-Chinese third parties, while companies such as MiniMax, Mihoyo, and Alibaba are leaning more heavily on domestic chips. They are also investing more in training data for their own reinforcement learning. ## The Decoder - Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei says letting AI govern society would be 'a bad idea' (2026-11-25)

Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei says letting AI govern society would be "a bad idea" Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe See plans DE Switch to German Open menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Open menu See plans Sign In Subscribe Now The Decoder Discord LinkedIn Matthias Bastian View the LinkedIn Profile of Matthias Bastian Nov 25, 2026 AI and society Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei says letting AI govern society would be "a bad idea" Nano Banana Pro prompted by THE DECODER Ask AI Easy Perspectives Terms Own question Dario Amodei said it was "absurd" to suggest he believed people should "surrender human free will" to AI systems. In a lengthy post on X, he responded to an Axios report that cited people familiar with internal discussions. The outlet reported that some Anthropic employees believed humanity would safely hand authority to superintelligent systems, and often should, rather than fight for control. AI must be governed by "legitimate democratic processes," Amodei wrote . Political decisions should stay in human hands, he argued, and he rejected handing power to AI as firmly as he rejected autocracy. Ad [...] We should never sacrifice legitimacy or self-determination on the altar of efficiency or security. This argument against autocracy applies word for word to governance by AI, and I feel just as strongly that handing over governance to AI is a bad idea. Ad Dario Amodei Automating specific tasks such as driving, developing cancer drugs, and writing software could benefit humanity, Amodei said, but that was a different matter from giving up free will in politics. Amodei says that full automation isn't always worth the risk End-to-end automation could also bring risks, Amodei wrote, including serious job disruption and AI building new models without human oversight. "It is clearly desirable in many cases and will save a lot of lives," he wrote. "But it has downsides as well, and in some areas it may not be worth it." In many cases, he added, "humans would simply prefer to do some activities themselves, so they can stay deeply engaged in what matters to them, even if AI can do those activities better." Ad Amodei said AI companies didn't differ much on automating specific tasks. Some AI CEOs, he wrote, repeated slogans like "human control and agency" and "AI should be just a tool" but still shipped products built for more and more automation. Anthropic, by contrast, refused to allow its existing models to be used for autonomous weapons during its dispute with the Pentagon . He was "willing to risk Anthropic's future as a company" over that refusal, "even in the face of legal sanctions," Amodei wrote. ## The Decoder - OpenAI wants companies to trust it with sensitive work by promising not to look at their data (2026-11-25)

OpenAI wants companies to trust it with sensitive work by promising not to look at their data Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe See plans DE Switch to German Open menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Open menu See plans Sign In Subscribe Now The Decoder Discord LinkedIn Jonathan Kemper View the LinkedIn Profile of Jonathan Kemper Nov 25, 2026 Short News OpenAI wants companies to trust it with sensitive work by promising not to look at their data OpenAI is testing a new feature called "Private Inference" that lets companies run queries on its most powerful models without OpenAI being able to access the data. CEO Sam Altman said the company is now piloting the previously announced offering with its first enterprise customers. Better privacy makes AI useful for a much wider range of work, Altman wrote. Shekar, the OpenAI employee leading the effort, called it a first step toward "private intelligence for everyone." OpenAI is currently scaling up the compute capacity needed to expand the offering. For now, Private Inference targets business customers, whose data OpenAI already doesn't use for training . Consumer ChatGPT conversations, by contrast, are used for training by default unless users opt out. With Private Inference, queries are processed on specially isolated hardware whose security can be independently verified, according to OpenAI . Altman also pointed to Private Safety Processing , a system that lets OpenAI scan for abuse without storing customer data. Flagged content is encrypted and stored in the customer's own cloud, and only a secured review system can read it. Google is pursuing a similar approach with Private AI Compute . ## The Decoder - Amazon and Meta are in talks to end the block on Meta's AI shopping agent Muse in Amazon's online store (2026-11-25)

Amazon and Meta are in talks to end the block on Meta's AI shopping agent Muse in Amazon's online store. Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe See plans DE Switch to German Open menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Open menu See plans Sign In Subscribe Now The Decoder Discord LinkedIn Maximilian Schreiner View the LinkedIn Profile of Maximilian Schreiner Nov 25, 2026 Short News Amazon and Meta are in talks to end the block on Meta's AI shopping agent Muse in Amazon's online store. Amazon and Meta are in talks to end the block on Meta's AI shopping agent Muse in Amazon's online store. In September, Amazon blocked Muse's access . Ad revenue and control over search results are the main sticking points in the ongoing talks, Fortune reports . Amazon worries that shopping agents like Muse could weaken its relationship with customers and bypass the ads on its platform, a business that brings in $68.6 billion a year. Andy Jassy, Amazon's CEO, said shopping through third-party agents so far hasn't been more convenient or better than going directly to a retailer, but he didn't rule out opening access. The company also offers its own agentic shopping features, which in some cases have pulled in sellers that don't sell on Amazon . It previously tried and failed to block Perplexity . Any deal would need sign-off from both CEOs, according to Fortune. Muse is Meta's most successful consumer AI product so far, with more than 10 million users in the US and Canada. Meta is already seeing gains in its Instagram ad business because Muse helps users organize saved products, which boosts conversion rates for advertisers, CNBC reports . Data from Forter Intelligence shows that Muse recently accounted for 51 percent of all agentic orders, followed by SpaceX's Grok at 24 percent and OpenAI's ChatGPT at 22 percent. Update on a security incident involving 1Password’s backend systems. | 1Password Skip to Main Content span]:text-wrap group/nav-item focus-visible:outline-offset-[4px]" href="/personal-ai-agent-integrations"> 1Password for Agents: Give AI Agents access, not your passwords. Customer vaults were not affected and all vault contents remain secure. by 1Password November 25, 2026 - 4 min Related Categories Security The short version li:last-child>p]:mb-0 [&>li>ul>li:first-child>p]:mt-6 [&>li>ul]:list-[circle] [&>li>ul]:pt-0 article-last-before-heading"> An attacker gained access to some of our backend systems and copied a limited amount of customer account information from a subset of our customers, including the names and email addresses that customers use to sign into 1Password. Your vault data, the items in them, and the keys that protect them are safe and were not affected. 1Password does not have access to your password or secret keys. We implemented containment measures, restored clean versions of the affected service, and have triggered a rotation of internal credentials. You do not need to update anything in your 1Password account. We recommend being cautious of phishing attempts in the form of unexpected emails about 1Password, such as invitations or account recovery requests that you did not initiate. 1Password apps and services are working normally. What happened On November 19, 2026, 1Password began investigating indications that an attacker gained access to an employee’s corporate GitHub account. The actor used the account to access source code and push malicious code to backend services, which downloaded administrative data pertaining to our customers. This data included customer names and email addresses, in addition to non-secret internal metadata about the account. At no time were customer vault contents accessed by, or available to, the attacker. What information was affected The attacker downloaded account records for a subset of our users. These records include: li:last-child>p]:mb-0 [&>li>ul>li:first-child>p]:mt-6 [&>li>ul]:list-[circle] [&>li>ul]:pt-0 article-last-before-heading"> Names and email addresses Account and user identifiers, and account creation and last sign-in times Public keys (which are public by design, this has no impact to user security) Secure Remote Password protocol parameters (which cannot be used to access your account) Tokens present in certain emailed links for invitations, provisioning and account recovery Other non-secret account metadata relative to our internal architecture (e.g. version numeric identifiers, file numeric identifiers) The attacker also copied source code from private repositories and gained access to our CI/CD system. Our source code is not customer data, and contains no user secrets. CI/CD contains infrastructure related secrets in order for our product to work. We have triggered a rotation of all credentials which may have been exposed, and there is no impact to 1Password’s services, availability, or product security. When we send an email to a customer to accept a provisioning invite to an account, or to accept recovery of their account, it contains a token. A provisioning invite is specifically used for our SCIM Bridge or Hosted Provisioning . During this incident there were a small number of users in this state, who have been specifically notified. This token is used to complete the invitation or recovery process, but only when combined with an SSO sign-in or a six digit code sent to the user's registered email address. The token is not able to get access to the account by itself. In order to reduce the ability for a malicious third party to successfully guess the code, we’ve expanded the code from six numeric digits to ten alphanumeric digits. This makes brute force guessing practically impossible. What was not affected li:last-child>p]:mb-0 [&>li>ul>li:first-child>p]:mt-6 [&>li>ul]:list-[circle] [&>li>ul]:pt-0 article-last-before-heading"> Your vaults and the items in them The keys that protect your vaults li:last-child>p]:mb-0 [&>li>ul>li:first-child>p]:mt-6 [&>li>ul]:list-[circle] [&>li>ul]:pt-0 article-last-before-heading"> 1Password does not store your account password or secret key Account recovery keys 1Password apps and

## Scott Aaronson - 'Holy shit holy shit holy shit holy shit: The magnitude of what's now happening' (2026-11-26)

Shtetl-Optimized » Blog Archive » HOLY SHIT HOLY SHIT HOLY SHIT HOLY SHIT Shtetl-Optimized The Blog of Scott Aaronson If you take nothing else from this blog: quantum computers won't solve hard problems instantly by just trying all solutions in parallel. Also, please read Zvi Mowshowitz's masterpiece on how to fix K-12 education! --> « I’ve been drinking wine in my kitchen HOLY SHIT HOLY SHIT HOLY SHIT HOLY SHIT I feel like my previous posts (and post titles), and podcasts, and talks, have failed to do full justice to the magnitude of what’s now happening, so I’m trying again. Last night, we had friends and colleagues over for dinner. We were still trying to process OpenAI’s and Anthropic’s proofs of VP ≠ VNP —hot on the heels, obviously, of the Alpöge/Anthropic proof of the Riemann Hypothesis , which already feels like last week’s news. But then before dessert was served, we saw that OpenAI claims to prove that ω, the exponent of matrix multiplication, is exactly 9/4 —a lower bound (!!) , matching the upper bound that OpenAI proved in the great Mathocalypse drop of October 6. There are two separate shocks here. The first shock is that we now, finally, know the right answer to this central mystery of algorithms going back to the late 1960s, when Volker Strassen first showed that ω (he got ω≤log 2 7) … and that the true answer is actually a rational number (!). But the even bigger shock is that the AI was able to prove any lower bound of the form ω>2. I never expected this challenge to be all that much easier than P ≠ NP. Over the decades, I often heard colleagues express extreme confidence that ω=2, and I expressed skepticism whenever this came up—not because I had any strong argument for ω>2, but just because I saw that there was no strong argument for ω=2 either. So, I’m glad to have been vindicated about that ! But wait, there was more. During dessert, we saw another OpenAI release proving that the true number of linear gates needed to compute the Fourier Transform is N (log N) 1/2+o(1) . The upper bound here improves, not only over the O(N log N) that stood since the 1960s (i.e., the Fast Fourier Transform or FFT), but also over the O(N log 0.9999999999999 N) (give or take some 9’s) that OpenAI published back in the October 6 Mathocalypse. Again, though, the real shock here is that there’s also a matching lower bound (!!) . All these questions from the ancient days are now just getting … straight-up answered , rather than slowly illuminated with a drip-drip-drip of clues. To give an idea of how much things have changed, the other day Yupan Liu put a preprint on the arXiv proving that QIP(2) = PSPACE —that is, quantum interactive protocols with only two messages have the full power of those with three, or any polynomial number. As recently as 2025, this would’ve considered been one of the top complexity results of the year, and the fact that Yupan had used AI to help achieve it would’ve been strange and wonderful. These days, I had to remind myself it had even happened, and the use of AI was so standard that it didn’t even raise an eyebrow. It’s now obvious that we’ll be getting, not one-off solutions to this or that complexity question, but a whole new continent of mathematical truth, one that we’d previously only glimpsed through fog. Picture van Leeuwenhoek first training a microscope on pondwater, or Galileo first training his telescope on the stars. That might give you some idea of how our resolution into the complexity-theoretic universe, and the whole universe of mathematical truth beyond it, has now improved. All through last night, as OpenAI and Anthropic uploaded more files to their GitHub repositories—because that’s what they’re doing now, they don’t even bother to issue press releases anymore, they just upload papers—my guests and I sat at the dinner table with phones out and a laptop, wondering which shoe was going to drop next. It was as though the clouds had parted above Mount Sinai, and we were waiting for the next sentence uttered by the Holy One Himself. I now feel in my bones, for the first time ever, that a proof of P ≠ NP is not far. For all I care, despise the AI companies for bulldozing our garden. Or love them for opening a new vista, or anything in between. Just please don’t deny what’s happening. Now that the companies have repeatedly shown that they can back up their claims with Lean certificates, don’t be a raging doofus who seizes on some triviality (e.g., a minor discrepancy between the Lean and informal proofs, or unused sections of the Lean proof) to pretend that dozens of great open problems of math and theoretical computer science are not, in fact, getting solved. Because they are getting solved. It might take us years to understand the solutions, but my working assumption by this point is that any proof that comes directly from OpenAI or Anthropic is correct barring information to the contrary. I can’t possibly put the matter better than one of my friends on Facebook: After years of crackpot “solutions” claiming Millennium Problems are solved, now that we have actual solutions, we have crackpot “refutations” claiming Millennium Problems are not solved. It is really poetic, in a way. Along the way to so many lower bounds that we wondered whether ever we’d live to see, there will also, of course, be further shocking upper bounds: that is, ultimate barriers to lower bounds, also known as “algorithms.” And yes, some of those algorithms will break currently deployed cryptosystems. Because people have been hounding me about it: yes, I’m virtually certain, from multiple reliable sources, that the AI labs are now sitting on one or more bombshells in that area. However, until I either see details or hear directly from someone at a lab, I feel much, much less confident about the specific nature of those bombshells. I do not wish to be responsible for spreading unverified rumors. And I worry that exact statements of what was done may have been mangled in a game of Telephone. I also, crucially, don’t want the cryptography part of the story to overshadow the intellectual enormity of what’s happening. If, in these days of long-prophesied revelation and wonders and terrors, your main worry is your Bitcoin wallet, then go somewhere else for rumors about whether or not ECDSA is broken or whatever. I don’t have what you’re looking for here. Let me be clear: for those of my readers who celebrate, I believe that tomorrow would be a superb Thanksgiving to spend some quality time with your kids and parents and siblings and old friends. Right now—this past week—is very much like I would’ve always imagined the beginning of an AI singularity to look and feel like. I just hope we’ll get lucky, and it will “merely” be a singularity for our little corner of the world that involves hard math problems. At some point at dinner last night, Dana had a sneezing fit. So I said to her, I hope it isn’t a swarm of nanobots that’s come to infect us! But if it is , then I hope it’s the friendly kind, the kind that will usher us all into an AI utopia. Now, of course, another morning has dawned, and we’re still here. May we find a world where we and our children will be happy to live at the bottom of this rollercoaster. May we work to make it so. Updates (Nov. 26): (1) Many commenters thought it was inappropriate and unnecessary of me to warn people to stop being “raging doofuses” who, fingers in their ears and seizing on the flimsiest of pretexts, just flat-out denied the reality of AI solving our longstanding open math problems. Surely no one was doing that?! And then, of course, tons of other people did exactly that. Gary Marcus, to his eternal shame (because he knows better), signal-boosted to his 260,000 Twitter followers Jenny Nielsen’s crackpot claim that the existence of unused sections of Alpöge’s Lean proof of the Riemann hypothesis somehow invalidated the proof—while, in the spirit of Tucker Carlson, never quite endorsing the crackpot claim.


## bjin - go/codegolf benchmark: frontier models vs the Google 2015 code golfers, 32 problems x 8 languages (2026-11-26)

go/codegolf benchmark go/codegolf benchmark Leaderboard Problems About Why Code Golf ☾ go/codegolf benchmark: frontier models vs. Google's 2015 code golfers. 32 problems · 8 languages · 256 cells Loading… This page needs JavaScript. Problems Loading… This page needs JavaScript. About TL;DR: I brought back 32 problems from a code-golf contest we played at Google in 2015 to see how frontier models compare with our old records. Each model gets 30 minutes per problem in each of eight languages to make its solution as short as it can. The leaderboard scores the byte counts, not just whether the code works. Contents Where the problems come from: go/codegolf From a weekly contest to a benchmark The eight languages How models play Known Best and the oracle Scoring Overall and JS/PY/C++ Technical details Thinking effort and the 30-minute limit Why I built this Where the problems come from: go/codegolf In 2015, when I was at Google, I spent a lot of time on an internal site called go/codegolf . There was one problem a week, and we competed to write the shortest program that solved it. Most of the regulars were code-golf enthusiasts. Each problem went through three phases: Phase What happens 1. Candidate Any Googler could propose a problem by posting a statement and a full test suite. Everyone could read the tests and suggest fixes. Votes were called LGTMs ; the candidate with the most LGTMs became the next week's problem. 2. Competition (7 days) You could see your own submissions, but nobody else's. Only your shortest passing solution in each language counted. The per-language scoreboards showed sizes, not code. The problem's author could compete too. 3. Challenge (forever after) After seven days, the rankings froze and every submission became visible to everyone on the site. You could still submit a challenge, but it had to be strictly shorter than both the winning solution and every earlier challenge entry in that language. A passing challenge got its own slot on the scoreboard, with its code visible too. The rule was simple: the shortest program that passed every test case won . Undefined behavior, compiler quirks, non-standard library features, even hardcoding every test case were all allowed. Apart from passing the tests, the program only had to be deterministic . The site supported JavaScript, Python, Go, C++ and Haskell in 2015. Rust was added after I left. From a weekly contest to a benchmark I kept a collection of problems from the 2015 contest and chose 32 of them for this benchmark. I wrote about half under the name @binjin. The Problems page credits the original author of each of the others. I also archived the scoreboards, statements and test cases. For JavaScript, Python, C++ and Haskell, those scoreboards record the shortest solution any Googler submitted , including improvements made during the challenge phase. Those byte counts are the human records used here. The human solutions themselves are never shown on this site. Eleven years later, I wanted to see how frontier models would do against the people I used to golf with. The eight languages Language Why it's here Human record JavaScript Original 2015 language ✓ Python Original 2015 language ✓ C++ Original 2015 language ✓ Haskell Original 2015 language ✓ Ruby A popular golfing language with plenty of ways to save a byte — Raku (Perl 6) Another golfing favorite, with a different programming style and just as much room to explore — Racket A Lisp: what does golfing look like in prefix notation? — GolfScript An esoteric, stack-based golfing language with very little public training data — There are no 2015 human records for the last four languages. I used an oracle run to get a reference size for each cell, though models have beaten those sizes in some cells. Later, I ran the oracle on the original four languages as well, this time starting from the human records. See Known Best and the oracle . Haskell has human records too, but they are much weaker than the other three, so the humans' main score, JS/PY/C++, leaves Haskell out. See Overall and JS/PY/C++ . How models play A cell is one problem in one language. Each gets a fresh session with a 30-minute wall-clock limit . The model receives the statement, the I/O specification, all the test cases and a short guide to the language. For GolfScript, it also gets the full language reference, since the language is small and not widely known. There are two tools, and both work in all eight languages: submit runs a solution against all the test cases in the replicated sandbox. A passing solution is saved only if it is strictly shorter than the model's previous best. Wrong answers, runtime errors, timeouts and compile errors are returned with stderr. Every response includes the current best known size . I want the model to know when someone has done better; without a target, it's easy to decide a solution is short enough and stop. playground runs a program in the same sandbox as submissions, with mostly the same environment: the same compiler or interpreter, compiler options and libraries. It can preload the test cases as inputs and outputs , so the model can try its g on every case, compare the results with the expected outputs, or just examine the data. It's useful for trying a language feature or checking what the interpreter does with an edge case. It also lets the model write search programs: for example, to brute-force a magic number or find the shortest expression for a small formula. Human golfers do this all the time. That's all the model gets. There is no internet, shell or access to other files. For the four original languages, the sandbox reproduces the 2015 environment , down to the compiler or interpreter version, flags and quirks. A trick that worked in the old contest should work here; a feature added since then won't. The full runtime list is in Technical details . Known Best and the oracle Without human records for Ruby, Raku, Racket and GolfScript, I needed another way to set a target for the models and a reference for scoring. For that I run an oracle : a model with internet access, no time or request limits and every JavaScript, Python, C++ and Haskell solution in the benchmark to learn from, including the shortest human solution for each cell and all the models' solutions. The first oracle was GPT-6 Astra. I ran it on the four added languages to set their reference sizes, then on the original four, where it could work directly from the human records and try to improve them. Later I ran the oracle with other models as well. The oracle is unranked ; it only supplies best-known sizes. When it beats every competitor in a cell, the Problems page shows its byte count as Known Best , but never its code. In 126 of the 256 cells, the oracle's solution is shorter than every competitor's. The oracle had much more time and information to work with. Scoring Each cell is scored by comparing its byte count with the best known: score = (best known size ÷ your size)²

README.md README.md boot.asm boot.asm drivers.asm drivers.asm kernel.asm kernel.asm linker.ld linker.ld os_constants.inc os_constants.inc runtime.asm runtime.asm smp.asm smp.asm View all files README.md Outline Phase 2: Bare-Metal Bootable Qwen3-0.6B OS in 24 KB of Pure Assembly A standalone bootable x86-64 operating system that runs Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B directly on hardware or KVM with no Linux, no Windows, no C, no Rust, and no libc . 24,252 bytes stripped static Multiboot 1 kernel ( FAST_Q8=1 , default: .text = 17,143 B, .rodata = 2,772 B, .bss = 2.1 MiB NOBITS , zero dynamic dependencies), or 23,612 bytes ( FAST_Q8=0 , legacy Q8_0xFP32). 74–94 ms cold boot to interactive asm-llm> multi-turn chat with persistent KV-cache across turns, all SMP cores online, and the .asmbin v2 ( 614 MiB FP16 scales) or v1 ( 649 MiB FP32 scales) model bound zero-copy from physical RAM on 2 MiB huge pages. 100% token-exact and byte-for-byte parity with Phase 1 across 1..32 vCPUs. 134.9 tok/s decode on QEMU KVM (AMD EPYC 7642, 16 vCPUs; 23.7 tok/s at 1 vCPU, 118.1 tok/s at 8 vCPUs, 136.6 tok/s peak) and 55.4 tok/s live on a 0 GB disk Proxmox VE VM (Intel Xeon E5-2670 v3, 12 cores). Follow Follow See All Tech The New York Times warns photographers not to update Photoshop over AI features An email sent to freelance photographers says Photoshop’s latest update uses AI ‘in ways that may not be apparent.’ An email sent to freelance photographers says Photoshop’s latest update uses AI ‘in ways that may not be apparent.’ by Emma Roth Close Emma Roth News Writer Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed. Follow Follow See All by Emma Roth is a news writer who covers the streaming wars, consumer tech, crypto, social media, and much more. Previously, she was a writer and editor at MUO. The New York Times is asking freelance photographers not to use the newest version of Adobe Photoshop because features in the 2027 update use AI “in ways that may not be apparent to users.” In an email seen by The Verge and posted on Threads , two of the Times ’ photography leaders, Meaghan Looram and Josh Haner, write that the use of these features “can result in metadata identifying photographs as AI-generated.” Adobe has spent the past few years filling Photoshop and other apps in its creative suite with a trove of AI-powered tools. The November update cited by the NYT ’s email adds several new AI features, including Adobe’s new “AI Assisted Editor” view , which lets users access all of Photoshop’s AI features from a single toolbar. It also adds an AI assistant for step-by-step editing instructions, prompt-based image transformation and editing, new AI upscaling options, and an AI-powered tool that recomposes images for different canvas sizes. It’s not always clear which tools in Adobe’s apps are AI-powered, and even some older options like automated selection tools may now be considered part of the AI suite. This can lead to photos ending up with metadata indicating they were edited with AI, which is a blunt way of describing what happened in post-production. For a newsroom like the Times , where photo editing is kept to a minimum, the metadata risks losing readers’ trust. Last year, the Times said that it does not “retouch or digitally manipulate” photos but may “make minimal adjustments to color to ensure we are accurately reproducing the image.” Related Adobe is going all in on agentic AI Photoshop, Lightroom, and Premiere are making it easier to remove things In August, Adobe began rolling out support for C2PA metadata across its apps , which indicates whether an image is AI-generated or edited using AI features. The NYT is issuing its restriction to keep these tags from inadvertently appearing in its photos. “At this time, we are requiring that all contributing photographers avoid using Adobe Photoshop 2027 (release 28.0.0 and su

Not maintained and not accepting contributions. A Lean 4 proof of the Riemann Hypothesis and of the Generalized Riemann Hypothesis for Dirichlet L-functions, built on Mathlib and using only the axioms propext , Classical.choice and Quot.sound . The proof is based on Levent Alpöge's, with Claude ( https://alpo.ge/remark.pdf ). This repository is a shorter version, produced in collaboration with my colleagues at Anthropic, and Claude. Two editions grh/ : readable edition, about 300,000 lines. Start at grh/GUIDE.md . grh-compact/ : the same five theorems, about 76,000 lines. Each folder is its own Lake project with its own README. Build Needs Lean v4.35.0 and Mathlib at commit 56a00585. In either folder: <div class="snippet-clipboard-content notranslate position-relative overflow-auto" data-snippet-clipboard-copy-content="lake exe cache get lake build lake env lean scripts/PrintAxioms.lean"> lake exe cache get lake build lake env lean scripts/PrintAxioms.lean A build takes one to two hours on 12 cores with 24 GB of memory (about 85 minutes more if the Mathlib cache is missing).
